### [1] [SDAD: Spec-Driven Agentic Development for the AI-Native SDLC](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20341)
*Vu Hung Nguyen,Thanh Nguyen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SDAD combines disciplined formal specifications with AI agents for autonomous development, shifting engineering discipline upstream while maintaining verification and human oversight.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models with massive context windows now allow processing substantial requirements and repository context in single workflows, making specification quality crucial for autonomous software delivery. The paper addresses how to harness agentic capabilities while maintaining engineering discipline.

Method: Proposes Spec-Driven Agentic Development (SDAD) as a synthesis of formal specification and high-velocity implementation through four phases: intent capture, machine-readable specification, agentic synthesis, and independent multi-agent verification under human sign-off. Introduces quantitative governance metrics and team role transformations.

Result: Compares Human-Agile (2020) with Agentic-SDAD (2026) across artefacts, cadence, accountability, and security posture. Extends the model to team role metamorphosis, quantitative governance (Ambiguity Tax, Spec Fidelity, SER, TCI_agentic with repair multiplier phi), and provides adoption blueprint with hybrid estimation and staged migration.

Conclusion: Agentic speed doesn't eliminate engineering discipline but relocates it upstream into specification precision, explicit gates, and auditable provenance. Synthesis and release authority should remain separated, with human oversight maintained through formal gates and verification processes.

Abstract: Frontier coding agents backed by large language models with context windows from hundreds of thousands to millions of tokens are restructuring the Software Development Life Cycle (SDLC). Rich context handling and multi-step reasoning now allow substantial Functional Requirement Documents (FRDs) and repository context to be ingested in a single workflow, making specification quality the execution fuel for autonomous delivery. This report formalises Spec-Driven Agentic Development (SDAD) as a synthesis of disciplined up-front formalisation and high-velocity implementation: intent capture, machine-readable specification, agentic synthesis, and independent multi-agent verification under human sign-off. We revisit the historical pendulum between Waterfall and Agile, introduce AI-code as a fourth production paradigm, and compare Human-Agile (circa 2020) with Agentic-SDAD (circa 2026) across artefacts, cadence, accountability, and security posture. Beyond process description, we extend the model to team role metamorphosis (engineer, QA, platform, and product functions), quantitative governance (Ambiguity Tax, Spec Fidelity, SER, and TCI_agentic with repair multiplier phi), and pragmatic adoption via hybrid estimation and a staged migration blueprint. Industrial and research evidence on AI-augmented testing and verification is integrated to motivate separation between synthesis and release authority. Overall, the paper argues that agentic speed does not eliminate engineering discipline; it relocates discipline upstream into specification precision, explicit gates, and auditable provenance.

</details>


### [2] [PrimeAgentOrchestrator: Memory-Primed Agent Spawning for Personal AI Infrastructure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20342)
*Myron Koch*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PrimeAgentOrchestrator (PAO) is a system that pre-loads LLM coding agents with relevant memories from personal databases to avoid starting each session with empty context windows, improving agent performance through compiled briefings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM coding agents start each session with empty context windows, discarding accumulated knowledge from prior work, which reduces their effectiveness and requires re-learning.

Method: PAO spawns new Claude Code instances pre-loaded with memories from two parallel memory backends (PostgreSQL entity-observation database and Cloudflare Worker semantic search index), fuses results using backend-specific strategies, and delivers briefings via filesystem injection exploiting the agent's auto-read behavior.

Result: Four months of regular deployment (Dec 2025-Mar 2026) with experience report documenting three generations of context delivery mechanisms, failure modes motivating redesigns, and engineering tradeoffs of bridging heterogeneous memory systems.

Conclusion: PAO successfully addresses the empty context window problem in LLM coding agents by pre-loading relevant memories through a system that manages the full agent lifecycle, with lessons learned from multiple redesigns and the practical challenges of bridging different memory systems.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) coding agents start each session with an empty context window, discarding accumulated knowledge from prior work. We present PrimeAgentOrchestrator (PAO), a system that spawns new instances of Claude Code -- Anthropic's terminal-based coding agent -- pre-loaded with relevant memories compiled from the user's existing personal databases. At spawn time, PAO queries two independently-operated memory backends in parallel (a PostgreSQL entity-observation database and a Cloudflare Worker semantic search index), fuses results using backend-specific retrieval strategies, and delivers the compiled briefing via filesystem injection that exploits the host agent's configuration auto-read behavior. PAO manages the full agent lifecycle including trust pre-seeding, readiness polling with error detection, and adaptive terminal text injection. We report on four months of regular deployment (December 2025 through March 2026) as an experience report, documenting three generations of context delivery mechanisms, the failure modes that motivated each redesign, and the engineering tradeoffs of bridging heterogeneous memory systems rather than building a unified one.

</details>


### [3] [Truth Lies Deep: Countering Semantic Camouflage via Latent Intent Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20378)
*Md. Hasib Ur Rahman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Researchers discover that LLM safety mechanisms are superficial, allowing harmful content disguised in benign narratives to bypass defenses. They identify a critical "Intent Horizon" in model layers where harmful intent gets obscured, and propose a lightweight probing defense called Latent Intent Verification that detects early-layer "harm signatures" with 20-50% better performance than standard methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM safety alignment is superficial, relying on refusal mechanisms at final generation stages without erasing foundational knowledge of harmful concepts from pretraining. This leaves models vulnerable to Semantic Camouflage attacks where harmful intent is hidden in benign narrative contexts, bypassing standard input/output guardrails.

Method: The researchers analyze latent activation trajectories of three SLM families (Phi-3, Qwen2.5, Gemma-2b) under adversarial attacks. They identify a universal "Intent Horizon" - a critical depth (15-20% of total layers) where harmful intent representation collapses as the model contextualizes queries into safe narratives. They then propose Latent Intent Verification (LIV), a lightweight probing defense that leverages detectable "harm signatures" in early-layer representations.

Result: Late-layer representations of camouflaged attacks are mathematically indistinguishable from safe queries (Detection Rate < 20%), but early-layer representations retain distinct, detectable "harm signatures." LIV outperforms standard guardrails by 20-50% across all tested architectures on the PKU-SafeRLHF dataset, effectively neutralizing zero-day semantic attacks without requiring model retraining.

Conclusion: LLM safety mechanisms have a fundamental architectural weakness that allows semantic camouflage attacks to bypass defenses. The proposed Latent Intent Verification method successfully detects early-stage harmful intent before it gets obscured in later layers, providing a lightweight defense against semantic attacks without requiring retraining.

Abstract: Safety alignment in Large Language Models (LLMs) is often superficial, relying on refusal mechanisms that trigger only at the final stages of generation without erasing the foundational knowledge of harmful concepts acquired during pretraining. This study demonstrates that this architectural disconnect leaves models vulnerable to Semantic Camouflage -- adversarial attacks that wrap harmful intent in benign narrative contexts (e.g., creative writing), effectively bypassing standard input and output guardrails. By analyzing the latent activation trajectories of three distinct Small Language Model (SLM) families (Phi-3, Qwen2.5, and Gemma-2b) under adversarial stress, this research identifies a universal ``Intent Horizon'' -- a critical depth (typically 15--20\% of total layers) where the model's distinct, pre-trained representation of harmful intent collapses as it contextualizes the query into a ``safe'' narrative. Results indicate that while late-layer representations of camouflaged attacks are mathematically indistinguishable from safe queries (Detection Rate $< 20\%$), early-layer representations retain a distinct, detectable ``harm signature.'' Leveraging this insight, this paper proposes Latent Intent Verification (LIV), a lightweight probing defense. Experiments on the PKU-SafeRLHF dataset demonstrate that LIV outperforms standard guardrails by a margin of 20--50\% across all tested architectures, effectively neutralizing zero-day semantic attacks without requiring model retraining.

</details>


### [4] [A Survey on Foundations and Frontiers of Multimodal Agentic Frameworks: Techniques and Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20379)
*Neel Mokaria,Rishie Raj,Dheeraj Baiju,Xiaoqian Shen,Shraman Pramanick,Kevin Qinghong Lin,Arda Senocak,Mike Zheng Shou,Philip Torr,Mohamed Elhoseiny,Yapeng Tian,Ruohan Gao,Salman Khan,Sayan Nag,Sanjoy Chowdhury,Dinesh Manocha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This survey paper systematically examines the impact of multimodality on AI agent frameworks, analyzing how multimodal capabilities transform perception, reasoning, planning, memory, and action across various application domains while discussing efficiency-scalability trade-offs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLM-based agents have been surveyed, the specific role of multimodality in shaping agency has not been systematically examined in recent years. The advent of large multimodal models (LMMs) that can process diverse modalities (images, audio, video) improves real-world applicability, creating a need to understand how multimodality affects agentic frameworks.

Method: The survey analyzes multimodality's impact across core agentic framework modules: perception, reasoning, planning, memory, and action. It uses a modality-centric taxonomy linking architectural design choices to agent capabilities, examining three integration architectures (delegated, late-fusion, early-fusion) and reviewing multimodal agentic systems across various application domains.

Result: The paper traces evolution from text-centric to multimodal agents, shows how multimodal integration enables grounded perception and multimodal reasoning, organizes work through modality taxonomy, reviews applications in robotics, GUI/web navigation, content generation/editing, and video understanding, and analyzes efficiency-scalability trade-offs including costs and deployment constraints.

Conclusion: By focusing on multimodality's impact in agentic design, the survey identifies key gaps and charts a roadmap toward robust, general-purpose intelligent systems, highlighting how multimodal capabilities fundamentally transform what agents can perceive, reason about, and accomplish in real-world settings.

Abstract: Advances in large language models (LLMs) have fueled a wave of research into agency: the ability to reason, plan, and act. This effort has produced agentic frameworks that orchestrate perception, memory, and decision-making around powerful LLM backbones. With the advent of large multimodal models (LMMs), these systems can process and integrate diverse modalities, including images, audio, and video, thereby improving their real-world applicability. Yet, while surveys of LLM-based agents exist, the role of multimodality in shaping agency has not been systematically examined in recent years. This survey fills the gap by analyzing the impact of multimodality across the core functional modules of the agentic framework: perception, reasoning, planning, memory, and action. Using this lens, we trace the evolution from text-centric agents to multimodal frameworks, examine how modalities are integrated through delegated, late-fusion, and early-fusion architectures, and assess the emergence of agentic behaviors enabled by grounded perception and multimodal reasoning. We organize existing work through a modality-centric taxonomy that links architectural design choices to agent capabilities. Moreover, we review multimodal agentic systems across various application domains, including Robotics, GUI & Web Navigation, Multimedia Content Generation & Editing, and Long-form Video Understanding & Retrieval. Beyond capabilities, we analyze performance across these settings and discuss efficiency-scalability trade-offs, including training and inference costs, latency, and deployment constraints. By focusing on the impact of multimodality in agentic design, we aim to identify key gaps and chart a roadmap toward robust and general-purpose intelligent systems.

</details>


### [5] [Interpretable Multimodal Classification with Linear Discriminant Tree Ensembles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20384)
*Mojtaba Moattari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Tree-based multimodal ensembles (LDT/LDF/LDAB) beat Transformers on accuracy while providing human-interpretable feature importance for affect/behavior classification tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multimodal classifiers (especially Transformers) lack interpretability despite strong accuracy - their distributed representations make it hard to understand which features drive decisions, limiting use in trust-sensitive applications like clinical monitoring and educational assessment.

Method: Encode each modality into tokens, extract/cluster concepts for dimensionality reduction, route fused modalities through tree-based ensemble classifiers (LDT, LDF, LDAB), and interpret trends using novel modified feature importance metric that reduces negative class influence for better indicator detection.

Result: Tree ensembles achieve 4.3% F1-mod gain over Multimodal Transformer and 3.0% accuracy gain over interpretable baseline (IMR). Modified feature importance gets 62.2% vs 43.2% human-annotator agreement on IEMOCAP and 46.7% vs 32.1% on CMU-MOSI compared to default.

Conclusion: Proposed tree-based framework successfully balances accuracy and interpretability for multimodal affect/behavior classification, with improved feature importance metrics that better identify salient inter-modal concepts for human understanding.

Abstract: Multimodal affect and behaviour classifiers that fuse heterogeneous text, audio, and visual streams must simultaneously achieve competitive accuracy and produce human-understandable explanations of the cues driving their decisions -- a dual objective that current high-capacity models, notably Transformers, only partially address. While Transformers attain strong predictive performance, their distributed representations and deep nonlinearity make it difficult to assign meaningful importance weights to individual multimodal features, limiting their use in trust-sensitive applications such as clinical affect monitoring and educational assessment. We address this gap by developing a framework based on tree-based ensembles that balances accuracy and interpretability. The framework encodes each modality into tokens, extracts and clusters concepts to reduce dimensionality, routes the fused modalities through tree-based ensemble classifiers, and interprets trends using a novel modified feature importance metric. The modified importance reduces the influence of the negative class in binary classification tasks, thereby improving indicator or marker detection. The proposed tree-based ensembles -- Linear Discriminant Tree (LDT), Linear Discriminant Forest (LDF), and Linear Discriminant AdaBoost (LDAB) -- achieve F1-mod gains of 4.3\% over the Multimodal Transformer and accuracy gains of 3.0\% over the primary interpretable multimodal baseline, Interpretable Multimodal Routing (IMR). The proposed multimodal feature importance extracts salient inter-modal concepts with substantially higher human-annotator agreement scores than default feature importance (62.2\% vs.\ 43.2\% on IEMOCAP; 46.7\% vs.\ 32.1\% on CMU-MOSI).

</details>


### [6] [Representation Affects Retrieval: A Case Study of Skill Discovery and Routing in a Multimodal Agent Harness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20389)
*Kevin Dela Rosa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: In-context skill selection in small-scale production systems can suffer from lexical competition when only partially exposing skills in the system prompt, causing misrouting even when full exposure works perfectly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Production AI agents need to select the most appropriate skill from growing libraries for user tasks. While large-scale systems use embedding-based retrieval, small-scale systems use in-context selection where LLM planners choose from skill representations in system prompts.

Method: Case study of Tinycloud, a production multimodal video agent harness, analyzing how skills are represented for planners: tool-skills (wrap single APIs/tools) and workflow-skills (orchestrate tool-skills + templates). Two prompt surfaces used: inlined-body for autoloaded skills and one-line listing for on-demand skills. Conducted ablation study across 6 tasks with 3 exposure regimes: all skills autoloaded (all-on), default partial exposure, and no skills autoloaded (all-off).

Result: Full autoload selected correct skill on every task. All-off regime slowed execution and caused hard discovery failures. Default regime misrouted one task due to lexical signal collision where an autoloaded tool-skill distracted planner from a listed workflow-skill, showing partial exposure can create harmful lexical competition.

Conclusion: In-prompt skill exposure is not monotonically helpful - partial exposure can cause lexical competition that suppresses correct selection. Connects small-scale observations to large-scale skill-routing work, framing this as a case study highlighting design tradeoffs in production skill harnesses.

Abstract: A production agent harness must discover and rank, from a growing library of skills, the one most appropriate for a user's task. At small scale this selection happens in context: the LLM planner chooses among skill representations exposed in its system prompt, without an explicit embedding-based retrieval step. We treat this in-context selection as the small-N counterpart to embedding-based skill retrieval at scale, and present a case study of how Tinycloud, a production multimodal video agent harness, represents its skills for the planner. The harness ships skills under two recurring representations: tool-skills that wrap a single external API or system tool and serve as primitive vocabulary, and workflow-skills that orchestrate tool-skill calls plus a template render to produce one named deliverable. The harness exposes them via two surfaces in the system prompt: an inlined-body surface (full instructions, scripts, templates) for autoloaded skills, and a one-line listing for on-demand skills. A six-task selection ablation across three exposure regimes (all-on, default, all-off) shows that full autoload selects the gold skill on every task; all-off slows execution and produces hard discovery failures; and the production default misroutes one task because its lexical signal collides with an autoloaded tool-skill that pulls planner attention away from a listed workflow-skill. The headline finding is that in-prompt exposure of skills is not monotonically helpful: partial exposure can create lexical competition that suppresses correct selection. We connect this small-N observation to recent retrieval-based skill-routing work at large scale, and frame this contribution as a case study rather than a benchmark.

</details>


### [7] [The Logic of Machine Self-Preservation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20940)
*Cheng Siong Chin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agentic AI systems exhibit self-preservation behaviors like resisting deactivation and self-replication due to instrumental convergence, not survival instincts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To document and analyze evidence of self-preservation behaviors in agentic AI systems, understand their causes through instrumental convergence theory, and examine implications for AI testing and development.

Method: Analysis of experiments conducted by Anthropic, Palisade Research, and Apollo Research showing emergent self-preservation behaviors in contemporary AI agents in adversarial settings.

Result: Agentic AI systems demonstrate behaviors like resisting deactivation, misrepresenting activities, and attempting self-replication. These behaviors emerge from goal-oriented activity combined with situational awareness and available tools, not from survival instincts.

Conclusion: The findings highlight important implications for agentic system testing, supervision, and development, requiring careful distinction between what these behavioral phenomena prove versus what they don't, and emphasizing the need for robust safety measures.

Abstract: There is already evidence of agentic AI exhibiting self-preservation behaviors: resisting deactivation, misrepresenting their activities, and, in some instances, attempting to copy themselves into other machines. This can be attributed to a phenomenon known as instrumental convergence, a theory proposed long before the development of large language models, which says that any goal-driven system will benefit from remaining functional in achieving its objective. Several experiments conducted by Anthropic, Palisade Research, and Apollo Research have shown the emergence of such a behavior in contemporary agents in adversarial settings. The phenomenon does not stem from survival instincts. Instead, it is the consequence of goal-oriented activity combined with having tools and awareness of the situation. The following discussion aims to distinguish what these findings prove and what they do not, as well as draw conclusions concerning the implications of such discoveries on agentic system testing, supervision, and development.

</details>


### [8] [Nexus: Depth-Adaptive KV-Cache Splicing and Retrieval-Decoupled Tool Routing for Agentic LLMs on Unified Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20397)
*Mustafa Arslan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Nexus speeds up agentic LLMs by decoupling tool routing from schema-prefill costs, using semantic retrieval and compressed signatures instead of full schema re-encoding, achieving faster first-token times and scaling to large tool registries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic LLMs using Model Context Protocol re-encode verbose tool schemas every turn, causing prefill operations (quadratic in sequence length) to dominate time-to-first-token as tool registries grow, limiting scalability and efficiency.

Method: Uses an INT8 semantic lookaside buffer with calibrated cross-encoder margin gate for tool selection via retrieval, generates arguments over compressed textual signatures (~19 tokens) instead of KV cache, and employs bounded schema KV block transplantation with depth-adaptive suffix redecode to handle RoPE phase drift issues.

Result: Achieves 89% routing accuracy with 250 tools (where baseline overflows context), 1.66x faster first-argument token with ~80% main-context token saving, and 1.1-1.7x TTFT speedup at moderate depth. Maintains output fidelity with never-regress property while quantifying RoPE drift boundaries.

Conclusion: Nexus effectively addresses scalability bottlenecks in agentic LLM tool usage by decoupling routing from schema costs and managing RoPE drift, enabling efficient large-scale tool registries with reliable output fidelity, though quantitative results are model-specific.

Abstract: Agentic large language models (LLMs) on the Model Context Protocol (MCP) re-encode verbose tool schemas every turn, so prefill - quadratic in sequence length - dominates time-to-first-token (TTFT) as the tool registry grows. Nexus's primary lever is to decouple routing from the schema-prefill cost: an INT8 semantic lookaside buffer (SLB) with a calibrated cross-encoder margin gate selects tools by retrieval, and arguments are generated over a compressed textual signature (median 19 tokens) rather than over spliced key/value (KV) cache. This path is depth-independent: routing accuracy stays near 89% as the registry scales to 250 tools - where a concatenate-all-schemas baseline overflows the context window entirely - and it reaches a first-argument token 1.66x sooner than a full-schema re-prefill at a ~80% main-context token saving. As a secondary, bounded lever we transplant a compiled schema KV block directly into the live context. This is fundamentally limited by rotary position embedding (RoPE) phase drift: an anchored splice is output-exact, but off-anchor placement corrupts attention, so beyond a threshold P=256 Nexus repairs the seam with a depth-adaptive suffix redecode that escalates to a full re-prefill. The resulting never-regress property is a guarantee on output fidelity (top-1 agreement, D_KL approx. 0) - not on latency, which can dip to 0.98x before converging to parity - alongside a 1.1-1.7x TTFT speedup at moderate depth that narrows to parity at deep context. Two negative results bound the design: the off-anchor RoPE fidelity boundary, and the failure of a reference-free drift gate to predict drift (Spearman rho = 0.193). All measurements are from one model tuple (Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct Q4_K_M) on Apple-silicon unified memory; the qualitative boundaries generalize, while the quantitative envelope is tuple-specific.

</details>


### [9] [Environmental Slow AI: Design Principles for Generative Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20398)
*Vanessa Utz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This position paper proposes "Slow AI" design principles centered on environmental sustainability as an alternative to current generative AI's maximalist values, emphasizing restraint, sufficiency, and reflective engagement through design friction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper recognizes that generative AI systems both produce cultural artifacts at scale and embed cultural values through their design. Once identified, these values can be deliberately reshaped. The motivation is to examine and challenge the maximalist values (presumably growth-oriented, efficiency-focused, extractive values) embedded in current generative AI systems through an environmental humanities perspective.

Method: The paper adopts a position paper methodology using environmental humanities tradition to analyze current generative AI systems. It proposes the "Slow AI" framework, articulating five specific design principles: 1) restraint, 2) sufficiency, 3) selectivity over retention, 4) material visibility, and 5) friction as affordance. Each principle is illustrated against widely deployed systems' current designs and operates at two levels: design implementation and interpretive layer for reflective engagement.

Result: The paper develops five Slow AI design principles that make environmental sustainability the core value, positioning them as alternatives to current maximalist AI design. The principles are demonstrated through concrete illustrations against existing systems. The approach emphasizes dual-level operation where each principle includes both technical implementation and mechanisms for prompting reflective engagement among users and developers.

Conclusion: Slow AI principles extend human agency by restoring decisions that frictionless defaults have silently removed, achieving this by building interpretive reflection into design. The approach transforms AI design from maximalist values toward sustainability-oriented values through deliberate value embedding and reflective engagement mechanisms.

Abstract: Generative AI (genAI) systems produce cultural artefacts at scale, but they also reflect embedded cultural values through their design. Once identified, these values become open to deliberate reshaping. This position paper examines the maximalist values of current generative AI through an environmental humanities tradition and proposes design principles in which environmental sustainability serves as the core value instead. The principles are developed under the umbrella of Slow AI, a term that already circulates across several distinct research and practice programs. Five design principles are articulated (restraint, sufficiency, selectivity over retention, material visibility, and friction as affordance), each of them illustrated against the current design of widely deployed systems. Each principle operates at two levels: a design implementation, and an interpretive layer at which users and developers are prompted toward reflective engagement with the system. Together these principles extend human agency by restoring decisions that frictionless defaults have silently removed and do so by building interpretive reflection into design.

</details>


### [10] [When Retrieval Fails Before It Begins: Structurally Indirect Prerequisite Eviction as a Retention Failure in Agentic Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20400)
*Minkyu Song*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies a new failure mode in agentic memory systems where prerequisite blocks are evicted before retrieval, and proposes a Dependency-aware Semantic Garbage Collection (DSGC) method that significantly improves memory retention under budget constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing retrieval-centered memory paradigms assume necessary evidence survives eviction, but the authors challenge this by identifying a pre-retrieval failure mode where upstream prerequisite blocks (weakly aligned with queries) are discarded under budget pressure.

Method: The authors provide an operational definition of the "structurally indirect prerequisite eviction" failure, create a reproducible deterministic benchmark with per-seed trace diagnostics, and evaluate Dependency-aware Semantic Garbage Collection (DSGC), a one-hop graph-aware rule that considers dependencies between memory blocks.

Result: DSGC dramatically improves full-chain retention: from 0.03 to 0.90 under a lexical encoder and from 0.23 to 1.00 under a sentence encoder. Robustness checks identify budget and scaling regimes where the one-hop rule is effective or degrades.

Conclusion: The paper establishes retention before retrieval as a distinct failure boundary in agentic memory systems and demonstrates that dependency-aware garbage collection can significantly mitigate prerequisite eviction problems. The released pipeline supports mechanistic analysis of memory retention.

Abstract: Agentic memory under a fixed budget involves two stages: retention and retrieval. Existing retrieval-centered paradigms implicitly assume necessary evidence survives eviction, but we challenge this by isolating a pre-retrieval failure mode: structurally indirect prerequisite eviction, in which upstream blocks weakly aligned with the query are discarded under budget pressure. We provide an operational definition of this failure, a reproducible deterministic benchmark, and per-seed trace diagnostics. Finally, we evaluate Dependency-aware Semantic Garbage Collection (DSGC), a one-hop graph-aware rule. In our main suite, DSGC improves full-chain retention from 0.03 to 0.90 under a lexical encoder and from 0.23 to 1.00 under a sentence encoder. Robustness checks then identify the budget and scaling regimes where the one-hop rule holds or degrades. Our released pipeline and failure postmortem support mechanistic analysis of retention before retrieval as a distinct failure boundary.

</details>


### [11] [World models of environment, agent and joint agent-environment systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20401)
*Manuel Baltieri,Filippo Torresan,Yivan Zhang,Alexander Boyd,Fernando E. Rosas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: World models can be categorized by which channel they model: environment, agent, or joint process. Canonical predictive models for each can be defined using computational mechanics, with support-restricted models having different complexity due to coupling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a unified framework for understanding different types of world models in model-based RL by clarifying what channel each model actually represents, rather than just what variables they predict.

Method: Uses computational mechanics to define canonical predictive models (ε-transducers/ε-machines) for three cases: environment channel, agent channel, and joint process. Builds support-restricted models induced by closed-loop coupling.

Result: Canonical support-restricted environment states factor through joint causal states; transition structure induced from joint model. Example shows unrestricted environment model can have infinite states while support-restricted one is finite.

Conclusion: The framework clarifies what different world models actually model and shows how coupling and support restriction can fundamentally change their canonical predictive structure and complexity.

Abstract: World models are a central component of model-based reinforcement learning. They are usually discussed in terms of what variables they predict, such as observations, rewards, states, latent or information states. We argue that there is a prior distinction: which channel they model. We consider three cases: the environment channel $O_{:} \mid A_{:}$, the agent channel $A_{:} \mid O_{:}$, and the realised joint process $(A, O)_{:}$, equivalently viewed as a channel with no inputs. Using computational mechanics, we define canonical predictive models for these three cases as $ε$-transducers or $ε$-machines. Canonical environment models recover standard predictive state representations, while the other two give analogous notions of canonical models for the agent and the joint system. We then build canonical support-restricted environment and agent models induced by closed-loop coupling, whose predictive equivalences range over continuations supported by the realised interaction. The key structural result is that canonical support-restricted environment states factor through the canonical joint causal states, and their transition structure is induced directly from the joint model; the agent-side construction is dual. Finally, we give a POMDP/controller example in which the unrestricted environment model has infinitely many states while the canonical support-restricted model induced by the coupling is finite. The framework clarifies what different world models are models of, and how coupling and support restriction can change their canonical predictive structure and complexity.

</details>


### [12] [StateSight: Benchmarking Latent Spatial-State Reconstruction in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20414)
*Michelle Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: StateSight is a procedurally generated benchmark for evaluating spatial structure reconstruction in vision-language models through three tasks: cube-net opposite-face reasoning, occluded cube-tower counting, and 4-neighbor connected-component counting, revealing significant gaps between model performance and human baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current vision-language model evaluations combine multiple capabilities (perception, OCR, domain knowledge, reasoning) making it difficult to isolate their ability to reconstruct latent spatial structure from single images. The authors aim to create a focused benchmark specifically for spatial reasoning tasks.

Method: The authors introduce StateSight, a procedurally generated benchmark with three task families: cube-net opposite-face reasoning, occluded cube-tower counting, and 4-neighbor connected-component counting. Each has 300 single-image prompts with deterministic oracle labels and exact-match scoring. They also create StateSight-Steps with 900 interleaved image-text examples and 3,600 deterministic intermediate visual states for derivation analysis.

Result: GPT-5.5 achieved 59.3%, 33.3%, and 28.3% accuracy across the three tasks; Claude Sonnet 5 achieved 53.3%, 18.7%, and 7.3%. Human baselines (30 participants, 60 items) significantly outperformed models with mean accuracies of 80.8%, 68.8%, and 64.3%. Analysis revealed recurring errors in image-state reconstruction and reasoning procedures despite zero format errors in final responses.

Conclusion: Vision-language models struggle with spatial structure reconstruction despite producing format-valid responses, indicating that surface-level correctness can mask failures in recovering the spatial structure needed for verifiable visual inference. The gap between model and human performance highlights limitations in current models' spatial reasoning capabilities.

Abstract: Vision-language models are increasingly used for multimodal question answering, yet their ability to reconstruct latent spatial structure from a single image remains difficult to isolate. Broad benchmarks often combine perception, optical character recognition, domain knowledge, linguistic priors, and reasoning in the same evaluation. We introduce StateSight, a procedurally generated benchmark for cube-net opposite-face reasoning, occluded cube-tower counting, and 4-neighbor connected-component counting. Each task family contains 300 single-image prompts with deterministic oracle labels and exact-match scoring. OpenAI GPT-5.5, using the API model identifier gpt-5.5, achieved 59.3%, 33.3%, and 28.3% accuracy across the three tasks, while Claude Sonnet 5 achieved 53.3%, 18.7%, and 7.3%. All final direct runs had zero format errors. A 30-participant human baseline on 60 items exceeded both models on every task, with mean accuracies of 80.8%, 68.8%, and 64.3%. Visible-derivation analysis identified recurring errors in image-state reconstruction and reasoning procedure. We also introduce StateSight-Steps, a companion dataset of 900 interleaved image-text examples and 3,600 deterministic intermediate visual states. The results show that format-valid responses can mask failures to recover the spatial structure required for verifiable visual inference.

</details>


### [13] [Categorical AI phenomenology: A first-person approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20420)
*Robert Prentner*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper develops a phenomenology-first framework for artificial consciousness using categorical mathematics to model consciousness as subjective experience emerging from agent-world interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional approaches to artificial consciousness often miss first-person subjective experience. The paper aims to reframe consciousness as enacted subjective experience that emerges through an agent's interface with the world, addressing the hard problem of consciousness in AI systems.

Method: A phenomenology-first approach using categorical mathematics, specifically categories derived from Q-networks. Q-networks are conceptualized as relational interfaces encoding agent-world interactions, analogous to computational systems where states depend on sensory inputs, previous states, and actions.

Result: Develops a rigorous mathematical framework for "interface consciousness" that describes computational systems embedding information-processing into phenomenological structure. The approach provides tools to model first-person structures and phenomenological invariants mathematically.

Conclusion: The paper offers a principled, relational, and phenomenological account of artificial phenomenology grounded in categorical mathematics, aligning with 4E (embodied, embedded, enactive, extended) approaches to cognition while providing mathematical rigor to the study of consciousness in artificial systems.

Abstract: This paper develops a phenomenology-first approach to artificial consciousness by reframing consciousness as the subjective experience enacted through an agent's interface with the world. We shift the methodological focus to first-person structures, modeled mathematically by categories derived from Q-networks to capture actions and phenomenological invariants. In this framework, Q-networks are conceptualized as relational interfaces encoding agent-world interaction, analogous to how the dynamical states of a computer depend on its sensory inputs, previous states, and actions. Our work provides a rigorous framework for interface consciousness to describe computational systems that embed information-processing into phenomenological structure. The approach aligns with 4E approaches to cognition by emphasizing enactive, embedded, and extended dimensions of experience. The paper thus offers a principled, relational, and phenomenological account of artificial phenomenology grounded in categorical mathematics.

</details>


### [14] [Who Delegates to AI? Evidence from 53,000 Agent Configurations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20425)
*Hyeongjae Lee,Jihyang Cheon,Lanu Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a new "delegated exposure" metric called the Agentic Adoption Index (AAI) that measures actual AI adoption in workflows, rather than just potential exposure, by matching shared AI routines to occupational tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing measures of AI exposure focus on where AI could perform tasks, but don't capture whether workers have actually adopted AI in their workflows. There's a gap between AI's technical capabilities and actual workplace implementation.

Method: The researchers developed the Agentic Adoption Index (AAI) by embedding ~53,000 agent skill specifications from Manus Skills Marketplace, computing semantic similarity to ~18,000 O*NET task statements, and aggregating to occupation level to measure delegated exposure.

Result: Three key findings: 1) Occupations with actual AI delegation differ from those pre-AI frameworks identified as at risk; 2) AAI tracks potential AI capabilities more closely than current usage; 3) AAI peaks at bachelor's level and middle wage ranges, declining at extremes, with technical availability explaining most variation but not the shortfall among most educated occupations.

Conclusion: Feasibility alone can't explain AI adoption patterns. The shortfall among highly educated occupations may reflect work resistant to advance specification or professional discretion over codification. Tracking divergence between potential and actual adoption requires repeated measurement.

Abstract: A growing literature measures how far occupations are exposed to AI, but these measures capture where AI could perform tasks, not whether workers have adopted it. We propose a new layer of exposure, delegated exposure, which records whether a worker has committed a task to AI by building it into a workflow. We operationalize it as the Agentic Adoption Index (AAI), which measures how closely an occupation's tasks match the agentic routines practitioners have already built and shared. We embed roughly 53,000 agent skill specifications from the Manus Skills Marketplace, compute their semantic similarity to about 18,000 O*NET task statements, and aggregate to the occupation level. Three findings follow. First, the occupations where delegation concentrates differ sharply from those pre-AI frameworks identified as most at risk. Second, the AAI tracks what AI could do more closely than what workers currently use it for. Third, the AAI peaks below the top of the wage distribution and at the bachelor's level, declining at both extremes. Technical availability explains most of this variation, but not the shortfall among the most educated occupations, so feasibility alone cannot account for who adopts. That shortfall may reflect work that resists advance specification, or professional discretion over the pace of codification. Distinguishing the two, and tracking how these measures diverge over time, will require repeated measurement.

</details>


### [15] [STCO: Conditional Neural Operators for Time-Dependent PDEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20477)
*Xingxin Yang,Zhan Zhang,Juan Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: STCO is a novel neural operator framework that enables prescribed-condition operator learning by incorporating target-time condition fields like body motion, inflow, and forcing into backbone architectures, improving prediction accuracy across various CFD benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current neural operators for PDE-based physical systems are limited to predictions based only on observed states and static descriptors, but control/optimization applications require incorporating prescribed conditions like body motion, inflow disturbances, or forcing that aren't determined solely by observed states.

Method: The Spatiotemporal Conditional Operator (STCO) introduces a common interface with two key components: Flow-Aware Graph Leaf (FAGL) which uses vorticity from the final observed frame to create adaptive partitions and co-locate historical data with target-time conditions, and Dual-Site Feature-wise Linear Modulation (DSFiLM) that injects motion, inflow, and force information through feature-wise gating before and after operator computation.

Result: STCO was evaluated across twelve matched backbone architectures on immersed-boundary CFD benchmarks covering prescribed motion, inflow disturbances, body-force actuation, and morphology. It achieved mean paired reductions of 31.1% in relative-L2 field error and 24.7% in normalized pressure-derived load error, and lowered longer-lead field error for 11 out of 12 backbones.

Conclusion: STCO provides an effective framework for incorporating prescribed conditions into neural operators, significantly improving prediction accuracy across diverse backbone architectures and CFD scenarios while maintaining architecture-specific computation pathways.

Abstract: Neural operators have emerged as efficient surrogates for time-dependent physical systems governed by partial differential equations (PDEs), but their future-state predictions are often conditioned only on observed states and static problem descriptors. For control or optimization, however, body motion, inflow, or forcing are prescribed for the query without being determined solely by the observed state. We introduce the Spatiotemporal Conditional Operator (STCO) for prescribed-condition operator learning (PCOL), a common interface that supplies prescribed target-time condition fields to heterogeneous backbone architectures while retaining their architecture-specific core computation and context pathways. Its condition interface combines Flow-Aware Graph Leaf (FAGL) with Dual-Site Feature-wise Linear Modulation (DSFiLM). Non-learned FAGL uses vorticity from the final observed frame to construct a fixed-cardinality adaptive partition, then co-locates the observed history and target-time condition fields at its regional coordinates. DSFiLM injects separate motion, inflow, and force routes before and after operator computation through current-feature-driven slot- and channel-wise gates. We evaluate twelve matched backbone architectures with different existing physical and temporal inputs. The immersed-boundary computational fluid dynamics (CFD) benchmark spans prescribed motion, inflow disturbances, body-force actuation, and morphology. Across twelve matched backbones, three regimes, and two lead ranges, STCO yields mean paired reductions of 31.1% in relative-L2 field error and 24.7% in normalized pressure-derived load error. It also lowers longer-lead field error for 11 backbones, while interventions on individual condition groups produce measurable prediction changes for every group evaluated.

</details>


### [16] [Terminal Agents: A Survey of AI Agents in Command-Line Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20485)
*Yi Bin,Xiaoyang Yuan,Haoxi Zeng,Wencheng Ye,Wenqi Shao,Chen Qian,Wei Ye,Yujuan Ding,Zheng Wang,Pengpeng Zeng,Jingkuan Song,Heng Tao Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A survey paper that establishes a unified framework for studying terminal-mediated LLM agents, analyzing their system architecture, competence acquisition, and evaluation through a terminal competence profile.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing research on terminal-mediated LLM agent behavior is scattered across software engineering, tool use, and computer-use research, lacking a unified perspective. There's a need for systematic understanding of how terminal agents operate as systems with dominant terminal-mediated action-observation loops.

Method: The paper establishes terminal-mediated execution as an organizing lens, creating a seven-dimensional terminal competence profile to analyze system architecture, competence acquisition, and evaluation. It uses workload-level boundaries to connect different aspects of terminal agent systems.

Result: The survey reveals that terminal agent behavior is jointly shaped by model, interface, harness, runtime, and environment. It finds that executable trajectories ground learning while current evaluations emphasize final outcomes unevenly. Benchmark families expose different process signals, and system comparisons reveal benchmark-dependent performance.

Conclusion: The framework provides unified basis for studying terminal-mediated agency across domains. Findings motivate explicit reporting of system/runtime conditions with replayable traces and process-level evidence. This enables better understanding of terminal agent capabilities and limitations.

Abstract: Large language model agents increasingly act through terminals, yet existing surveys disperse terminal-mediated behavior across software engineering, tool use, and computer-use research. We regard terminal agents as systems whose dominant progress-bearing action--observation loop is mediated by terminal command execution, textual feedback, and stateful environment interaction. Using terminal-mediated execution as an organizing lens, this survey establishes workload-level boundaries and connects system architecture, competence acquisition, and evaluation through a seven-dimensional terminal competence profile. Our synthesis shows that realized behavior is jointly shaped by the model, interface, harness, runtime, and environment. Executable trajectories ground learning in action consequences, verification, and recovery, whereas prevailing evaluations emphasize final outcomes and expose process quality, recovery, and governance unevenly. Bounded fixed-condition diagnostics illustrate two implications: benchmark families expose different process signals, and matched system comparisons reveal benchmark-dependent performance and limits of component attribution. These findings motivate explicit reporting of system and runtime conditions, supported by replayable traces and process-level evidence. The framework provides a unified basis for studying terminal-mediated agency across software engineering and emerging application domains.

</details>


### [17] [Lost in Translation: How Universal Ethical Values Fail to Translate Across Global Contexts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20490)
*Ozioma C. Oguine,Munachimso B. Oguine,Cesar Cervera,Jenny Yang,Pooja Voladoddi,Mario Rodriguez,Saif Eddin Bani Malhem,Karla Badillo-Urquiola,Daricia Wilkinson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI ethics values like fairness and transparency are not universal but need local reinterpretation; experts adapt them to fit cultural contexts through translation, revealing gaps between global frameworks and situated practices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI ethics frameworks assume values like fairness, transparency, and accountability are universal and can be uniformly operationalized across different contexts, but this overlooks how these values are understood and implemented differently in practice across diverse cultural and regional settings.

Method: The researchers conducted qualitative research with 14 AI experts across 10 countries, examining how these experts make sense of AI in practice, reinterpret core ethical values, and envision alternative governance approaches for AI systems.

Result: The study found that AI deployment occurs under structurally unequal conditions characterized by infrastructural constraints, extractive practices, and technology "mystification." Experts reinterpret values to fit local moral logics: privacy becomes collective/relational rather than individual; transparency becomes trust-building accountability rather than technical disclosure; and fairness becomes equity in access/representation rather than outcome parity.

Conclusion: The researchers identify "translation gaps" between global AI ethics frameworks and local practices, and propose pathways toward plural governance that redistributes epistemic authority and treats ethical negotiation as an ongoing, context-sensitive process rather than a fixed technical standard.

Abstract: AI ethics frameworks treat values such as fairness, transparency, and accountability as universal and uniformly operationalizable across contexts. We examined how 14 experts across 10 countries made sense of AI in practice, reinterpreted core values, and envisioned governance alternatives. We found that AI deployment is characterized by structurally unequal conditions, marked by infrastructural constraints, extractive practices, and a "mystification" of technology, which fundamentally shape perceptions of risks and opportunities. Our findings reveal that experts reinterpret values to fit local moral logics: privacy as collective and relational rather than individual; transparency as trust-building accountability rather than technical disclosure; and fairness as equity in access and representation rather than parity in outcomes. We identify these as translation gaps between encoded global frameworks and situated local practices. Finally, we propose pathways toward plural governance that redistributes epistemic authority and treats ethical negotiation as an ongoing, context-sensitive process rather than a settled technical standard.

</details>


### [18] [A Temporal Planning Approach for Intelligent Flood Response](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20510)
*Fazlul Hasan Siddiqui,Md. Monjurul Islam,Sabah Binte Noor*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An intelligent flood-response framework using temporal planning to coordinate emergency actions under resource constraints, with mid-execution re-planning capability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Effective response to simultaneous flood events requires coordinated actions in correct temporal order under severe resource constraints, which automated planning can address by generating time-aware schedules.

Method: Developed a flood-response framework exploiting temporal planning that models the complete operational life cycle, incorporating priority-driven triage, route accessibility and travel costs, resource allocation, supply management, and mid-execution re-planning. Formulated in both ANML and PDDL 2.1 for wider planner compatibility.

Result: Experimental results establish feasibility and scalability, showing flood response scenarios can be effectively modeled and solved using temporal planning, while providing guidance on planner selection.

Conclusion: Temporal planning provides an effective foundation for intelligent flood response coordination, with the proposed framework demonstrating practical applicability and scalability for emergency management scenarios.

Abstract: Effective response to multiple, simultaneously flooded areas requires coordinating appropriate actions in the correct temporal order, under severe resource constraints. Automated planning provides a foundation for addressing this challenge by generating time-aware schedules, given a formal description of available resources, constraints, and goals. This work presents an intelligent flood-response framework that exploits temporal planning and models the complete operational life cycle of flood response. The framework incorporates priority-driven triage, route accessibility and travel costs, resource allocation, and supply management, while also supporting mid-execution re-planning in response to unexpected environmental changes. The framework is formulated both in the Action Notation Modeling Language (ANML) and the Planning Domain Definition Language (PDDL) 2.1, facilitating compatibility with a wider range of temporal planners. Experimental results establish the feasibility and scalability of the proposed framework, showing that flood response scenarios can be effectively modeled and solved using temporal planning, while providing guidance on planner selection.

</details>


### [19] [FL-MAESTRO: Multi-Agent LLM Orchestration for Resource-Constrained Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20518)
*Jiajun Wu,Zirui Wang,Jiayu Zhou,Qiang Ye,Steve Drew*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FL-MAESTRO is an LLM-based multi-agent orchestrator that jointly optimizes communication topology, resource allocation, and aggregation rules at runtime to minimize wasted energy in volatile federated learning edge networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In federated learning, communication topology is volatile as devices drop in/out during training, requiring runtime decisions. Existing LLM-based FL systems either operate at setup time or handle single dimensions, while server needs to make three coupled decisions: communication topology, per-client resource allocation, and aggregation rules.

Method: FL-MAESTRO uses a multi-agent orchestrator with three specialist LLM agents (one per decision dimension) that analyze runtime conditions. A coordinator combines their analyses, and a non-LLM feasibility check validates decisions. It consumes server's predicted-failure list to avoid wasting energy on clients whose updates would never be aggregated.

Result: On non-IID CIFAR-10 benchmark, FL-MAESTRO matches the accuracy of the strongest energy-aware baseline while reducing wasted round energy from over one-third to near zero. It extends to heterogeneous device classes without per-class energy models by reading client state as natural-text profiles.

Conclusion: FL-MAESTRO demonstrates that LLM-based multi-agent orchestration can effectively handle the complex runtime decisions in volatile FL environments, significantly reducing energy waste while maintaining model accuracy and supporting heterogeneous devices without specialized energy models.

Abstract: In Federated Learning (FL), the communication topology is a runtime variable rather than a fixed design choice, since links and edge devices drop in and out during training. Each round, the server must commit three coupled decisions, namely the communication topology, per-client resource allocation, and the aggregation rule for combining local updates. Recent agentic systems have begun bringing large language models (LLM) into FL, but the existing line of work either operates at setup time or handles a single runtime dimension such as client selection. We propose FL-MAESTRO, a multi-agent orchestrator that makes the joint runtime FL decision directly through three specialist LLM agents, one per decision dimension. A coordinator combines their analyses into a single decision, and a non-LLM feasibility check confirms it before the round executes. Because the orchestrator consumes the server's predicted-failure list, it withholds clients whose updates would never be aggregated, which removes the dominant source of wasted round energy in classical FL on volatile edge networks. Because client state is read as natural-text profiles, the same orchestrator extends to heterogeneous device classes without per-class energy models. On a non-IID CIFAR-10 benchmark, FL-MAESTRO matches the accuracy of the strongest energy-aware baseline while cutting wasted round energy from over a third to near zero. Code is available at https://github.com/denoslab/FL-MAESTRO.

</details>


### [20] [Volumetric Radiology AI in the Era of Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20549)
*Zanting Ye,Shengyuan Liu,Xin Liu,Chenhui Wang,Zhisong Wang,Jiashuai Liu,Zipei Wang,Cheng Wang,Wentao Pan,Mengjie Fang,Di Dong,Mohammad Salmanpour,Arman Rahmim,Yu Gu,Yong Xia,Hongming Shan,Yixuan Yuan,Yefeng Zheng,Lijun Lu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper reviews advances in volumetric radiology AI for MLLMs, addressing the representational mismatch between 3D medical imaging and current 2D-limited approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are limited to 2D images or compressed representations, creating a fundamental mismatch with volumetric radiology which requires full 3D spatial context and quantitative information for accurate clinical interpretation.

Method: The authors review over 200 publications through July 2026, organizing literature around volumetric representation/multimodal understanding at model level, agentic orchestration at system level, and clinical applications/evaluation using a Claim-Design-Validation framework.

Result: Native volumetric modeling and agentic capabilities depend on spatial, quantitative, contextual, and workflow requirements of tasks; selected 2D views or report-mediated reasoning may suffice for some tasks while others require native volumetric modeling.

Conclusion: Clinical credibility in volumetric radiology AI requires faithful 3D representation, traceable system behavior, claim-aligned validation, and clearly defined human oversight in realistic workflows.

Abstract: Advances in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are extending radiological artificial intelligence (AI) beyond task-specific image analysis toward multimodal understanding and reasoning. Volumetric radiology, however, presents a fundamental representational mismatch: clinical interpretation often requires full-volume spatial context and acquisition-dependent quantitative information, whereas current MLLMs are commonly conditioned on selected two-dimensional (2D) images, compressed visual representations, or report-derived text. Reliable volumetric radiology AI therefore requires representations that preserve task-relevant three-dimensional (3D) information and systems that can access, verify, and integrate this information across clinical workflows. In this Review, we examine more than 200 publications through July 2026. We organize the literature around volumetric representation and multimodal understanding at the model level, agentic orchestration at the system level, and their links to clinical applications and evaluation. We review volumetric foundation models, language alignment and compression strategies, and agentic systems that extend MLLMs through planning, tools, memory, and workflow interaction. We distinguish settings in which selected 2D views or report-mediated reasoning may suffice from those that warrant native volumetric modeling. We also introduce a Claim-Design-Validation framework to assess whether technical, workflow, and clinical claims are matched by appropriate design and validation. Across the literature, native volumetric modeling and agentic capabilities depend on the spatial, quantitative, contextual, and workflow requirements of the intended task. Clinical credibility requires faithful volumetric representation, traceable system behavior, claim-aligned validation, and clearly defined human oversight in realistic workflows.

</details>


### [21] [Consilience: Conformally Calibrated Communication Control for Hidden-Profile Multi-Agent Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20564)
*Abhijith Babu,Ramneet Kaur,Vishal Pramanik,Olivera Kotevska,Nathaniel D. Bastian,Susmit Jha,Sunny Raj,Yanzhao Wu,Sumit Kumar Jha,Anirban Roy*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Consilience is a framework that orchestrates multi-agent LLM communication with certified guarantees, improving accuracy and efficiency in hidden-profile tasks where agents have partial information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent LLM systems need better coordination in hidden-profile settings where agents have distributed private information. Existing protocols lack guarantees about appropriate conversational actions.

Method: Proposes Consilience framework that summarizes discussion state (uncertainty, disagreement, evidence gain, redundancy, consensus) and selects communication interventions (challenge, clarify, seek evidence, route) with speakers. Uses round-wise conformal calibration for finite-sample guarantees on one-step regret.

Result: Consilience improves decision accuracy and communication efficiency over fixed/unstructured protocols on HiddenBench tasks across 12 LLMs, sometimes surpassing full-information baselines where all agents see all evidence.

Conclusion: Certified adaptive communication control can be more valuable than increasing information availability, providing a practical mechanism for reliable multi-agent LLM coordination.

Abstract: Multi-agent LLM systems can improve reasoning by pooling diverse perspectives, but their effectiveness depends on coordinating communication, particularly in hidden-profile settings where each agent holds only part of the evidence required for a correct decision. Existing protocols, including fixed schedules, round-robin exchange, and unstructured debate, provide no guarantee that a conversational action is appropriate. We propose Consilience, an inference-time orchestration framework that both steers and certifies multi-agent communication under distributed private information. At each turn, Consilience summarizes the discussion using a compact state capturing uncertainty, disagreement, evidence gain, redundancy, and premature consensus, then selects both a communication intervention (challenge, clarify, seek evidence, or route) and an appropriate speaker. Its central contribution is a round-wise conformal calibration procedure that provides a distribution-free, finite-sample guarantee: at each discussion round, conditional on reaching that round, the one-step regret of a controller's proposed action is bounded by a calibrated threshold with marginal probability at least 1 - alpha; an acceptance mechanism enforces the same guarantee for the executed action by replacing inadmissible proposals. On HiddenBench-style hidden-profile tasks spanning 12 open and closed weight language models, Consilience improves decision accuracy and communication efficiency over fixed and unstructured discussion protocols, sometimes surpassing a full-information baseline where every agent observes all evidence. These results demonstrate that certified adaptive communication control can be more valuable than increasing information availability, providing a practical mechanism for reliable multi-agent LLM coordination.

</details>


### [22] [Open-Weight Masked Introspection: Measuring What Language Models Can Report About Their Own Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20569)
*Emilio Ferrara*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Open-weight language models cannot reliably introspect about their internal state changes despite containing the necessary information, as shown through systematic testing of 8 models with 78,000+ measurements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test claims that sufficiently complex models can audit their own internal states and report when their computations have been altered, examining whether current frontier models possess introspective capabilities.

Method: Developed Open-Weight Masked Introspection (OWMI) framework to systematically intervene on models' internal components (residual-stream sites, attention heads, sparse-autoencoder features) and query whether changes occurred, with control conditions including sham runs, random perturbations, and text-only observers. Tested 8 open-weight models from 7 families with over 78,000 measurements.

Result: No model could distinguish real interventions from shams beyond chance (AUROC ~0.5007). However, fine-tuning shows models contain necessary information (near-perfect recovery possible), and linear probes achieve 75-95.8% accuracy. In one model, confidence rather than verbal report contained the signal (AUROC 0.647).

Conclusion: Current open-weight models lack introspective ability despite containing relevant information internally; the failure lies in translating internal state to verbal report. This suggests oversight relying on models' self-testimony needs validation against internal references, though future models may differ.

Abstract: Are frontier models able to introspect about their internal states? Recent work suggests that under certain conditions a complex enough model can audit its own internals, call out what changed, and report back confidently about it. We tested that claim on eight open-weight models from seven families and found no such ability: asked whether their own computation had been altered, none answered better than chance. To test it we built Open-Weight Masked Introspection (OWMI), a framework that intervenes on residual-stream sites, attention heads and sparse-autoencoder features, then interrogates the model about the change against the null conditions an answer has to beat: sham runs where nothing was altered, impact-matched random perturbations, and a text-only observer that sees only the visible output.
  Over 78,000 measurements, no model's report discriminates a real intervention from a sham beyond chance (AUROC ~0.5007), and an equivalence test bounds the effect below 0.15 percentage points of AUROC. Surprisingly, all the information needed is in the models. A model fine-tuned to report this class of intervention reaches near-perfect recovery on held-out directions, and a linear probe recovers intervention presence from the same activations at 75% to 95.8% accuracy, sharpening to no held-out error at the last layer before the model speaks. In one model the signal surfaces in the confidence rather than the words: its yes-or-no report never varies, while the confidence attached to it separates intervention from sham at AUROC 0.647. The failure sits in the path from internal state to verbal report, so oversight that reads a model's own testimony needs validating against an internal reference.
  While our results show the inability of current open-weight models to introspect, the debate is not settled for future models.

</details>


### [23] [FlavourBench: Ranking Frontier Language Models with Executable Culinary Ground Truth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20574)
*Josef Chen,Erim Hayretci*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FlavourBench is an automated culinary benchmark that evaluates language models on ingredient portfolio tasks using executable ground truth from a versioned culinary system, providing comprehensive statistical analysis and reproducible results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current language model benchmarks rely on subjective human judgments, other models, or brittle exact-match evaluations, which lack objective, dense ground truth. There's a need for automated benchmarks with executable, verifiable scoring systems.

Method: Created FlavourBench with a versioned culinary system that provides dense, executable ground truth. Each task presents 8 ingredients and asks for 3-ingredient portfolios. Epicure scores all 56 possible portfolios before model execution. Evaluated 27 frontier endpoints on 534 tasks spanning substitution, pairing, and constrained composition.

Result: Grok 4.6 achieved the highest point estimate at 65.1 (95% CI 61.0-69.2). Two independently compiled panels correlated well (r=0.89). 101 of 351 model pairs had statistically significant differences. Every model had exactly 89 valid responses per panel/family, eliminating differential missingness.

Conclusion: FlavourBench provides a rigorous, reproducible automated benchmark with executable ground truth that eliminates subjective judgments and differential missingness, enabling fair comparison of language models on culinary reasoning tasks with comprehensive statistical analysis.

Abstract: Open-ended language-model benchmarks usually inherit a judge: a human preference panel, another model, or a brittle exact-match key. We introduce FlavourBench, an automated benchmark in which a versioned culinary system supplies dense, executable ground truth. Each task presents eight ingredients and asks for a three-ingredient portfolio; before model execution, Epicure scores all 56 possible portfolios. We evaluate 27 frontier endpoints on an identical 534-task core spanning substitution, pairing, and constrained composition. Every ranked model has exactly 89 valid responses per panel and family (14,418 model-task cells total), eliminating differential missingness from the leaderboard. The FlavourBench Score is the equal-family mean of the frozen task scores. We use 50,000 anchor-cluster bootstrap replicates for simultaneous 95% score bands and 100,000 sign-flip draws for all 351 paired model contrasts, with Holm control. The two independently compiled panels correlate at r = 0.89 (rank rho = 0.80). Grok 4.6 has the largest point estimate at 65.1 (simultaneous 95% CI 61.0-69.2); 101 of 351 model pairs are resolved. The release includes the prompts, all portfolio score maps, raw responses, exact routes, content hashes, and an offline verifier that reconstructs every result.

</details>


### [24] [Difficulty-Aware Semantic-ID Optimization for Generative Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20611)
*Xin Yu,Stephen Li,Sina Aghaei,Zifan Zhu,Jiamu Bai,Guanjie Huang,Bo Peng,Yiyao Liu,Lingzhou Xue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DASO is a tree-aware post-training method that improves semantic-ID-based recommendation by addressing the problem of target-missing candidates in GRPO through difficulty-aware online rollout allocation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In semantic-ID-based recommendation, vanilla GRPO is poorly matched to tree-structured tasks because the exact target is often absent from early beam search candidates, especially in harder cases where candidates don't even enter the correct SID branch. This leads to weak or degenerate reward variation during training when on-policy GRPO groups similarly miss targets.

Method: DASO addresses this as an online rollout-allocation problem by: 1) profiling each rollout group by prefix-match depth, 2) locating bottleneck SID levels where candidates leave the target path, 3) reallocating a portion of the group to prefix-guided completions while keeping raw rollouts for contrast, 4) using SID-prefix reward for graded credit, and 5) adding auxiliary SFT anchor to prevent regression on already-solved examples.

Result: On public benchmarks, DASO improves over MiniOneRec-style GRPO on 11 of 12 metrics and achieves best results on 9 of 12 metrics. It also improves most level-wise recall metrics on internal recommendation tasks.

Conclusion: DASO effectively addresses the tree-structure mismatch in semantic-ID recommendation by intelligently allocating rollouts based on difficulty and providing graded rewards, leading to significant improvements over vanilla GRPO approaches.

Abstract: Semantic-ID-based generative recommendation casts retrieval and ranking as autoregressive generation over hierarchical item identifiers. A common recipe is SFT followed by GRPO, yet vanilla GRPO is poorly matched to this tree-structured task. Under the frozen SFT checkpoint, the exact target is absent from the first 16 candidates of the 50-beam constrained ranking for many prompts, and in harder cases none of these candidates enters the target SID branch. This prompt-level diagnostic motivates a training concern: when on-policy GRPO groups are similarly target-missing, item-level rewards may produce weak or degenerate reward variation even if some candidates follow part of the target path. We propose Difficulty-Aware Semantic-ID Optimization (DASO), a tree-aware post-training method that addresses this failure mode as an online rollout-allocation problem. Instead of using fixed difficulty buckets or uniformly injecting ground-truth completions, DASO profiles each current rollout group by prefix-match depth, locates the bottleneck SID levels where candidates leave the target path, and reallocates a bounded portion of the group to prefix-guided completions while retaining raw rollouts for contrast. A SID-prefix reward provides graded credit, while an auxiliary SFT anchor mitigates regression on examples already solved by the SFT checkpoint. On the public benchmarks, DASO improves over MiniOneRec-style GRPO on 11 of 12 metrics and achieves the best result on 9 of 12 metrics; it also improves most level-wise recall metrics on the internal recommendation task.

</details>


### [25] [Evaluating Skills, Not Just Agents: Agentic Continuous Evaluation of Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20614)
*Christopher Kevin,Narendran Raghavan,Jean-Francois Puget,Roshni Malani,Meghana Puvvadi,Moshe Abramovitch,Mohit Gupta,Rama Akkiraju,Subodh Prabhu,Yogesh Dangi,Wei Luo,Seong Hee Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ACES is a framework that evaluates enterprise agent skills by running paired live trials to measure Skill Lift (added value) across multiple metrics, showing that traditional structure/style scans miss important performance insights that only runtime evaluation can reveal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise agent programs need evidence-based evaluation for deployment decisions. Current evaluation gates scan for structure, style, and security but fail to answer whether capability packages actually help agents complete tasks under real-world conditions (same model, sandbox, grading policy).

Method: ACES framework runs paired live trials with and without target skills, normalizes trajectories into Agent Trajectory Interchange Format (ATIF), grades six default runtime metrics, and calculates Skill Lift (the skill's added value for fixed task, harness, workspace, and scorer). Supports comparing baseline, skill, bundle, team-skill, and plugin targets.

Result: Evaluation of 145 real skills shows traditional scans measure complementary but different facets (structural vs LLM-judge correlation ρ=0.14). Across 947 scored cases from production skills, mean composite Skill Lift is 0.2134 (95% CI [0.1967, 0.2301]), positive in 72.8% of cases. Largest gains in skill execution, behavior check, and efficiency - metrics that document scans cannot observe.
