The paper investigates whether environmental priors can serve as auxiliary geospatial data for EEG-based classification when individual exposure data is unavailable.

Method: Combined 30-channel EEG from EAV benchmark (42 participants) with environmental representations from OpenAQ, Sentinel-2, Sentinel-5P, and OpenStreetMap for Astana. Used dual-tower architecture: EEG-Conformer representations with graph-based environmental encoder. Environmental context treated as literature-informed prior rather than measured exposure. Used subject-level repeated splits, permutation/label-shuffling controls, dose-response reversal, and domain-shift experiments.

Result: Multimodal model achieved 76.2% accuracy vs 67.4% for EEG alone. Controls disrupting environmental-label structure retained part of the gain (not solely attributable to environmental info). Replacing Astana environmental distribution with Singapore distribution reduced accuracy to 72.8%.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates technical feasibility of using environmental priors as auxiliary modality but doesn't establish observed/causal exposure-affect associations. Provides framework for future mobile EEG-environment studies with implementation available on GitHub.

Abstract: Environmental exposures such as air pollution and greenness have been associated with affective and cognitive outcomes, but EEG and environmental datasets are rarely jointly georeferenced. We investigate whether literature-informed environmental priors can serve as an auxiliary geospatial modality for EEG-based affective-state classification when individual-level exposure data are unavailable. We combine 30-channel EEG from the EAV benchmark (42 participants, aged 20-30 years) with environmental representations derived from OpenAQ, Sentinel-2, Sentinel-5P, and OpenStreetMap data for Astana. A dual-tower architecture combines EEG-Conformer representations with a graph-based environmental encoder. Because the datasets are not co-registered, environmental context is treated as a literature-informed prior rather than measured exposure. Subject-level repeated splits, permutation and label-shuffling controls, dose-response reversal, and domain-shift experiments distinguish architecture-level gains from prior-dependent gains. The multimodal model achieves 76.2% accuracy versus 67.4% for EEG alone. Controls disrupting environmental-label structure retain part of this gain, indicating that the improvement is not attributable solely to environmental information. Replacing the Astana environmental distribution with an independently modeled Singapore distribution reduces accuracy to 72.8%. These findings demonstrate technical feasibility but do not establish an observed or causal exposure-affect association. The study provides a framework for future jointly collected mobile EEG-environment studies. Implementation: https://github.com/r11up/geo-cog

</details>


### [50] [Certified Multi-Turn Robustness for LLM Safety via Compositional Bounds and Safety Persistence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20820)
*Yang Liu,Bin Chong,Wenkai Yang,Shuai Zhang,Yancheng Chen,Feiyu Han,GuoZhen,Cheng Zhang,Huaibing Xie,Changze Lv,Shihan Dou,Pluto Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MTCR framework provides certified robustness against multi-turn jailbreak attacks for LLMs with tighter bounds and horizon estimates than naive approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing certified robustness methods only work for single-turn inputs and fail for multi-turn attacks; naive multi-turn composition leads to exponentially degrading bounds, leaving LLMs vulnerable to progressive manipulation in conversations.

Method: Uses State-Adversarial MDPs to model conversational safety, with compositional certification via embedding-space mode decomposition, (α,β)-safety persistence for improved degradation rates, matching information-theoretic upper bounds, and a unified algorithm combining these techniques.

Result: Experiments on six LLMs show empirical safety consistently exceeds the certified bounds under both ε-bounded and Crescendo-style attacks, with improved degradation rates from p̅^k to β^k (where β > p̅).

Conclusion: MTCR provides the first certified robustness framework for multi-turn jailbreak attacks with tighter bounds than naive approaches, enabling interpretable safety guarantees for conversational AI systems.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are vulnerable to multi-turn jailbreak attacks that progressively manipulate conversation context. Existing certified robustness methods are limited to single-turn inputs; naive multi-turn composition yields bounds that degrade exponentially in the number of turns. We introduce Multi-Turn Certified Robustness (MTCR), a framework that models conversational safety via State-Adversarial MDPs and defines $k$-turn certified robustness as the worst-case safety probability across $k$ adversarial turns. MTCR comprises: (i) compositional certification via embedding-space mode decomposition, yielding tighter certified lower bounds than naive multiplication; (ii) $(α,β)$-safety persistence, improving the degradation rate from $\underline{p}^{k}$ to $β^k$ (with $β> \underline{p}$) and yielding interpretable horizon estimates; (iii) matching information-theoretic upper bounds establishing tightness; and (iv) a unified algorithm combining these results. Experiments on six LLMs under $ε$-bounded and Crescendo-style attacks confirm that empirical safety consistently exceeds the certified bounds.

</details>


### [51] [Prediction certification cannot replace explanation certification: a competence envelope for trustworthy AI under compound stress](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20825)
*Nataliya Shakhovska,Ivan Izonin,Stergios-Aristoteles Mitoulis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Prediction-based metrics (accuracy, calibration, coverage) are insufficient for AI trustworthiness - models can be identical on these metrics yet differ arbitrarily in explanations and real behavior, requiring analysis of decision mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI systems make critical decisions (medical, safety, authentication) and are trusted based on prediction accuracy and confidence. Current safeguards use prediction-based certification (accuracy, calibration, coverage), but it's unclear if these are sufficient for true trustworthiness.

Method: Proves a separation theorem showing reliable and compromised models can be identical under prediction-side certificates. Introduces "competence envelope" framework combining prediction and explanation certification into single deployable criterion. Evaluates across diverse datasets and model classes.

Result: Established that prediction-side certification alone cannot detect model compromise. Competence envelope framework reveals failure modes invisible to prediction-only methods. Requires evidence about model's decision mechanism in addition to outputs.

Conclusion: Prediction-based metrics are fundamentally insufficient for AI trustworthiness certification. Detection of compromised models requires access to decision mechanisms and explanation fidelity, not just prediction outputs. Competence envelope provides operational framework for comprehensive certification.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence systems increasingly make consequential judgments - which patient is deteriorating, which building is safe to enter, whether an image is authentic and are trusted on the strength of how accurately and confidently they predict. The safeguards that certify them are correspondingly prediction-based: accuracy, calibration and conformal coverage all measure how well a model performs. Whether such checks are sufficient to establish model trustworthiness has remained unclear. Here we prove that they cannot. We establish a separation theorem showing that a reliable model and a compromised one can be identical under every prediction-side certificate, including accuracy, calibration and coverage, yet differ arbitrarily in explanation fidelity and deployment behaviour. Detecting this failure requires access to the model's decision mechanism in addition to its predictions. We introduce the competence envelope as an operational framework that combines prediction and explanation certification into a single deployable criterion. Across diverse datasets and model classes, the proposed framework reveals failure modes that prediction-side certification alone does not capture. Certification against failures that are invisible in prediction behaviour therefore requires evidence about the model's decision mechanism as well as its outputs.

</details>


### [52] [Foundation Models for Partial Causal Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20841)
*Alexis Bellot,Anish Dhir*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper develops causal foundation models for estimating bounds on causal effects under unobserved confounding, translating the problem into learning distributions over functions that map data and assumptions to causal queries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a need to extend causal foundation models to handle partially-identifiable causal effects where multiple values are equally compatible with observed data due to unobserved confounding. Current approaches may not adequately address bounding interventions and counterfactuals from observational data alone.

Method: The authors define a canonical prior with full support over structural causal models with discrete observables. They then translate the bounding problem into learning distributions over functions that map data and structural assumptions to causal queries of interest.

Result: The paper successfully extends the causal foundational modeling paradigm to estimate partially-identifiable causal effects, enabling bounding of counterfactuals and interventions under unobserved confounding conditions.

Conclusion: This work provides a principled framework for causal inference in settings with unobserved confounding by leveraging causal foundation models to bound causal effects rather than point-estimate them, representing an important extension of the causal foundational modeling approach.

Abstract: This paper investigates the development of causal foundation models for bounding the effect of interventions and counterfactuals from observational data. We show that a canonical prior can be defined with full support over the space of structural causal models with discrete observables. With this canonical prior, we translate the problem of bounding counterfactuals into that of learning distributions over functions that map data (and possibly structural assumptions) to a causal query of interest. This extends the promising causal foundational modelling paradigm to the estimation of partially-identifiable causal effects, i.e., under unobserved confounding, where multiple values are equally compatible with the observed data and prior structural assumptions.

</details>


### [53] [TRACE: Agentic Catalog Enrichment with Multi-source Evidence Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20844)
*Rohan Kumar,Steven Xu,Kyle MacDonald,Matthew Long,Bernice Chow,Mac VanRenterghem,Sudeep Das*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRACE is a novel framework using agentic LLMs to automatically enrich sparse e-commerce product catalogs with accurate attribute values through evidence triangulation and verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: E-commerce product catalogs are often attribute-sparse, with important attributes buried in unstructured content or missing entirely. Manual enrichment is impractical due to catalog scale and rapid growth.

Method: TRACE uses two LLM agents: a ScoutAgent that triangulates multimodal evidence across merchant catalogs, syndicated feeds, and web search to propose candidate attribute values, and a JudgeAgent that verifies proposed values against supporting evidence and decides whether to publish or route to human review.

Result: On offline human evaluation: 98.2% accuracy at 74.7% attribute coverage. In production deployment: 90.4% increase in impression-weighted enrichment coverage across four business verticals. Online experiment: 0.48% increase in checkout conversion when enriched attributes were surfaced on product detail pages.

Conclusion: TRACE successfully automates catalog attribute enrichment at scale with high accuracy, significantly improving coverage and conversion rates in e-commerce systems.

Abstract: Product catalogs underpin search, discovery, and recommendation in e-commerce, yet they are often attribute-sparse: the attributes shoppers and downstream systems rely on are either buried in unstructured content such as titles and images or missing from the catalog altogether. Manually enriching e-commerce catalogs is impractical given their scale and rapid growth. This paper introduces TRACE, a novel framework for automated catalog attribute enrichment using agentic Large Language Models (LLMs). A ScoutAgent triangulates multimodal evidence across merchant catalogs, syndicated feeds, and identity-matched web search to propose candidate attribute values with supporting evidence, while a JudgeAgent verifies the proposed value for each attribute value against its supporting evidence and decides whether to publish it or route it to human review. On an offline human evaluation dataset, TRACE's proposed attribute values were 98.2% accurate at 74.7% attribute coverage. Deployed in production on an industry-scale catalog, TRACE increased impression-weighted enrichment coverage across four business verticals by 90.4%. An online experiment subsequently showed that surfacing the enriched attributes on the product detail page increased checkout conversion by 0.48%.

</details>


### [54] [RAG Deserves an Index: Why Ingest-Time Compilation Beats Query-Time Interpretation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20845)
*Kyle Wild,Yusuke Takahashi,Asako Uraki*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Ingest-Time Semantic Compilation (ISC) indexes corpus meaning at write time into maintained structures (embeddings + validated claims) for efficient semantic retrieval, dramatically reducing query costs while maintaining accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current retrieval-augmented QA systems inefficiently re-derive semantic meaning for each query and discard the work, analogous to "full-table scans" in databases. Context volume grows faster than per-token price reductions, making this approach increasingly costly.

Method: ISC compiles corpus meaning at ingest time into a queryable substrate with two layers: incrementally maintained embeddings and atomic claims with provenance validated at compile time. This substrate is treated as a first-class database object with its own DDL, maintenance contracts, migration contracts, and cost model.

Result: ISC shows 33.7x cheaper incremental updates than reconstruction while maintaining floating-point precision tracking. On broadcast-interview transcripts, compiled claims achieve 85.2% accuracy with ~2.2k tokens vs 72.5% with ~16.3k tokens for best chunk baselines. Only hybrid retrieval+reranking baseline keeps pace but requires 21x more query-path tokens.

Conclusion: ISC offers a database-inspired solution to semantic retrieval inefficiencies by compiling meaning at write time. The paradigm shift enables significant cost reductions while maintaining accuracy, opening a systems agenda for compilation planners and read planning.

Abstract: Nearly every retrieval-augmented question-answering system in production ships with a hidden interpreter: on each query a language model re-derives the meaning of raw corpus text and then throws that work away. Cheaper models do not close the gap: per-token prices have fallen by orders of magnitude while inference spend has risen, because context volume grows faster than prices fall. This is the modern equivalent of the full-table scan, and the remedy is the one databases found fifty years ago: do the expensive work once, at write time, into a maintained structure that makes reads cheap. A corpus whose read pattern is known before it ever meets a user can and should be indexed too.
  We call the paradigm ingest-time semantic compilation (ISC): compile a corpus's meaning into a queryable substrate with two coupled layers - incrementally maintained embeddings, and atomic claims whose provenance is validated at compile time - and treat that substrate as a first-class database object with its own DDL, maintenance contract, migration contract, and cost model.
  Two existence proofs support it. Substrate upkeep scales with change rather than corpus size: incremental updates run 33.7x cheaper than reconstruction while tracking it to floating-point precision. And on a held-out sample of 500 broadcast-interview transcripts, compiled claims as the retrieval payload win all 32 budget-by-model cells: 85.2% correct from roughly 2.2k reader tokens against 72.5% from 16.3k for the best chunk configuration anywhere. The only baseline that keeps pace is a contextualized-chunk pipeline with hybrid retrieval and reranking, statistically indistinguishable from compiled claims at roughly twenty-one times the query-path tokens - and it reaches that parity, we argue, precisely because it has itself begun to compile. We close with the systems agenda this opens, from compilation planners to read planning.

</details>


### [55] [MGAL: A Multilingual Granularity-Aware Long-Context Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20853)
*Chunhan Li,Chenglin Xu,Zongyang Zhang,Jiale Liu,Zhuoxi Rao,Xudong Jia,Junxiu He,Menglin Yang,Wenjuan Gong,Zhengzhe Liu,Chengwei Qin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MGAL is a new multilingual benchmark that systematically evaluates long-context LLMs across different linguistic granularities and positions within documents, revealing fine-grained performance patterns and new challenges in semantic understanding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing long-context LLM benchmarks are limited to document level and focus mainly on high-resource languages, leaving many fine-grained challenges insufficiently evaluated. There's a need for more granular, multilingual evaluation.

Method: Constructed MGAL benchmark from United Nations reports spanning 8K to 128K tokens across six official UN languages. Covers four coherent linguistic granularity levels (word, sentence, paragraph, document) and stratifies entries by position (beginning, middle, end) at both document and paragraph levels.

Result: LLMs perform well at word-level tasks but struggle with coarser-grained ones; closed-source models have advantage in lower-resource languages. Identified new challenges: (1) Under local semantic crowding, models follow surface cues rather than discourse role; (2) Gap between fluency and consistency where generated text reads smoothly but drifts from source facts.

Conclusion: MGAL enables systematic diagnosis of multilingual long-context comprehension across granularities, revealing important limitations and patterns in current LLMs that need addressing for better document understanding.

Abstract: Evaluation of long-context Large Language Models (LLMs) has advanced rapidly. However, most existing benchmarks are limited to the document level and focus mainly on high-resource languages, leaving many fine-grained challenges insufficiently evaluated. To address this gap, we present MGAL, the first multilingual, granularity- and position-aware long-context benchmark. MGAL is constructed from United Nations (UN) reports spanning 8K to 128K tokens across the six official UN languages. It covers four coherent levels of linguistic granularity (word, sentence, paragraph, and document) and further stratifies entries by their position within the document (begin, middle, and end), indexed at both the document and paragraph levels. This design enables systematic diagnosis of multilingual long-context comprehension across different granularities.
  Through extensive experiments and analyses, we find that: (1) LLMs perform well at word-level tasks but struggle with coarser-grained ones; and (2) Closed-source models retain a clear performance advantage in lower-resource languages. We further identify two new challenges: (1) Under local semantic crowding, where neighboring sentences share topics and entities, models tend to follow surface cues (e.g., connectives like ``however'' or repeated entities) rather than the discourse role of the sentence in surrounding context (e.g., background, outcome); and (2) A gap between fluency and consistency in generated outputs, where models produce text that reads smoothly but drifts from the source facts. In addition, we observe several patterns in line with prior studies, including reliance on nearby evidence and reuse of options under uncertainty.

</details>


### [56] [Coverage-Driven Verification for Safety-by-Design in AI-Based Collision Avoidance Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20864)
*Thomas Stefani,Johann Maximilian Christensen,Elena Hoemann,Frank Köster,Sven Hallerbach*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents a method for assessing representativeness of AI/ML operational design domains in aviation safety, addressing EASA requirements for safety-critical AI integration through statistical distribution comparison techniques.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The aviation sector requires AI/ML systems to comply with stringent safety standards, with EASA emphasizing the need to demonstrate representativeness and completeness of Operational Design Domains (ODD) and data distributions. However, there's a lack of structured engineering processes for defining target distributions and evaluating representativeness within ODDs for safety-critical AI applications.

Method: The paper proposes a methodical process starting with identification of suitable target distributions, guiding developers from ODD definition and parameter distribution modeling to quantitative assessment of coverage results. The method examines chi-squared goodness-of-fit test but finds it unsuitable for large datasets, instead adopting Kullback-Leibler divergence and Cramér's V for representativeness assessment.

Result: The method is demonstrated using AI-based airborne collision avoidance with experimental data from Horizontal and Vertical Collision Avoidance System simulations. Results show how statistical distribution comparison methods can support representativeness assessment for safety-critical AI applications.

Conclusion: The proposed method contributes to developing a systematic Safety-by-Design AI engineering process aligned with emerging EASA guidance, enabling proper assessment of ODD representativeness for safety-critical aviation AI systems.

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence (AI) offers significant potential for future aviation systems; however, its integration into safety-critical applications requires compliance with the aviation sector's stringent safety standards. For AI and Machine Learning (ML)-based systems, the European Union Aviation Safety Agency (EASA) emphasizes the need to demonstrate the representativeness and completeness of the Operational Design Domain (ODD) and the associated data distributions used during development and verification. Despite this requirement, a structured engineering process for defining target distributions and evaluating representativeness within ODDs remains largely unexplored. This work presents a method for representativeness assessment of AI/ML constituent ODDs in the context of aviation safety assurance. Starting from the methodical identification of suitable target distributions, a process flow is proposed that guides developers from ODD definition and parameter distribution modeling to the quantitative assessment and interpretation of coverage results with respect to EASA's learning assurance objectives. As quantitative measures, the chi-squared goodness-of-fit test is examined and found unsuitable for the large data sets arising in this setting, leading to the adoption of the Kullback--Leibler divergence and Cramér's $V$ for the representativeness assessment. The method is demonstrated using the example of AI-based airborne collision avoidance, employing experimental data from previous Horizontal Collision Avoidance System (HCAS) and Vertical Collision Avoidance System (VCAS) simulations. The results illustrate how statistical distribution comparison methods can support the assessment of representativeness for safety-critical AI applications and contribute toward a systematic Safety-by-Design AI engineering process aligned with emerging EASA guidance.

</details>


### [57] [ReCurveflow: A Flow Matching Framework that Learns Curved Reaction Trajectories to Predict Transition State Geometries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20869)
*Seungheun Baek,Mogan Gim,Jaewoo Kang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReCurveflow is a novel flow matching framework that predicts chemical reaction transition states using curved reference paths and off-path correction, achieving state-of-the-art performance on multiple metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current transition state prediction methods use straight linear paths for supervision, but these don't align with actual reaction trajectories, limiting accuracy and real-world applicability.

Method: Proposes ReCurveflow framework that uses flow matching supervised on continuously curved reference paths interpolated from full NEB-derived molecular geometry bands, with off-path correction for better resistance to exposure bias.

Result: Achieves best results on majority of split-metric combinations against seven baselines across three data splits and six evaluation metrics, generating trajectories that closely track reference NEB paths.

Conclusion: ReCurveflow provides accurate transition state prediction, eases NEB optimization bottlenecks, and demonstrates effective corrective behavior in learned velocity fields, offering improved TS prediction framework.

Abstract: Predicting transition states (TS) in chemical reactions is crucial, as they provide insights into reaction mechanisms. Recent work on TS prediction have focused on flow matching supervised on straight linear paths that do not align with actual reaction trajectories. We propose a novel flow matching-based framework ReCurveflow that learns to predict TS geometries supervised on continuously curved reference paths interpolated from a full NEB-derived band of molecular geometries. We also introduce off-path correction, which grants ReCurveflow with the ability to produce corrective velocity fields when engaged off-path geometry states during inference rollout, leading to better resistance against exposure bias and accuracy in TS prediction. Across three data splits and six evaluation metrics, ReCurveflow achieves the best result on the majority of split-metric combinations against seven baselines. Qualitative analyses further show that ReCurveflow generates reaction trajectories with energy profiles that closely track the reference NEB path, provides initializations that ease the NEB optimization bottleneck, and exhibits the intended corrective behavior in its learned velocity fields. The ReCurveflow codebase is publicly available at https://github.com/dmis-lab/ReCurveflow.

</details>


### [58] [UpgradeBench: A Decision-Centric Benchmark for Upgrading Fine-Tuned LLM Specialists](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20918)
*Ye Chen,Weining Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: UpgradeBench is a longitudinal benchmark that evaluates migration decisions for task-specific adapters across consecutive model releases, assessing retraining performance, adapter transferability, and recovery strategies for maintaining specialists during model upgrades.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Organizations face migration decisions when new base-model releases occur: should they retain existing task-specific adapters, port them, refresh from preserved behavior, or retrain from scratch? Prior work only evaluates isolated model pairs without considering real model release sequences and practical upgrade scenarios.

Method: Created UpgradeBench covering four consecutive Qwen releases, one continuation checkpoint, six tasks, and two model sizes, augmented with OLMo checkpoints having known training lineage. The benchmark disentangles three core questions about specialist performance improvements, asset transferability, and usable recovery resources. Includes a lightweight CKA probe to predict cross-version adapter portability.

Result: Upgrade gains vary across task-scale-release episodes; direct adapter copying depends on continued pretraining distance (retention drops from 0.88-0.99 at 46B tokens to zero at 2.9T tokens); teacher relabeling can recover specialists without fresh annotations; a fixed decision policy yields 0.37pp mean quality regret at one-third the compute cost; CKA probe predicts portability with Spearman 0.74 correlation.

Conclusion: UpgradeBench provides a systematic framework for evaluating migration decisions during model upgrades, showing that upgrade patterns are heterogeneous, adapter portability decays with training distance, and lightweight probes can effectively predict transfer success, enabling more informed and cost-effective upgrade strategies.

Abstract: Organizations maintain task-specific adapters for open-weight language models, and each new base-model release forces a migration decision: retain existing specialists, port adapters, refresh from preserved behavior, or retrain. Prior transfer work evaluates isolated model pairs, without studying these choices across real model release sequences. We present UpgradeBench, a decision-driven longitudinal benchmark covering four consecutive Qwen releases, one continuation checkpoint, six tasks, and two model sizes, augmented by OLMo checkpoints with known training lineage. The benchmark disentangles three core questions: whether a new checkpoint improves fixed-recipe retrained specialist performance, whether specialization assets transfer across versions, and what recovery resources are usable. We observe upgrade gains differ across task-scale-release episodes: some retrained baselines improve while others stay within training noise, with durability ranging from under one release interval for text-to-SQL to over fourteen months for intent classification. Direct adapter copying depends neither on architecture nor model family: on OLMo, retention drops from 0.88-0.99 at 46B-token continued pretraining to zero at 2.9T tokens; annealing and model souping introduce no extra harm, with portability decaying with continued-pretraining distance. Given preserved input data, teacher relabeling recovers target-base specialists without fresh gold annotations, though compute savings are not guaranteed. Simulating a fixed decision policy over 33 upgrade episodes yields 0.37pp mean quality regret with zero behavioral regressions at one-third the compute and label cost of full retraining. A lightweight CKA probe over 256 prompts predicts cross-version adapter portability (Spearman 0.74 across eight model pairs). We release per-example predictions, cost logs, split manifests, and evaluation code.

</details>


### [59] [Graph-Operator World Models for Morphology-Parameter Generalization in Continuous Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20936)
*Xu Yang,Yiqin Yang,Qianchuan Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GraphOp-WM is a structured world model that factorizes transitions into morphology-independent dynamics and morphology-conditioned operators to generalize across unseen robot morphology parameters for articulated systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing world models for continuous control degrade when morphology parameters (link lengths, masses, damping, actuation) change. Current approaches provide parameters as conditioning but don't specify which parts of learned transitions should remain reusable vs. change with morphology, limiting generalization.

Method: Propose Graph-Operator World Models (GraphOp-WM) that represent bodies and kinematic relations as attributed graphs, factorizing transitions into: 1) morphology-independent local dynamics basis, and 2) morphology-conditioned structured operator with node-local modulation, kinematic-tree coupling, and low-rank global correction. Uses architectural information separation, basis normalization, and paired-morphology supervision.

Result: The method provides graph-level readout and edge-wise action representations compatible with reward, value, and TD-MPC-style planning. Authors define controlled MuJoCo parameter splits covering interpolation, extrapolation, and held-out compositions of link geometry, mass, damping, and actuation parameters across Hopper, Walker2d, and HalfCheetah environments.

Conclusion: GraphOp-WM offers a principled approach to generalize across unseen morphology parameters by explicitly separating reusable dynamics from morphology-dependent transformations, enabling better adaptation to varying robot morphologies within articulated families.

Abstract: World models for continuous control are commonly trained for a fixed physical system and can degrade when known morphology parameters such as link lengths, masses, damping, and actuation change. Existing approaches often provide these parameters as conditioning information, but leave unspecified which part of the learned transition should remain reusable and which part should change with morphology. We propose Graph-Operator World Models (GraphOp-WM), a structured world model for generalization across unseen morphology parameters within related articulated robot families. GraphOp-WM represents bodies and their kinematic relations as an attributed graph and factorizes each transition into a morphology-independent local dynamics basis and a morphology-conditioned structured operator. The operator combines node-local modulation, kinematic-tree coupling, and a low-rank global correction, while architectural information separation, basis normalization, and paired-morphology supervision encourage static morphology dependence to be carried by the operator pathway. Graph-level readout and edge-wise action representations provide a compatible interface for reward, value, and TD-MPC-style planning. We further define controlled MuJoCo parameter splits covering interpolation, extrapolation, and held-out compositions of link geometry, mass, damping, and actuation parameters in Hopper, Walker2d, and HalfCheetah.

</details>


### [60] [No Judgment Without a Reason: Counterfactual Receipts for Versioned AI Evaluators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20938)
*Ye Chen,Weining Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Paper proposes a framework for evaluator reasoning accountability with judgment receipts and counterfactual testing, showing strong models have flawed reasoning despite high accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard evaluation only checks final label correctness, ignoring whether correct judgments come from valid reasoning (evidence, consistent rules, proper rule application). Evaluators often produce correct labels via flawed reasoning, which is critical for systems gating actions, routing reviews, or supplying training feedback.

Method: Formalizes evaluator reasoning accountability via three core sources: grounds, norms, and authority. Creates an eight-cell counterfactual judgment cube to characterize judgment updates. Defines judgment receipts as minimal source replacement sets to explain judgment transitions. Presents ReasonBench, a policy and logical reasoning benchmark with verifiable receipts covering 19,520 cases and 7,200 controls for evaluation.

Result: Strong models show high accuracy but severe reasoning flaws. Qwen3-1.7B reaches 98.41% receipt accuracy but cube prediction drops to 96.99%. Meaning-preserving source permutations reduce valid receipt recovery to 54.8% and 49.2%. Models trained on single-source changes retain 93.75% accuracy but recover only 7.16% of receipts for multi-source updates. Permutation retraining boosts consistency but worsens cube prediction deficits.

Conclusion: Structured counterfactual supervision fails to guarantee robust reasoning. Reason-aware evaluation must decouple prediction and certification, reporting transformation consistency alongside standard accuracy for trustworthy evaluator auditing.

Abstract: Evaluators often produce correct labels via flawed reasoning, a critical failure for agentic systems gating actions, routing reviews, or supplying training feedback. Standard evaluation only verifies final label correctness, ignoring whether judgment changes stem from valid evidence, consistent rules, or proper rule applicability. We formalize evaluator reasoning accountability via three core sources: grounds, norms, and authority. Varying these sources yields an eight-cell counterfactual judgment cube to characterize judgment updates. We define judgment receipts as minimal source replacement sets that reproduce revised verdicts to explain judgment transitions. We derive certification cost bounds for black-box evaluators and present ReasonBench, a policy and logical reasoning benchmark with verifiable receipts covering 19,520 cases and 7,200 controls. In frozen evaluations, Qwen3-1.7B reaches 98.41% receipt accuracy, while cube prediction scores 96.99%, a consistent 1.42-point drop validated by Qwen3-0.6B replication. Strong standard accuracy masks severe robustness flaws. Meaning-preserving source permutations reduce valid receipt recovery to 54.8% and 49.2% for direct and cube prediction. Models trained on simple single-source changes retain 93.75% verdict accuracy but recover only 7.16% of receipts for complex multi-source updates. Permutation retraining boosts consistency to 96.6% yet worsens cube prediction deficits. Structured counterfactual supervision fails to guarantee robust reasoning. We show reason-aware evaluation must decouple prediction and certification, reporting transformation consistency alongside standard accuracy for trustworthy evaluator auditing.

</details>


### [61] [TLive-Omni: An Omni-Modal Understanding Model for E-Commerce Live Streaming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20958)
*Yibo Hu,Yu Qian,Mao Gu,Yingfan Tao,Yuhao Chen,Yongdong Luo,Zhuoqun Liu,Meiguang Jin,Junfeng Ma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TLive-Omni is an omni-modal understanding model for e-commerce live streaming that integrates video, audio, images, and text through a unified representation space and innovative temporal alignment techniques, achieving strong performance through progressive training and reinforcement fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: E-commerce live streaming requires understanding complex, noisy, temporally extended streams where product information is distributed across multiple modalities (speech, video frames, product images, overlaid text, user queries), creating a challenging omni-modal understanding problem.

Method: 1. TLive-Omni model with unified multimodal representation space
2. Per-vGrid: timestamped token organization for temporal alignment between video grids and audio
3. Three-stage supervised training: from omni-modal perception to instruction-following responses
4. Faithful-RFT: reinforcement fine-tuning for answer faithfulness and expression quality
5. Compact data production engine generating training signals for various tasks
6. Scalable training techniques including synchronized length-grouped sampler and dynamic sampling strategy

Result: Experiments demonstrate strong performance across live-commerce domain tasks and excellent generalization on general benchmarks. The model effectively handles real-time demands while maintaining answer faithfulness and expression quality.

Conclusion: TLive-Omni provides an effective solution for omni-modal understanding in e-commerce live streaming through innovative temporal alignment, progressive training approaches, and reinforcement fine-tuning for faithful responses, with demonstrated strong performance and generalization capabilities.

Abstract: E-commerce live streaming requires omni-modal understanding of noisy, temporally extended streams, where product facts are distributed across speech, video frames, product images, overlaid text, and user queries. We present TLive-Omni, an omni-modal understanding model tailored to live-commerce scenarios. It maps image, video, audio, and text inputs into a unified representation space. For long-form live streaming analysis, we introduce Per-vGrid, a timestamped token organization that groups each video grid with its temporally corresponding audio within explicit boundary tokens to facilitate temporal alignment. We design a three-stage supervised training recipe that progressively develops live-commerce understanding, from omni-modal perception to instruction-following responses. We then propose Faithful-RFT, a reinforcement fine-tuning stage that further improves answer faithfulness and expression quality while meeting real-time demands, scoring final responses directly with task-verifiable feedback rather than optimizing for reasoning-style exploration during rollout. Moreover, TLive-Omni is supported by a scenario-oriented atomic capability taxonomy and a compact data production engine that converts live-commerce audio, image, and video streams into training signals for speech recognition, speaker analysis, product visual grounding, text recognition, temporal grounding, video dense caption, and omni-modal QA, etc. For scalable training, a synchronized length-grouped sampler reduces padding while preserving comparable workloads across workers, while a lightweight dynamic sampling strategy regenerates rollout groups with near-zero reward variance to maintain meaningful relative advantages for GRPO. Experiments on e-commerce live streaming benchmarks demonstrate strong performance across live-commerce domain tasks, together with excellent generalization on general benchmarks.

</details>


### [62] [Can Scientific Claims Be Removed from Large Language Models? A Systematic Evaluation of Claim-Level Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20960)
*Snigdha Paul,Manasi Patwardhan,Arman Cohan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Scientific claim unlearning benchmark (SciUnlearn) introduced for removing outdated scientific knowledge from language models, showing current methods fail at claim-level forgetting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language models are trained on static scientific corpora while scientific knowledge continuously evolves, creating risk of disseminating outdated or retracted information. Scientific claims are interconnected and evolving, requiring specialized unlearning approaches beyond instance-level forgetting.

Method: Introduces the task of Scientific Claim Unlearning and presents a new benchmark called SciUnlearn. Evaluates current unlearning approaches on this benchmark to assess their effectiveness at claim-level knowledge removal.

Result: Current unlearning approaches are unable to effectively eliminate claim-level scientific knowledge and often achieve only superficial suppression rather than true forgetting of interconnected claims.

Conclusion: There is a need for specialized unlearning methods designed for structured knowledge removal in scientific contexts, as existing approaches fail to properly handle interconnected, evolving scientific claims.

Abstract: Language models (LMs) are trained on static scientific corpora, whereas scientific knowledge continuously evolves through correction and revision. Scientific claims encoded within these models may later become retracted, disproven, or updated by subsequent research, creating the risk of disseminating outdated information in scientific workflows. This creates a need for LMs to forget obsolete scientific claims. Machine unlearning offers a promising solution by enabling knowledge removal while maintaining overall model utility. Existing studies primarily investigate instance-level forgetting; however, scientific claims introduce additional challenges because they are interconnected, and continually evolving. To address this gap, we introduce the task of Scientific Claim Unlearning and present a new benchmark, SciUnlearn. We show that current unlearning approaches are unable to effectively eliminate claim-level knowledge and often achieve only superficial suppression, highlighting the need for specialized methods designed for structured knowledge removal.

</details>


### [63] [TreeWY: Speculative Verification for Gated DeltaNet Hybrids](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20961)
*Sneha Murthy Ghantasala*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposed method eliminates expensive recurrent state snapshots in hybrid models (linear-attention + small recurrent state) during speculative decoding, enabling wider draft trees and better memory efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hybrid models (Gated DeltaNet/GDN layers) with small recurrent states are memory-efficient for ordinary decoding but problematic for speculative decoding. Current systems require full recurrent state snapshots at every draft position, making wide, high-acceptance draft trees memory-infeasible due to inability to share snapshots across tree branches.

Method: Use a tree-structured WY transform of the gated delta rule to compute every draft node's output with a single triangular solve. Only reconstruct the accepted state on commit, storing a small pseudo-value matrix instead of per-node states. The derivation depends only on the gated delta rule, not on other architectural details.

Result: In serving benchmarks on Qwen3.5 35B and 397B models: cuts speculative recurrent-state memory and KV-cache pressure at identical acceptance length; turns freed HBM into higher throughput and much lower TTFT where memory binds; costs a few percent where memory doesn't bind. Enables wider, higher-acceptance draft trees with same memory budget, though not yet a throughput win.

Conclusion: The proposed method eliminates memory bottlenecks in speculative decoding for hybrid models, enabling wider draft trees and improving throughput and latency in memory-constrained scenarios without significant performance penalty in other cases.

Abstract: Modern open models are hybrids: most layers are linear-attention (Gated DeltaNet, GDN) layers carrying a small fixed-size recurrent state instead of a growing key-value (KV) cache. This makes ordinary decoding memory-efficient, but hurts speculative decoding. To verify a batch of draft tokens and then roll back the rejected ones, today's systems snapshot the full recurrent state at every draft position for GDN layers, and those snapshots cannot be shared across branches of a draft tree, so a wide, high-acceptance tree becomes memory-infeasible. We remove the snapshots. Using a tree-structured WY transform of the gated delta rule, we compute every draft node's output with a single triangular solve and reconstruct only the one accepted state on commit, storing a small pseudo-value matrix instead of per-node states; the derivation depends only on the gated delta rule, not on any other architectural detail. In serving benchmarks on two scales of one hybrid model family (Qwen3.5 35B and 397B) this cuts speculative recurrent-state memory and KV-cache pressure at identical acceptance length, turning the freed HBM into higher throughput and much lower time-to-first-token (TTFT) wherever memory binds, and costing a few percent where it does not. For tree width the same memory buys affordability: a wider, higher-acceptance draft becomes possible, though not yet a throughput win.

</details>


### [64] [Generalizing Soft Tissue Deformation and Force Prediction Across Material Stiffness and Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20967)
*Madina Kojanazarova,Sidaty El Hadramy,Philippe C. Cattin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents a softness-conditioned graph neural network that achieves real-time deformation and force prediction for soft tissues by addressing the critical dependency on well-calibrated constitutive models and enabling generalization across different stiffness levels and geometries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate soft tissue simulation is essential for surgical training, pre-operative planning, and haptic feedback systems, but existing learning-based surrogate models lack reliability due to poor calibration of constitutive models. Current approaches fail to provide systematic guidance for model selection across different stiffness levels and don't generalize well across varying tissue stiffnesses or geometries.

Method: 1) Comprehensive calibration of hyperelastic constitutive models in SOFA Framework using gravity-loaded silicone beams with different stiffnesses; 2) Using calibrated simulations as training data; 3) Developing a softness-conditioned equivariant graph neural network that enables deformation and force prediction across multiple tissue types and unseen geometries.

Result: The model achieves sub-millimeter mean deformation accuracy with 0.010s inference time (real-time performance). The study demonstrates that force prediction quality is directly tied to upstream calibration consistency, showing the critical importance of well-calibrated constitutive models for reliable force estimation.

Conclusion: The proposed approach successfully addresses the reliability gap in learning-based soft tissue simulation by providing systematic calibration methods and a generalized neural network architecture that works across different stiffness levels and geometries, paving the way for more robust real-time surgical simulations and haptic feedback systems.

Abstract: Accurate soft tissue simulation is essential for surgical training, pre-operative planning, and haptic feedback systems. While learning-based surrogate models trained on data using the finite element method (FEM) offer a promising path to real-time inference, their reliability depends on well-calibrated constitutive models. Existing approaches neither provide systematic guidance on model selection across stiffness levels, nor generalize across different tissue stiffnesses or geometries. We perform a comprehensive calibration of hyperelastic constitutive models in the SOFA Framework using gravity-loaded silicone beams with different stiffnesses. Using calibrated simulations as training data, we use a softness conditioned equivariant graph neural network, enabling deformation and force prediction across multiple tissue types and unseen geometries. Our model achieves sub-millimeter mean deformation accuracy at 0.010s inference time, while showing that force prediction quality is directly tied to upstream calibration consistency.

</details>


### [65] [Deep Learning Models Also Recall Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20970)
*Pierre Beckmann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces "feature recall" as a fundamental operation in deep learning where linear projections retrieve stored information scaled by input activations, contrasting it with feature combination and offering tools for mechanistic interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper is motivated by recent mechanistic interpretability research on how large language models recall facts from their weights, arguing that this points to a broader, more fundamental operation in deep learning models beyond just factual recall.

Method: The author defines feature recall conceptually, shows it applies across different neural network architectures, contrasts it with the established paradigm of feature combination, and considers methods for mechanistically identifying instances of feature recall.

Result: The paper establishes feature recall as a general operation in deep learning, demonstrating that linear projections function as retrieval mechanisms for stored information scaled by input activations, with applicability across various architectures.

Conclusion: Feature recall provides philosophers with a new conceptual framework for understanding deep learning and offers empirical directions for mechanistic interpretability research by identifying a fundamental computational operation underlying neural network behavior.

Abstract: Recent work in mechanistic interpretability has studied how large language models recall facts stored in their weights. This paper argues that factual recall points to something broader: a general kind of operation in deep learning models, which I call feature recall. The core observation is that a linear projection can be read as retrieving stored information scaled by input activations. I define feature recall, show it applies across architectures, and contrast it with the established paradigm of feature combination. I also consider how cases of feature recall might be mechanistically identified. The account gives philosophers a new conceptual tool for understanding deep learning, and points to empirical directions for mechanistic interpretability research.

</details>


### [66] [Belief Without Behavior: Measuring the Translation of Theory of Mind into Coordinated Social Action in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20975)
*Tonglin Yan,Gregoire Sergeant-Perthuis,David Rudrauf*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MOSAIC is a new benchmark that tests embodied agents' ability to translate theory of mind reasoning into coordinated multimodal behavior across verbal and nonverbal channels simultaneously, revealing critical bottlenecks in current VLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current benchmarks evaluate theory of mind reasoning and embodied behavior in isolation, leaving unmeasured the crucial gap between social inference and coordinated social action across multiple communication channels.

Method: Created MOSAIC benchmark where two embodied agents interact across cooperative/competitive scenarios requiring integration of verbal statements, spatial trajectories, gaze direction, and facial expression under systematically varied ToM constraints. Evaluated 13 models (including 11 VLMs) across 200 trials per model.

Result: VLMs fail to produce behaviors consistent with expected ToM-order constraints, and explicit ToM constraints produce no reliable behavioral change. Signal-level analysis reveals sequential bottlenecks: most models cannot produce coherent nonverbal signals, and even when signals are present, VLM agents fail to interpret and react to others' behaviors. PCM-LLM with explicit ToM module succeeds across all conditions.

Conclusion: Explicit belief-action coupling is necessary for translating theory of mind into coordinated multimodal social behavior. Current VLMs lack the architecture to bridge the gap between social inference and social action, highlighting the need for structured approaches that explicitly link reasoning to behavioral coordination.

Abstract: Effective social interaction requires agents to translate mental state inferences into coordinated behavioral signals across verbal and nonverbal channels simultaneously. Yet existing benchmarks evaluate theory of mind (ToM) reasoning and embodied behavior in isolation, leaving unmeasured the gap between social inference and social action. We introduce MOSAIC (Multimodal Orchestration of Social Action, Inference, and Communication), a controlled benchmark in which two embodied agents interact across cooperative and competitive scenarios requiring integration of verbal statements, spatial trajectories, gaze direction, and facial expression under systematically varied ToM constraints. Evaluating 13 models, including 11 VLMs, across 200 trials per model, we find that VLMs fail to produce behaviors consistent with the expected outcomes under ToM-order constraints, and that imposing explicit ToM-order constraints produces no reliable behavioral change aligned with the specified reasoning level. Signal-level analysis reveals two sequential bottlenecks: most models cannot produce directionally coherent nonverbal signals, and even when signals are present, VLM agents fail to interpret others behaviors and react to them. PCM-LLM, included as a structured architectural reference point with an explicit ToM module, succeeds across all conditions, suggesting that explicit belief-action coupling is a sufficient ingredient for this class of tasks.

</details>


### [67] [Don't Solve, Just Compare: Tiny Advisors for Runtime Intervention in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21027)
*Yanze Jiang,Mingxuan Li,Yuhao Wang,Shengfang Zhai,Jiaheng Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: COTA is a lightweight comparison-only framework for constructive runtime intervention in LLM agents that uses a tiny comparator to judge alternative continuations without needing expensive expert solvers or task-capable critics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM agents handle longer-horizon tasks requiring reasoning and decision-making, runtime intervention can improve reliability without retraining. Existing approaches either require expensive expert solvers or task-capable critics, creating significant costs or capacity demands.

Method: COTA introduces a comparison-only framework where a tiny comparator judges whether sampled alternatives lead to better continuations than the actor's proposal. It uses pairwise supervision from same-prefix counterfactual branches, and preferred alternatives are returned as non-binding advice for the original actor to replan.

Result: Across WebShop, ALFWorld, and tau^3-Retail with three different actors, COTA improves all nine evaluation settings and outperforms compared baselines, demonstrating effective constructive intervention even with substantially weaker auxiliary models.

Conclusion: Constructive runtime intervention can remain effective even when the auxiliary model has much weaker task-solving capability than the actor, enabling lightweight and cost-effective reliability improvements for LLM agents.

Abstract: LLM agents are emerging as an important paradigm for real-world tasks that require reasoning, tool use, and sequential decision-making. As these agents operate over longer horizons, runtime intervention offers a way to improve reliability without retraining the underlying actor. Failure detection alone is insufficient. Effective intervention must also provide a useful direction for recovery. Existing approaches often rely on an expert solver or a critic that generates task-specific corrections, incurring either the cost of another capable solver or the capacity demands of a task-capable critic. We introduce Comparison-Only Tiny Advisor (COTA), a comparison-only framework for constructive runtime intervention. In COTA, a tiny comparator judges whether sampled alternatives lead to better continuations than the actor's proposal, and repeated comparisons determine when intervention is warranted. We train the comparator using pairwise supervision constructed from same-prefix counterfactual branches. Preferred alternatives are returned as non-binding advice, leaving the original actor to replan. Across WebShop, ALFWorld, and tau^3-Retail with three actors, COTA improves all nine evaluation settings and outperforms the compared baselines. These results show that constructive runtime intervention can remain effective even when the auxiliary model has substantially weaker task-solving capability than the actor.

</details>


### [68] [Personalized Privacy Control in LLMs via Attention Head Intervention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21209)
*Junseok Kim,Nakyeong Yang,Kyomin Jung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces personalized privacy for LLMs, creates a benchmark (P3Bench) to evaluate personalized privacy policies, finds current models fail at enforcing these policies, and proposes an inference-time intervention method (Repair) to improve policy adherence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing contextual privacy approaches for LLMs don't account for individual user differences - they apply uniform privacy rules within contexts, but different users may have different disclosure preferences even in the same context.

Method: 1. Introduce personalized privacy concept that incorporates user-specific preferences; 2. Create P3Bench benchmark extending contextual privacy with personalized policies; 3. Test prompt-based approaches on models like Qwen2.5-7B and Gemma3-4B; 4. Propose Repair method - an inference-time attention head intervention technique to adjust disclosure behavior.

Result: Prompt-based policies fail badly: Qwen2.5-7B shows 51.25% policy ignorance ratio, Gemma3-4B shows 74.28%. The proposed Repair method significantly improves adherence to user-specific privacy preferences by reducing policy violation cases.

Conclusion: Personalized privacy is necessary as users have different disclosure preferences even within the same context. Current LLMs fail to enforce personalized policies via prompting alone, but inference-time interventions like Repair can effectively improve compliance with user-specific privacy preferences.

Abstract: The rise of agentic AI enables LLMs to access diverse user data, raising critical privacy concerns. Prior work on contextual privacy studies whether LLMs regulate information disclosure according to context-dependent norms. However, acceptable disclosure boundaries may vary across users even within the same context. To address this limitation, we introduce \textit{personalized privacy}, which incorporates user-specific disclosure preferences into privacy control. We further present P3Bench~(\textbf{P}ersonalized \textbf{P}rivacy \textbf{P}reservation \textbf{Bench}mark), a novel benchmark extending contextual privacy policies with personalized disclosure policies. Experiments show that prompt-based policies fail to reliably enforce personalized privacy policies, with Qwen2.5-7B and Gemma3-4B showing average policy ignorance ratios of 51.25\% and 74.28\%, respectively. Finally, to address this problem, we propose \textsc{Repair}, a robust inference-time attention head intervention method that adjusts disclosure behavior toward policy-consistent responses. Our method significantly improves adherence to user-specific privacy preferences by reducing cases where the model fails to follow the given policy.

</details>


### [69] [Evaluating Large Language Model Performance on International Maritime Dangerous Goods Code Compliance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21036)
*Alexander Thomas,Hubert P. H. Shum,Darren Nellis,Manli Zhu,Phatpicha Yochum,William Bartle,Daniel Wrightson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new benchmark (DGEval) evaluates LLMs on maritime dangerous goods regulations (IMDG Code), finding they perform well on structured lookups but poorly on safety-critical operational tasks, requiring human oversight.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Maritime transport of dangerous goods is high-consequence with complex regulations (IMDG Code). While practitioners increasingly use LLMs for decision support, there's no systematic evaluation of their reliability in interpreting safety-critical IMDG requirements.

Method: Created DGEval benchmark using 1,678 expert-written questions from NCB Hazcheck e-learning platform and structured Dangerous Goods List lookups. Evaluated 13 models across multiple configurations including domain-specific fine-tuned model, with tests on web search integration.

Result: Best-performing model exceeded human baseline on multiple-choice questions, but all models were weakest in safety-critical areas (stowage, segregation, regulatory recall). LLMs may support structured lookups with web search, but remain unreliable in operational areas.

Conclusion: LLMs can assist with certain compliance tasks but require human oversight and authoritative source verification for safety-critical applications. DGEval serves as an evolving safety assurance instrument for continuous evaluation as models improve.

Abstract: The transport of dangerous goods by sea is a high-consequence activity governed by the International Maritime Dangerous Goods (IMDG) Code, a complex regulatory framework where errors in classification, packaging, stowage, or segregation can result in fire, explosion, toxic release, or loss of life or vessel. Correct compliance requires accurately interpreting hundreds of pages of interacting provisions, updated on a two-year amendment cycle. Practitioners increasingly use Large Language Models (LLMs) as decision-support tools, yet no systematic evaluation exists of whether they can reliably interpret IMDG requirements for safety-critical use.
  This paper introduces DGEval, the first benchmark for evaluating LLM knowledge of IMDG Amendment 42-24. Built from expert-written questions on the NCB Hazcheck e-learning platform and structured lookups from the Dangerous Goods List (DGL), it comprises 1,678 questions across multiple-choice, open-ended, DGL lookup, and regulatory identification tasks. We evaluate 13 models from six providers across multiple thinking configurations, including one maritime domain-specific fine-tuned model, and test the effect of web search.
  Although the best-performing model exceeds the human practitioner baseline on multiple-choice questions, all models are weakest in the operationally safety-critical areas of stowage, segregation, and regulatory recall. These results indicate that LLMs may support compliance tasks, particularly structured DGL lookups with web search, but unreliability in operational areas and regulatory-text recall means human oversight and authoritative source verification remain necessary before deployment in any safety-critical context. DGEval is designed as a safety assurance instrument to be applied continuously as models evolve, not as a settled characterisation of current capability.

</details>


### [70] [Socialized Division and Collaboration: Rethinking Class-Incremental Learning under Optimization Conflicts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21044)
*Xinjie Yao,Zhihe Fan,Yunqi Zhu,Jiaqi Zhou,Dengyu Zhao,Zhoupeng Guo,Yan Fan,Guosong Jiang,Pengfei Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SDC reformulates continual learning by decomposing sessions across specialized models to handle optimization conflicts, using energy-based compatibility criteria for adaptive allocation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single-model continual learning struggles with destructive interference when successive sessions have incompatible optimization directions, causing catastrophic forgetting. The authors argue this is a structural limitation of forcing heterogeneous learning dynamics into one parameter space.

Method: Socialized Division and Collaboration (SDC) decomposes session learning across specialized models based on optimization conflicts. It uses an energy-based session-model compatibility criterion grounded in Helmholtz free energy to guide adaptive session allocation and model evolution. The framework integrates session assignment, model evolution, and collaborative inference into one pipeline.

Result: The paper presents a novel formulation that provides an alternative to monolithic continual learning approaches, demonstrating how specialization and collaboration can mitigate forgetting under persistent optimization conflicts.

Conclusion: SDC offers a principled multi-model approach to continual learning that handles conflicting objectives through specialized models and coordinated collaboration, highlighting a broader design principle for learning systems facing persistent optimization conflicts.

Abstract: Class-incremental learning is commonly instantiated as a single-model paradigm, where a unified model sequentially adapts to an unbounded stream of sessions. While effective under mild distributional shifts, this formulation becomes strained when successive sessions induce incompatible optimization directions, leading to destructive interference and catastrophic forgetting. We argue that such forgetting reflects a structural limitation of enforcing heterogeneous learning dynamics within a single parameter space. Motivated by social solidarity theory, we propose Socialized Division and Collaboration (SDC) as a reformulation of continual learning that decomposes session learning across specialized models in response to optimization conflicts, while enabling coordinated collaboration. To support this formulation with a principled allocation mechanism, we introduce an energy-based session-model compatibility criterion grounded in Helmholtz free energy, which guides adaptive session allocation and model evolution under conflicting objectives. This framework integrates session assignment, model evolution, and collaborative inference into a unified pipeline, offering an alternative to monolithic continual learning formulations and highlighting a broader design principle for learning under persistent optimization conflicts.

</details>


### [71] [The Cost of a Physics Prior Is Bounded by the Ablation Gap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21059)
*Boris Kriuk*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that the reported accuracy cost of enforcing shape constraints in machine learning is largely determined by free features and validation split design, not just properties of the prior. Using wildfire severity prediction as a case study, it demonstrates that cost estimates vary dramatically with validation protocols and proposes methods to screen for identifiable constraint experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Shape-constrained and physics-informed machine learning typically reports accuracy costs from enforcing priors and treats these costs as properties of the priors themselves. The authors challenge this assumption, arguing that reported costs are heavily influenced by experimental design choices, particularly free features and validation splits, making comparisons across studies difficult.

Method: Develops a theoretical bound showing that for any risk functional, the excess risk of a constrained model (P) is bounded between 0 and the excess risk of the ablated model that ignores the constrained features (D), with 0 ≤ P ≤ D. Empirically tests this using an ordinal wildfire severity prediction task (N=26,681, K=3 classes) with hard monotonic constraints on meteorological drivers while leaving spatial coordinates free. Uses a validation ladder from i.i.d. resampling to 2-degree spatial blocking to study protocol dependence.

Result: Shows that free features act as shields: spatial coordinates alone recover 92.9% of full model performance under spatial blocking, collapsing D from 0.1288 to 0.0427. The same monotonicity prior costs 0.0473 with shielding versus 0.3470 without (7.3x ratio). Demonstrates strong validation protocol dependence: coarsening spatial blocks from 1 to 10 degrees drives D from 0.0942 to 0.0050. Provides a two-fit screen to reject unidentifiable constraint experiments before training constrained models.

Conclusion: The accuracy cost of shape constraints depends more on experimental design (free features and validation splits) than on properties of the prior itself. Cost and compliance are independent measures, and reported constraint costs cannot be meaningfully compared across different validation protocols. The paper provides practical methods for bounding and interpreting constraint costs, including a self-calibrating resolution floor and a screening procedure for experiment identifiability.

Abstract: Shape-constrained and physics-informed learning reports an accuracy cost of enforcing a prior and treats it as a property of the prior. We show it is mostly a property of the free features and the validation split. Let P be the excess risk of restricting a hypothesis class to functions with a shape constraint on features S, and D the excess risk of the ablated model that ignores S. Because a function constant in x_j is both non-decreasing and non-increasing in x_j, the ablated class is contained in the constrained class, so 0 <= P <= D for every risk functional, with no convexity, smoothness, or realizability assumption. Empirically the bound is a sign test: a constrained model must never be beaten by its own ablation. We instantiate it on an ordinal wildfire-severity task (N = 26,681, K = 3) with hard monotone constraints on four meteorological drivers, coordinates left free, and a validation ladder from i.i.d. resampling to 2-degree spatial blocking. Coordinates act as a shield: alone they recover 92.9% of the full model's macro-F1 under spatial blocking, collapsing D from 0.1288 to 0.0427; the same prior costs 0.0473 shielded and 0.3470 unshielded, a ratio of 7.3 with identical physics. Because D is protocol-dependent it does not transfer: coarsening blocks from 1 to 10 degrees drives D from 0.0942 to 0.0050, leaving two configurations unidentifiable a priori. Inversions of the certified nesting bound the pipeline's additive resolution: over 318 comparisons they give a self-calibrating floor of 0.0220 macro-F1, below which no reported price is interpretable, including four cells in our own headline grid. Cost and compliance are independent: the unconstrained model violates the prior at rate 0.48-0.49 while enforcing it costs 0.0473. We give a two-fit screen that rejects unidentifiable experiments before a constrained model is trained.

</details>


### [72] [CellPath-Bench: A Multidimensional Benchmark for Whole-Slide Cellular Representations in Pathology Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21060)
*Bokai Zhao,Yiyang Zhang,Hanqing Chao,Yawei Ma,Long Bai,Tai Ma,Minfeng Xu,Ming Song,Tianzi Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CellPath-Bench is a new benchmark for evaluating pathology foundation models' cellular representation capabilities using spatially aligned H&E-Xenium tissue sections across multiple organs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks cannot systematically diagnose PFMs' whole-slide cellular representation capabilities, including cell-type decodability and transferability across tissue sections, datasets, and anatomical organs.

Method: Constructed a panel of 25 spatially aligned H&E-Xenium tissue sections spanning 11 organs and ~7M cells, harmonized into taxonomies. Benchmarks frozen WSI feature maps at registered nuclear coordinates using standardized multiclass linear probes with two metrics: Cell Representation Advantage (CRA) and Cell Representation Transferability (CRT).

Result: Benchmarked 30 pathology-specific and general-purpose foundation models through 304,920 runs, revealing substantial model-dependent differences in cell-type decodability and cross-domain generalization, yielding distinct multidimensional capability profiles.

Conclusion: CellPath-Bench provides a standardized framework for auditing cellular information in frozen PFM representations, enabling systematic evaluation of pathology foundation models' cellular representation capabilities.

Abstract: Pathology foundation models (PFMs) are increasingly used as general-purpose backbones, yet existing benchmarks cannot systematically diagnose their whole-slide cellular representation capabilities, including the decodability of cell-type information and the transferability of such information across tissue sections, datasets, and anatomical organs. We introduce CellPath-Bench, a cellular-resolution benchmark that evaluates frozen PFMs themselves. Following quality control of 52 candidate Xenium datasets, we construct a panel of 25 spatially aligned H\&E--Xenium tissue sections spanning 11 organs and 7,079,283 cells, harmonized into fine- and coarse-grained taxonomies. CellPath-Bench samples frozen WSI feature maps at registered nuclear coordinates and evaluates them using standardized multiclass linear probes. Cell Representation Advantage (CRA) measures the within-section advantage of nucleus-anchored representations over patch-level mean pooling, while Cell Representation Transferability (CRT) characterizes the generalization of cell-type decodability across tissue sections, datasets, and organs. We benchmark 30 pathology-specific and general-purpose foundation models through 304,920 runs across spatial readouts, magnifications, taxonomic granularities, and evaluation protocols. The results reveal substantial model-dependent differences in cell-type decodability and its cross-domain generalization, yielding distinct multidimensional capability profiles. CellPath-Bench provides a standardized framework for auditing cellular information in frozen PFM representations.

</details>


### [73] [Can Legal AI Know When It Is Wrong? And Do Students Know When It Is?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21089)
*Angel Mary John,Vipin Kumar Singh,Jerrin Thomas Panachakel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs in Indian judiciary show dangerous overconfidence in wrong legal verdicts, with law students poorly equipped to detect errors despite risks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Integrating LLMs into Indian judiciary promises improved access to justice but introduces severe risks of incorrect legal verdicts delivered with dangerous confidence, particularly concerning statutory updates like the Indian Contract Act amendments.

Method: Two-phase socio-technical audit: Phase I tested ChatGPT, Meta AI, and Perplexity AI on 60 cases regarding Indian Contract Act, using High-Confidence Error Rate (HCER) metric. Phase II surveyed 380 Indian law students on vulnerability to LLM overconfidence, measuring verification practices.

Result: All models struggled with statutory updates, with Meta AI most vulnerable (31.7% HCER, 9.1/10 confidence). Phase II revealed law students lack training on ethical AI use (71.1% no training) despite awareness of risks, and verification often occurs only reactively after encountering hallucinations.

Conclusion: LLMs in legal contexts exhibit dangerous 'inertia of confidence' leading to incorrect verdicts, while legal professionals lack proper training. The authors propose adversarial legal research pedagogy and source-grounded verification architectures to prevent systemic negligence.

Abstract: Integrating Large Language Models (LLMs) into the Indian judiciary promises access to justice but introduces severe risks. We identify the 'inertia of confidence'--an overconfidence phenomenon analogous to the Dunning-Kruger effect where LLMs provide incorrect legal verdicts with near-maximum confidence, driven by a hypothesized 'precedent overfitting' bias. Phase I of our socio-technical audit tested ChatGPT (GPT-5.2), Meta AI, and Perplexity AI on a 60-case battery regarding the Indian Contract Act, 1872, and the shift toward statutory enforcement of specific performance. We introduce the High-Confidence Error Rate (HCER) to quantify incorrect verdicts delivered with dangerous certainty (>= 9 on a 1-10 scale). All models struggled with statutory updates. Meta AI proved most vulnerable (31.7% HCER), frequently misapplying pre-amendment rules with a 9.1/10 mean confidence, followed by Perplexity (15.0%) and ChatGPT (6.7%).
  Phase II investigated human vulnerability to this overconfidence via a survey of Indian law students (N=380). Verification often functions as a reactive adaptation to machine hallucinations: students encountering fabricated citations reported higher verification scores (4.2/5) than those with no such encounters (2.8/5). Furthermore, while 81.6% knew submitting hallucinated cases can lead to contempt-of-court, 71.1% received no formal training on ethical AI use.
