We propose shifting toward adversarial legal research pedagogy and implementing source-grounded verification architectures to prevent systemic professional negligence.

</details>


### [74] [When Trust Meets Truth: Trust-Truth Separability in LLM-as-Judge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21097)
*Xin Sun,Di Wu,Yuchen Guo,Jiahuan Pei,Isao Echizen,Abdallah El Ali,Saku Sugawara*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-as-Judge systems show that trust scores and truth judgments are not independent - trust scores influence truth verdicts and vice versa, particularly when source cues (Human vs AI) are manipulated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-as-Judge systems produce multi-dimensional evaluations (trustworthiness, reliability, factuality) that are often treated as independent evidence. The paper investigates whether this independence assumption holds for common judgment pairs like trust scoring and binary truth classification.

Method: 1) Test on correctness-controlled QA to compare LLM judges' alignment of trust scores with truth verdicts vs human behavioral reference. 2) Apply stress tests by changing only source cues (Human vs AI) of identical QA pairs while keeping content identical.

Result: 1) LLM judges align trust scores with truth verdicts more tightly than human behavioral reference, showing weaker separation between trust and truth judgments. 2) Source attribution shifts not only trust scores but also truth verdicts and logit-derived correct-side probabilities - changing source from Human to AI influences both trust and truth judgments.

Conclusion: Current LLM-as-Judge protocols should not treat trust scores as independent evidence for truth judgments, as they are interdependent and influenced by factors like source attribution.

Abstract: LLM-as-Judge systems can produce multi-dimensional evaluations, such as trustworthiness, reliability, and factuality, and these outputs are often interpreted as independent evidence. We test this assumption for a common pair of judgments: trust scoring and binary truth classification. On correctness-controlled QA, LLM judges align trust scores with truth verdicts more tightly than human behavioral reference, suggesting weaker separations between trust and truth judgment. We then apply stress tests by changing only source cues of identical QA between Human and AI. Source attribution shifts not only trust scores but also truth verdicts and logit-derived correct-side probabilities. Results show that current LLM-as-Judge protocols should not treat trust scores as independent evidence for truth judgments.

</details>


### [75] [ReFrame: Evidence-Guided Test-Time Safety Alignment in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21100)
*Wenzheng Jiang,Xuankun Rong,Yuanzhao Zhai,Dawei Feng,Huaimin Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReFrame is a training-free framework that uses two agents sharing a local MLLM to generate risk/utility evidence and rewrite prompts, improving multimodal safety alignment without modifying deployed MLLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal LLMs make safety alignment challenging due to cross-modal jailbreaks, safety-awareness failures, and over-sensitive refusals. Existing methods often require retraining or internal-state inspection, which limits applicability to deployed closed-source MLLMs, motivating test-time safety alignment approaches.

Method: ReFrame uses a training-free multimodal input reframing framework with two agents sharing a lightweight locally deployed MLLM: 1) evidence-generation agent constructs complementary risk and utility evidence, and 2) rewrite-and-routing agent converts this into a safe proxy prompt and image-routing decision before calling the downstream MLLM.

Result: Experiments across multiple MLLMs and benchmarks show that ReFrame improves jailbreak defense, safety awareness, and oversensitivity reduction while preserving multimodal utility.

Conclusion: ReFrame provides an effective training-free approach for multimodal safety alignment that addresses key obstacles like utility dominance and reasoning inertia, enabling safer deployment of closed-source MLLMs without requiring internal access or model modifications.

Abstract: While multimodal large language models (MLLMs) extend model capabilities beyond text, they also make safety alignment increasingly challenging. Multimodal safety alignment methods must address cross-modal jailbreaks, safety-awareness failures, and over-sensitive refusals. However, existing methods often rely on retraining or internal-state inspection, limiting their applicability to deployed closed-source MLLMs and motivating test-time safety alignment. We analyze this setting and identify two key obstacles, utility dominance and reasoning inertia, which cause models to overlook latent risks or follow malicious reasoning trajectories. Guided by these insights, we propose ReFrame, a training-free multimodal input reframing framework where two agents share a lightweight locally deployed MLLM: the evidence-generation agent constructs complementary risk and utility evidence, and the rewrite-and-routing agent converts it into a safe proxy prompt and image-routing decision before calling the downstream MLLM, without modifying it or accessing its internal information. Experiments across multiple MLLMs and benchmarks show that ReFrame improves jailbreak defense, safety awareness, and oversensitivity reduction while preserving multimodal utility.

</details>


### [76] [Large Language Models at the Intersection of Software Engineering and Software Security:An Evidence-Centered Structured Survey and Research Agenda](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21107)
*Wei Lin,Tao Zhou,Zhaofei Xie,Changgui Hong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a structured survey synthesizing evidence on LLM-based software engineering and security agents, introducing an assurance framework that separates functional correctness, security, operational reliability, evidence provenance, and agent authority rather than relying on single benchmark scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are evolving from code completion tools to repository-scale agents handling complex workflows including security-sensitive tasks. Current evidence is divided between software engineering evaluations (focused on functional completion) and security evaluations (focused on vulnerabilities, secure generation, or exploit validation), creating a gap in comprehensive assessment.

Method: The authors conducted a structured survey of representative work available through May 31, 2026 across multiple dimensions: software engineering tasks, software security tasks, adaptation mechanisms, artifact granularity, and evaluation design. They developed a task taxonomy and introduced an assurance framework.

Result: The survey found that execution feedback and repository access improve engineering task completion but don't establish security, while static-analysis labels or vulnerability-classification scores rarely establish deployable correctness. Identified recurring validity threats include weak test oracles, duplicated/temporally leaked data, changing agent harnesses, proxy-only security checks, and under-reported budgets/human intervention.

Conclusion: Model capability should be judged as an assurance case supported by task-appropriate evidence rather than by a single benchmark score. The research agenda prioritizes jointly secure-and-functional benchmarks, repository-scale threat models, calibrated human oversight, longitudinal maintainability evidence, and reproducible agent evaluation.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are moving from code completion toward repository-scale agents that retrieve context, edit files, execute tools, and participate in security-sensitive workflows. The evidence for these systems, however, remains divided between software engineering evaluations centered on functional task completion and software security evaluations centered on vulnerability detection, secure generation, or exploit-oriented validation. This evidence-centered structured survey synthesizes representative work available through May 31, 2026 across software engineering tasks, software security tasks, adaptation mechanisms, artifact granularity, and evaluation design. In addition to a task taxonomy, we introduce an assurance framework that separates functional correctness, security, operational reliability, evidence provenance, and agent authority. The review shows that execution feedback and repository access can substantially improve engineering task completion, but do not by themselves establish security; conversely, static-analysis labels or vulnerability-classification scores rarely establish deployable correctness. We identify recurring validity threats--weak test oracles, duplicated and temporally leaked data, changing agent harnesses, proxy-only security checks, and under-reported budgets and human intervention--and derive a minimum reporting protocol for cross-study comparison. The resulting research agenda prioritizes jointly secure-and-functional benchmarks, repository-scale threat models, calibrated human oversight, longitudinal maintainability evidence, and reproducible agent evaluation. The central conclusion is that model capability should be judged as an assurance case supported by task-appropriate evidence, rather than by a single benchmark score.

</details>


### [77] [Root cause analysis via difference graph discovery from linear time-series data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21117)
*Anouk Ruer,Timothée Loranchet,Daria Bystrova,Charles K. Assaad*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper adapts difference graph discovery methods from population comparisons to time-series to identify effect-defying root causes - variables whose causal coefficients change between normal and anomalous regimes in complex dynamical systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the challenge of root cause analysis in complex dynamical systems, specifically identifying mechanisms responsible for anomalies. Traditional approaches may not capture changes in causal relationships during anomalous periods, which is crucial for understanding the underlying mechanisms driving abnormal behavior.

Method: The authors formalize the problem using linear discrete-time dynamic structural causal models. They adapt several difference graph discovery methods originally designed for comparing two populations to the time-series setting, where the two populations are replaced by normal and anomalous regimes. The approach focuses on detecting "effect-defying root causes" - variables whose causal coefficients change between regimes.

Result: The proposed approaches were evaluated on simulated data and demonstrated practical relevance on real-world datasets from IT monitoring and intensive care monitoring. The results show that difference graph discovery can effectively help localize causal mechanisms responsible for anomalous behavior in complex dynamical systems.

Conclusion: Difference graph discovery methods, when adapted to time-series analysis, provide a valuable framework for identifying effect-defying root causes in complex dynamical systems. This approach enables the localization of causal mechanisms that change during anomalous periods, offering practical utility in real-world monitoring applications.

Abstract: Root cause analysis aims to identify the mechanisms responsible for anomalies in complex dynamical systems. In this paper, we study root cause analysis in linear time-series through the lens of difference graph discovery. We focus on effect-defying root causes, corresponding to variables whose causal coefficients change between a normal and an anomalous regime. We formalize this problem using linear discrete-time dynamic structural causal models and adapt several methods originally introduced for discovering difference graphs between two populations to the time-series setting, where the two populations are replaced by a normal and an anomalous regime. We first evaluate the proposed approaches on simulated data, and then demonstrate their practical relevance on real-world datasets from IT monitoring and intensive care monitoring. Our results show how difference graph discovery can help localize causal mechanisms responsible for anomalous behavior.

</details>


### [78] [From Attention Masks to Inert Zero-Vector Tokens: OAttention and O-Closure for Token Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21174)
*Heyang Gong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OAttention introduces active-presence coefficients to gate token participation in attention, enabling exact zero-element tokens and compositional closure properties while maintaining compatibility with standard attention mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard attention masks only control which query-source pairs can interact, but don't provide proper representation of tokens that should be non-participating at the attention boundary. There's a need for tokens to have a proper "inactive" or "null" state that can be exactly represented and composed with other operations.

Method: Assign each token hidden carrier h_i an active-presence coefficient p_i = ||h_i||²/(τ+||h_i||²). This coefficient gates information emitted by token i and determines its mass in shared computations. OAttention gates receiver output by p_i and weights source j by p_j in both attention numerator and partition while keeping standard attention structure. This enables OComponents (OFFN, ONorm, OInject), presence-weighted standardization, and O-Closure law.

Result: The approach yields exact null-receiver, null-source insertion, self-attention insertion, and empty-support properties. In zero-fine-tuning retrofit of TabPFN v3 regressor, OAttention variants change mean RMSE by only +0.088% and +0.177% over 18 dataset-seed cases. Ablation shows OAttention alone doesn't preserve NULL state through ordinary host components, but OTransformer path does.

Conclusion: OAttention provides a principled way to handle token participation through active-presence coefficients, enabling exact null states and compositional closure. The approach shows compatibility with existing models while demonstrating the necessity of full OTransformer composition for maintaining null state properties. This represents a foundation for exact token absence handling rather than a universal solution.

Abstract: Attention masks are relation-level controls: they specify which query--source pairs may interact. They do not provide a representation-carried token state that is non-participating at the attention boundary. We assign each token hidden carrier \(h_i\) an active-presence coefficient \(p_i=\lVert h_i\rVert^2/(τ+\lVert h_i\rVert^2)\). The same coefficient has two roles: it gates information emitted by token \(i\), and it determines the mass with which token \(i\) enters computations shared with other tokens.
  OAttention is the support-coupled attention realization of this rule. It gates the receiver output by \(p_i\) and weights source \(j\) by \(p_j\) in both the attention numerator and partition, while retaining the standard score, visibility relation, exponential competition, and value aggregation. This makes the zero-vector token a zero element and yields exact null-receiver, null-source insertion, self-attention insertion, and empty-support properties. The same token-level presence gives local O-components (OFFN, ONorm, and OInject), presence-weighted OStandardize, the O-Closure law \(M(H\oplus0)=M(H)\oplus0\), and an OTransformer by residual and compositional closure.
  The canonical operator is checked by contract tests and a GPU evaluation. In a zero-fine-tuning retrofit of a cloned pretrained TabPFN v3 regressor, calibrated hidden-carrier OAttention and Full-O variants change mean RMSE by $+0.088\%$ and $+0.177\%$, respectively, over 18 matched dataset--seed cases. A two-block ablation shows that OAttention alone does not preserve a NULL state through ordinary host components, whereas the OTransformer path does. These are scoped tests of exactness, active-path compatibility, and compositional necessity; they do not establish universal no-loss, arbitrary-host closure, learned attraction to the origin, or a general semantics for missing values.

</details>


### [79] [SENTRY: Deterministic, Intelligent Risk Assessment for IT Change Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21203)
*Daniel Arulpragasam,Christer Henrysson,Ella Ly,Deepika Anbalagan,Leo Feng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SENTRY is a machine learning platform that replaces subjective questionnaires with deterministic XGBoost and hybrid RAG models for risk assessment in financial institution change management, achieving 0.87 ROC AUC and detecting high-risk changes 3.25x better than existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current risk assessment methods in large financial institutions rely on subjective, gameable questionnaires that poorly distinguish routine changes from those likely to trigger major incidents, creating auditability and accuracy concerns.

Method: SENTRY uses a deterministic ML pipeline with gradient-boosted decision trees (XGBoost) and hybrid retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). It combines structured operational metadata, application dependency graphs, historical incidents with semantic/lexical search over change requests. The system extracts risk signals from unstructured text, compresses them to scalar features for model inference while maintaining explainability via SHAP values.

Result: On enterprise-scale change data, SENTRY achieves 0.87 ROC AUC, 85% overall accuracy, and detects high-risk changes approximately 3.25 times more effectively than the existing questionnaire-based process.

Conclusion: SENTRY demonstrates that ML can provide accurate, consistent, and auditable risk assessments for technology change management in regulated financial institutions, though careful architectural trade-offs are required for compliance.

Abstract: Technology change management in large financial institutions depends on risk assessments that are accurate, consistent, and auditable. In practice, many institutions still rely on self-reported questionnaires. Those questionnaires are subjective, easy to game, and poor at separating routine changes from the ones that later trigger major incidents. This paper presents SENTRY, a risk assessment platform that replaces questionnaire-based scoring with a deterministic machine learning pipeline built from gradient-boosted decision trees (XGBoost) and hybrid retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). The system combines structured operational metadata, application dependency graphs, and historical incident records with a hybrid semantic and lexical search over historical change requests. The retrieval step captures the risk signal in unstructured change request text, then compresses that signal into a single scalar feature before model inference. That design keeps the model deterministic and preserves per-prediction explainability via SHAP values. Evaluated on enterprise-scale change data, SENTRY achieves a ROC AUC of 0.87 and 85% overall accuracy, and it detects high-risk changes at roughly 3.25 times the rate of the existing process. We close by examining the architectural trade-offs behind this design and what they imply for the use of machine learning in regulated change management.

</details>


### [80] [Enhancing LLMs in Predictive Political QA with Semi-Structured Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21218)
*Yinan Liu,Zihan Zhou,Zichun Jin,Xinyu Wang,Bin Wang,Xiaochun Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PSL framework combines actor stance signals and high-order structure signals from political records to improve predictive political question answering, outperforming existing augmentation methods across multiple datasets and LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Predictive political QA (like predicting voting behavior) requires reasoning beyond factual lookup. While external political resources provide historical evidence, they rarely contain direct answers. Existing LLM augmentation methods treat these resources as knowledge-based evidence but fail to fully model prediction-relevant signals, leaving important predictive patterns under-utilized.

Method: Proposes PSL, a dual-view framework that converts semi-structured political records into inference-oriented evidence. It extracts two complementary signals: 1) actor stance signals from question-relevant records (semantic view), and 2) high-order structure signals capturing indirect dependencies among actors via actor interaction graphs (vector view). The framework integrates these signals to enhance LLM reasoning for political predictions.

Result: PSL consistently outperforms baseline methods across three real-world datasets and multiple LLMs. Ablation studies confirm that both stance signals and structure signals provide complementary gains, demonstrating their combined effectiveness for predictive political QA.

Conclusion: The identification and modeling of complementary stance and structure signals from political records significantly improves predictive political QA performance. The PSL framework successfully converts raw political data into inference-oriented evidence, offering a more comprehensive approach to political reasoning compared to existing knowledge-based evidence methods.

Abstract: Predictive political question answering (QA), such as predicting how a political actor will vote, goes beyond factual lookup. External political resources offer rich historical evidence, but rarely contain the answer itself. Existing LLM augmentation methods, including actor-profile-based simulation and knowledge graph evidence injection, improve political reasoning but largely treat external resources as knowledge-based evidence, leaving prediction-relevant signals under-modeled. We identify two complementary signals for predictive political QA: actor stances that capture issue-specific preferences, and high-order structure signals that capture indirect dependencies among political actors. We propose PSL, a dual-view framework that converts semi-structured political records into inference-oriented evidence for LLMs. PSL extracts stance signals from question-relevant actor records in a semantic view, and learns structure-aware actor representations from an actor interaction graph in a vector view. Across three real-world datasets and multiple LLMs, PSL consistently outperforms baselines, with ablations confirming the complementary gains of stance and structure signals.

</details>


### [81] [Ontology-supported AI Model and Dataset Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21224)
*Jan Novacek,Ali Ahari,Tobias Müller,Sebastian Reiter,Alexander Viehl,Oliver Bringmann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A platform for AI model exchange with ontology-based semantic understanding to enable effective asset management and discovery in industrial AI contexts


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrial AI applications generate numerous assets (models, datasets) that need tracking, tracing, and discovery for transparent progress comparisons and deeper understanding. There's a need for effective exchange and management of these assets without semantic gaps in industrial contexts.

Method: Developed a platform for AI model exchange that facilitates usage, exchange, and analysis of AI models and datasets. Incorporated an ontology to foster common understanding of requirements and address semantic issues.

Result: Created a functional platform with ontology support that enables effective asset management and exchange. Demonstrated utility through a real-time critical systems use case illustration.

Conclusion: The platform with integrated ontology addresses industrial AI asset management challenges by enabling semantic-rich exchange and analysis, particularly valuable for complex domains like real-time critical systems.

Abstract: Recently, there has been a great deal of research into improving AI methods and their application. The main focus is on tracking progress, enabling transparent comparisons, and fostering a more profound understanding of AI. In that process, different organizations generate and use plenty of assets that need to be tracked, traced and managed. Moreover, it is important to discover assets relevant for the task at hand. This paper presents research aiming to contribute to answering the question of what is required to exchange and manage AI models and related assets effectively without semantic gaps in an industrial context. We introduce a platform for AI model exchange, which facilitates the usage, exchange, and analysis of AI models and datasets. The platform incorporates an ontology that can foster a more profound common understanding of what is required in these tasks and help tackle the issues mentioned above. Finally, we elucidate the utility of the platform through the illustration of a use case in the context of real-time critical systems.

</details>


### [82] [Fine-Grain GPU Parallelization of the Generalized Partition Crossover for Large-Scale Traveling Salesman Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21233)
*Swetha Varadarajan,Darrell Whitley*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A GPU implementation of the partition phase of Generalized Partition Crossover (GPX) for large-scale TSP achieves 48-625x speedup over CPU by reformulating the problem using graph-parallel techniques and coalesced memory access.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Genetic Algorithm-based TSP solvers like EAX achieve state-of-the-art performance but face scalability issues on parallel architectures due to irregular memory access patterns and sequential dependencies in crossover operations, especially for large problem sizes.

Method: Reformulate GPX partitioning as graph-parallel problem using coalesced memory layouts, ghost-node transformations, and connected-component analysis. Parallelize key steps (union of parent tours, splitting degree-four vertices, deletion of common edges, recombining component identification) using CUDA on GPU.

Result: Achieved 48-625x speedup over naive sequential CPU implementation on TSP instances ranging from 10,000 to 2 million cities, with significant memory overhead reduction.

Conclusion: Operator-level parallelism on GPU can substantially improve scalability of GA-based TSP solvers on modern many-core architectures, enabling efficient processing of very large problem instances.

Abstract: The Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) is one of the most extensively studied NP-hard optimization problems. Genetic Algorithm (GA)-based solvers, such as the Edge Assembly Crossover (EAX), achieve state-of-the-art performance on many benchmark instances. However, the scalability of these approaches in massively parallel architectures remains limited because crossover operations involve irregular memory access patterns, graph traversals, and sequential dependencies. Existing GPU-based TSP solvers primarily exploit population-level parallelism and are limited to relatively small problem sizes. This work presents a fine-grain GPU implementation of the partition phase of the Generalized Partition Crossover (GPX) operator for large-scale TSP instances. The proposed approach reformulates GPX partitioning as a graph-parallel problem using coalesced memory layouts, ghost-node transformations, and connected-component analysis. The im- plementation parallelizes the union of parent tours, the splitting of degree- four vertices, the deletion of common edges, and the identification of recombining components using CUDA. Experimental results on instances ranging from 10,000 to 2 million cities demonstrate substantial acceleration over a naive sequential CPU imple- mentation. The proposed GPU partitioning achieves speedups between 48x and 625x while significantly reducing memory overhead. The re- sults demonstrate that operator-level parallelism can substantially im- prove the scalability of GA-based TSP solvers on modern many-core architectures.

</details>


### [83] [CLEAR: Continuous Latent Adapter Routing for Utility-Preserving LLM Safety Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21278)
*Chengxiao Wang,Enyi Jiang,Xiaojing Liao,Sanmi Koyejo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CLEAR is a conditional safety adaptation framework that uses hidden-state gating to selectively activate safety adapters only for harmful inputs, improving the safety-utility trade-off in LLM alignment without degrading performance on benign prompts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current safety tuning methods (like SFT or LoRA) degrade model utility on benign inputs because they apply safety adaptations globally to all inputs, regardless of whether the prompt is harmful or benign. There's a need for selective safety interventions that only activate when needed.

Method: CLEAR (Continuous Latent Adapter Routing) uses a lightweight hidden-state gate to continuously control the activation strength of a safety low-rank adapter. The system dynamically routes safety interventions based on input characteristics, allowing selective activation only for harmful prompts while preserving normal operation for benign ones.

Result: On Llama-3-8B-Instruct, CLEAR reduces HarmBench attack success rate from 32.3% to 0.5%, while maintaining most of the base model's utility. It achieves up to 7.1 percentage points higher GSM8K accuracy compared to globally applied SFT or LoRA safety tuning methods.

Conclusion: CLEAR provides a promising mechanism for improving the safety-utility trade-off in LLM alignment by enabling conditional safety adaptation that only activates when needed, avoiding the utility degradation associated with globally applied safety tuning approaches.

Abstract: Improving the safety of large language models (LLMs) often comes at the expense of utility, as globally applied safety tuning may affect model responses to both harmful and benign inputs. We propose \textbf{C}ontinuous \textbf{L}at\textbf{E}nt \textbf{A}dapter \textbf{R}outing (CLEAR), a conditional safety adaptation framework that uses a lightweight hidden-state gate to continuously control the activation strength of a safety low-rank adapter. CLEAR aims to reduce harmful completions while avoiding unnecessary changes to the frozen backbone that could degrade performance on benign prompts. Experiments on widely used safety and utility benchmarks show that CLEAR improves robustness on HarmBench while reducing the utility degradation observed with globally applied safety tuning such as SFT or standard low-rank adaptation (LoRA). On Llama-3-8B-Instruct, CLEAR reduces HarmBench ASR from 32.3\% to 0.5\%, while retaining most of the base model's utility and achieving up to 7.1 percentage points higher GSM8K accuracy than globally applied SFT or LoRA. These results suggest that CLEAR is a promising mechanism for improving the safety--utility trade-off in LLM alignment.

</details>


### [84] [AUSO: Action-Level Unified Skill Optimization from Internalization to Utilization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21292)
*Huizu Lin,Chengkai Huang,Tianqi Gao,Tao Huang,Daijiao Liu,Tongxin Li,Xiaoyan Sun,Lina Yao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AUSO is a unified skill optimization method that treats skills differently throughout policy evolution by learning from them early, then consolidating autonomous problem-solving, and finally using them adaptively at action-level to improve decisions while suppressing harmful uses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods don't properly model the lifecycle of skills in agent learning - they either keep skills external, fully internalize them, or use noisy task-level success rates. These approaches fragment training, assign uniform importance to actions, and don't recognize that skills may help some decisions while distracting others.

Method: AUSO unifies skill learning and use through progressive, action-aware optimization. Early training: jointly learns from teacher guidance and environmental outcomes. Mid-training: emphasizes outcome-based policy optimization to build autonomous problem-solving. Late training: evaluates each sampled action under both skill-conditioned and skill-free contexts, coupling action-level information with trajectory outcome advantage to update beneficial skill-sensitive actions strongly while suppressing harmful ones.

Result: Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and SearchQA show that AUSO consistently improves agent performance and out-of-distribution generalization over competitive baselines.

Conclusion: AUSO successfully models the skill lifecycle by treating skills as an external source of supervision that gradually transitions into decision knowledge, with utilization adapted to action-level benefit, while reinforcement learning serves as the shared backbone across all training stages.

Abstract: Skills play different roles as an agent's policy evolves: they should first provide learnable knowledge, then support capability formation, and finally be invoked only when they improve individual decisions. Existing methods rarely model this lifecycle. They either keep skills outside the model, fully internalize them, or select among internalization and utilization objectives through noisy task-level success rates. Such designs fragment training and assign uniform importance to actions within the same trajectory, even though skill guidance may help some decisions while distracting others. To solve these problems, we introduce AUSO (Action-level Unified Skill Optimization), which unifies skill learning and skill use through a progressive, action-aware optimization process. At the beginning of training, AUSO jointly learns from teacher guidance and environmental outcomes, enabling the policy to acquire foundational skills without losing task-oriented feedback. It subsequently emphasizes outcome-based policy optimization to consolidate autonomous problem-solving ability. As the policy matures, AUSO evaluates each sampled action under both skill-conditioned and skill-free contexts. The resulting action-level information signal is coupled with the trajectory outcome advantage, allowing beneficial skill-sensitive actions to receive stronger updates and harmful ones to be suppressed. Therefore, skills gradually transition from an external source of supervision into decision knowledge whose utilization is adapted to its action-level benefit, while reinforcement learning remains the shared backbone across all stages. Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and SearchQA show that AUSO consistently improves agent performance and out-of-distribution generalization over competitive baselines.

</details>


### [85] [From Regulation to Implementation: A Critical Evaluation of LLM-Assisted Regulatory Compliance in Industry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21317)
*Adriana Watson,Marco Bücheler,Grant Richards*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Researchers investigate how data extraction challenges and regulatory ambiguity affect LLM-generated compliance artifacts for EU sustainability and privacy regulations, finding that stricter formatting guidelines reduce consistency issues but increase hallucinations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EU regulations like ESPR (requiring Digital Product Passports) and GDPR (requiring Data Protection Impact Assessments) create compliance artifacts that are difficult to produce due to heterogeneous data formats, scattered industrial data, lack of standardization, and interdisciplinary expertise requirements. While LLMs have been proposed to generate these artifacts, it's unclear how data extraction issues and regulatory vagueness affect the quality and consistency of LLM outputs.

Method: The study explores how data extraction instructions and regulatory vagueness impact LLM-produced compliance artifacts. Researchers benchmark different LLM models against manually created ground-truth schemas to evaluate the quality and consistency of generated artifacts for both types of regulations.

Result: Less strict guidelines (like DPIA formatting) require higher context prompts to maintain consistency and completeness. Stricter guidelines (like Digital Battery Passport formatting) result in more consistent outputs regardless of prompt context but lead to more hallucinations in the generated artifacts.

Conclusion: The effectiveness of LLMs for regulatory compliance artifact generation depends on the specificity of formatting requirements. Vague regulations require more contextual prompting, while strict formatting reduces consistency issues but introduces hallucination risks. This highlights the need for careful prompt engineering and model selection based on regulatory requirements.

Abstract: The European Union (EU) has emerged as a leading regulatory body in the development of sustainability and privacy regulations. While new regulation requirements vary, many include a documentation artifact to ensure compliance. Notably, the Ecodesign for Sustainable Products Regulation (ESPR) introduces Digital Product Passports (DPPs) for life cycle transparency, while the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) mandates Data Protection Impact Assessments (DPIAs) to mitigate privacy risks. Creating these compliance artifacts, however, is challenging. Industrial data, which often exists in heterogeneous formats and is scattered across company and supplier systems, is required for DPPs and can be difficult to extract into compliant DPP formatting. Furthermore, DPIA documents require interdisciplinary expertise and follow no standardized format, making development difficult for novel systems. To address the particular complexity of compliance artifact creation for both regulations, researchers have proposed the use of LLMs in the generation process; however, the impact of the aforementioned problems on the output of these systems is largely unaddressed. This work investigates the existing research gap by exploring how data extraction instructions and regulatory vagueness impact the quality and consistency of LLM-produced compliance artifacts. The resulting artifacts are evaluated by benchmarking different models against manually created ground-truth schemas. The results reveal that less strict guidelines, such as DPIA formatting, require higher context prompts to maintain consistency and completeness. Stricter guidelines, such as formatting for Digital Battery Passports (DBP), result in consistent results regardless of prompt context, but may lead to more hallucinations in the output

</details>


### [86] [Unified Branch-and-Bound Search for the Steiner Traveling Salesman Problem on Graphs of Convex Sets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21319)
*Jingtao Tang,Hang Ma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A branch-and-bound algorithm for Steiner Traveling Salesman Problem on Graphs of Convex Sets, with provable finite termination and certified optimality bounds, applied to mobile manipulator inspection with temporal logic constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Steiner-TSP on Graphs of Convex Sets combines complex constraints from routing, geometry, and logic needed for real-world applications like robotic inspection, where agents must visit target sets with continuous trajectories, optional transit points, and temporal logic precedence constraints.

Method: Unified branch-and-bound search over rooted walk prefixes using additive lower-bound-graph costs for committed prefixes and cut-separated connected-flow relaxation for residual cost estimation. Provides two traversal strategies (best-first and depth-first) with certified optimality guarantees.

Result: The algorithm finds feasible solutions on all benchmark instances within 30 seconds with mean certified optimality gaps of 28.1% (best-first) and 29.7% (depth-first), outperforming recent baselines that succeed on only about half of instances.

Conclusion: The proposed method effectively solves complex Steiner-TSP on Graphs of Convex Sets problems with provable finite termination and certified optimality bounds, enabling joint optimization of sensing modes, visitation orders, and continuous trajectories for real-world robotic applications with temporal logic constraints.

Abstract: We formalize the Steiner Traveling Salesman Problem (Steiner-TSP) on Graphs of Convex Sets (GCS), which seeks a minimum-cost closed trajectory through required convex sets while allowing optional transit vertices and revisits. To explore the resulting infinite solution space, we propose a unified branch-and-bound search over rooted walk prefixes. Additive lower-bound-graph costs bound committed prefixes, while a cut-separated connected-flow relaxation lower-bounds the residual cost of visiting every remaining target and returning to the root. Under a uniform positive-cost assumption, best-first traversal terminates after finitely many expansions on every feasible instance without an initial incumbent, whereas depth-first traversal does so once a finite incumbent is available. For a user-specified factor $ε\geq1$, a global lower bound certifies that either strategy's incumbent cost is at most $ε$ times the global optimum. We further demonstrate joint sensing-mode, visitation-order, and continuous-trajectory selection for a mobile-manipulator inspection task, including action precedences expressed in linear temporal logic over finite traces (LTL$_f$). Both traversal strategies find feasible solutions on all benchmark instances within 30s with mean certified optimality gaps of 28.1% and 29.7%, respectively, whereas two recent baselines succeed on only about half of the instances

</details>


### [87] [Anatomy-Informed Neural Networks: Encoding Anatomic Priors in Loss and Architecture, with an SE(3) Formulation of Guidewire-Induced Aortoiliac Deformation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21332)
*David P. Stonko*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AINN combines soft anatomic priors as loss penalties and hard priors in architecture to create anatomically plausible deep learning models that generalize better with limited data, demonstrated on aortoiliac tree deformation during endovascular procedures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep-learning models of anatomy can be numerically plausible yet anatomically impossible, and they generalize poorly when data are scarce. There's a need for anatomically-informed neural networks that ensure physically plausible predictions while reducing data requirements.

Method: Introduces Anatomy-Informed Neural Networks (AINN) with soft anatomic priors as penalty terms in loss functions and hard anatomic priors built into architecture. Applied to aortoiliac tree deformation when stiff wire is introduced endoluminally, using SE(3) representation of curves, Cosserat-rod wire model, coupled elastic energy minimization with contact forces, and supervision via Wasserstein-2 optimal-transport loss between predicted projections and angiograms.

Result: Developed AINN framework on clinical test case showing how to predict aortoiliac tree deformation from 2D angiograms. The kinematics, loss, and projection are verified against ground truth, though predicted displacement is not yet mesh-converged. No network training was performed in this initial work.

Conclusion: AINN provides a promising framework for anatomically plausible neural networks that can generalize better with limited data. Future work will transfer this in silico model to real CT scans to test whether it improves predictive accuracy and reduces training data requirements.

Abstract: Deep-learning models of anatomy can be numerically plausible yet anatomically impossible, and they generalize poorly when data are scarce. We introduce Anatomy-Informed Neural Networks (AINN), in which soft anatomic priors enter as penalty terms in the loss (e.g., a branching penalty that treats a renal transplant artery off the iliac instead of the aorta as unexpected rather than impossible), in direct analogy to a physics-informed neural network, and hard anatomic priors (e.g., continuity of the vessel) are built into the architecture and state representation, making such invalid predictions impossible by construction wherever the prior admits architectural enforcement.
  We develop it on a clinical test case with limited data: how the aortoiliac tree deforms when a stiff wire is introduced endoluminally. This is important to contemporary aortic surgery and will matter to autonomous endovascular navigation. We lift the vessel centerline and the wire path from R^3 to curves of frames in the Lie group SE(3), and couple a Cosserat-rod wire to a tortuosity-modulated, anatomically anchored vessel through a unilateral lumen-contact inequality. The prediction is a constrained minimizer of the coupled elastic energy, with contact forces as its Lagrange multipliers. Supervision is a Wasserstein-2 optimal-transport loss between the predicted projection through the C-arm geometry and the observed angiogram, so a 2D angiogram can train a 3D prediction. The kinematics, loss and projection are verified against known ground truth; the mechanics solver only against its own optimality conditions, and predicted displacement is not yet mesh-converged. Here, no network is trained. Future work will transfer this in silico model to real CT scans and test whether it improves predictive accuracy and reduces the training data required.

</details>


### [88] [VIALS: A Benchmark for Visual Interpretation of Artifacts in the Life Sciences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21357)
*Elaine Lau,Thanuka Udumulla,Lee Izhaki-Tavor,Francisco Guzmán,Nicholas Magazine,Jonas Mueller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VIALS is a benchmark for evaluating AI models on interpreting real-world scientific visual artifacts (gel blots, microscopy images, etc.) that scientists routinely use in life sciences workflows, where current vision-language models perform poorly despite humans finding the tasks straightforward.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current frontier vision-language models can describe natural images fluently but fail to interpret scientific visual artifacts that are central to life sciences workflows, limiting AI's utility in professional scientific settings where such interpretation is essential for decision-making.

Method: Created VIALS, a visual question-answering benchmark with 161 interpretation tasks spanning various visual artifacts used in biotech experimental workflows (gel blots, microscopy images, plasmid maps, flow cytometry plots, molecular structures), testing models on real-world scientific images rather than polished textbook figures.

Result: Vision-language models show poor performance on VIALS tasks, revealing limitations in domain-specific knowledge and visual reasoning capabilities for scientific artifacts, while scientists with relevant domain expertise find the same tasks straightforward.

Conclusion: AI systems must improve their ability to interpret scientific visual artifacts to be truly useful in professional life sciences workflows, where such images are central to scientific reasoning, communication, and decision-making processes.

Abstract: In professional life sciences workflows, scientists routinely interpret visual artifacts (gel blots, microscopy images, plasmid maps, flow cytometry plots, molecular structures, ...) to inform research decisions. We introduce VIALS, a visual question-answering benchmark with 161 such interpretation tasks, spanning the types of artifacts examined throughout experimental workflows in the biotech industry (rather than polished figures from publications and textbooks). While frontier vision-language models can now fluently describe natural images, we find that they are unable to accurately interpret these scientific images, reflecting limitations in domain knowledge and domain-specific visual reasoning capabilities. In contrast, scientists with relevant domain expertise find these visual interpretation tasks straightforward. AI that cannot similarly interpret such images will have limited utility in professional life sciences workflows, where such artifacts are central to how scientists reason, communicate, and make decisions.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [89] [Bankruptcy Prediction via Hybrid Resampling and Stacking Ensemble Techniques with Explainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI)-Driven Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20343)
*Obu-Amoah Ampomah,Edmund Fosu Agyemang,Kofi Acheampong,Louis Agyekum,Enock Adu Bonsu,Eric Nyarko*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A bankruptcy prediction framework combining consensus feature selection, hybrid resampling (SVM-SMOTE, SMOTE-Tomek, SMOTE-ENN), stacking ensembles (ML classifiers + DL models), and SHAP explainability achieves superior minority-class detection in imbalanced financial data, with GRU+SMOTE-ENN and stacking ensembles showing best performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The need for improved bankruptcy prediction systems that can effectively handle severely imbalanced financial datasets, where minority class (bankrupt firms) detection is crucial but challenging, and to provide both reliable predictions and interpretable results for early warning systems.

Method: 1) Consensus-based feature selection using 5 algorithms reduced 96 variables to 23 robust features. 2) Three hybrid resampling techniques (SVM-SMOTE, SMOTE-Tomek, SMOTE-ENN) balanced training data. 3) Comparison of 5 ensemble ML classifiers (GB, XGB, HGB, LightGBM, AdaBoost) vs 5 DL models (RNN, LSTM, GRU, DNN, MLP). 4) Hybrid stacking ensembles combining ML classifiers as base learners with DL models as meta-learners. 5) SHAP for feature importance explanation.

Result: Resampling strategy significantly influenced performance: SVM-SMOTE and SMOTE-Tomek favored accuracy/specificity, while SMOTE-ENN provided stronger minority-class detection. Best standalone model: GRU with SMOTE-ENN (recall: 0.8627, G-mean: 0.8517, ROC-AUC: 0.9431). Best stacking ensemble: SMOTE-ENN with (GB+XGB+HGB+LGBM+AB)+LSTM. SHAP identified leverage, profitability, solvency, and operational efficiency as key bankruptcy predictors.

Conclusion: The integrated framework combining consensus feature selection, hybrid resampling, stacking ensembles, and explainable AI provides superior bankruptcy prediction with enhanced minority-class detection and interpretability. GRU with SMOTE-ENN and certain stacking ensembles offer optimal performance balance, supporting the development of more reliable and explainable early warning systems for financial distress.

Abstract: This study develops and evaluates a bankruptcy prediction framework that integrates consensus-based feature selection, hybrid resampling, stacking ensembles, and explainable artificial intelligence to improve minority-class detection in severely imbalanced financial data. Using the Taiwanese Bankruptcy Prediction dataset from the UCI Machine Learning Repository, five feature-selection algorithms were first applied, and a consensus retention rule reduced the input space to 23 robust variables. The balanced training data were then generated using SVM-SMOTE, SMOTE-Tomek, and SMOTE-ENN. Five ensemble machine learning classifiers, namely gradient boosting, extreme gradient boosting, histogram-based gradient boosting, LightGBM, and AdaBoost, were compared with five deep learning models, including RNN, LSTM, GRU, DNN, and MLP. In addition, hybrid stacking ensembles combined the five machine learning classifiers as base learners with each deep learning model as a meta-learner. Model performance was assessed using accuracy, recall, specificity, G-mean, and ROC-AUC, while SHAP was used to explain feature contributions. The results show that resampling strategy materially shaped model behavior. SVM-SMOTE and SMOTE-Tomek favored accuracy and specificity, whereas SMOTE-ENN delivered stronger minority-class detection. Among standalone models, the GRU with SMOTE-ENN achieved the best overall predictive balance, with recall of 0.8627, G-mean of 0.8517, and ROC-AUC of 0.9431. Among stacking ensembles, SMOTE-ENN with (GB+XGB+HGB+LGBM+AB)+LSTM provided the strongest compromise between sensitivity and specificity. SHAP analysis identified leverage, profitability, solvency, and operational efficiency indicators as the most influential predictors of bankruptcy risk. These findings support more reliable and interpretable early warning systems for financially distressed firms.

</details>


### [90] [Machine Learning and ARIMA Model Averaging for Adaptive Public Health Forecasting: Comparative Evaluation and an Ontario COVID-19 Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20406)
*Yushu Zou,Ye Li,Johra Moosa,Martin Grunnill,Samir N. Patel,Venkata R. Duvvuri*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper compares ARIMA, random forest, and XGBoost for COVID-19 case forecasting in Ontario, finding that different models excel in different scenarios. The authors propose MLAMA, a performance-weighted ensemble that adapts weights by forecast horizon and achieves the lowest errors across most conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Public health forecasting needs to balance responsiveness to abrupt changes in surveillance data with robustness against noise, reporting artifacts, and temporary trends. There's a need for practical frameworks that can adapt to different operating conditions rather than relying on single universally preferred models.

Method: The study evaluated ARIMA, random forest, and XGBoost models using 190 weekly COVID-19 case observations from Ontario (Jan 2020-Oct 2023). Used rolling-origin time-series cross-validation to preserve temporal order. Performance was assessed across three dimensions: responsiveness after turning points, forecast horizons (1-6 weeks), and amount of historical training data. Developed MLAMA - a non-negative performance-weighted ensemble with weights varying by forecast horizon and responsiveness setting.

Result: ARIMA adapted rapidly after turning points but its normalized error increased at longer horizons. Random forest and XGBoost were less responsive initially but maintained more stable normalized error over longer horizons. For two-week forecasts, training on recent data outperformed longer historical periods, especially for XGBoost. MLAMA achieved the lowest normalized mean absolute percentage error across most forecast horizons and ranked among the best-performing methods across responsiveness settings.

Conclusion: Forecasting models should be selected according to operating conditions rather than relying on a single universally preferred approach. MLAMA provides a practical framework for combining complementary statistical and machine-learning forecasts. The approach supports adaptive model selection based on specific forecasting needs.

Abstract: Public health forecasts must respond to abrupt changes in surveillance data without over-extrapolating noise, reporting artifacts, or temporary trends. We evaluated autoregressive integrated moving average (ARIMA), random forest, and extreme gradient boosting (XGBoost) models using 190 weekly observations of publicly available Ontario COVID-19 case counts from January 2020 to October 2023. Rolling-origin time-series cross-validation preserved temporal order during model tuning and evaluation. Performance was assessed across three operating dimensions: responsiveness following selected turning points, forecast horizons of one to six weeks, and the amount of historical training data. We also developed Machine Learning and ARIMA Model Averaging (MLAMA), a non-negative performance-weighted ensemble with weights that vary by forecast horizon and responsiveness setting. Retrospective comparisons showed that ARIMA adapted rapidly after turning points but its normalized error increased at longer horizons. Random forest and XGBoost were less responsive initially but maintained more stable normalized error over longer horizons. For two-week forecasts at the end of the study period, training on the most recent data outperformed using longer historical periods, particularly for XGBoost. MLAMA achieved the lowest normalized mean absolute percentage error across most forecast horizons and ranked among the best-performing methods across responsiveness settings. These findings support selecting forecasting models according to operating conditions rather than relying on a single universally preferred approach. MLAMA provides a practical framework for combining complementary statistical and machine-learning forecasts. The accompanying Python package is currently maintained in a private repository while software validation and reproducibility testing are completed.

</details>


### [91] [From Thermal Preference Prediction to Adaptive Thermal Intervention: A Reinforcement Learning Approach Using Physiological and Environmental Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20423)
*Isibor Kennedy Ihianle,Emmanuel Manu,Ehsan Asnaashari,Mojgan Jadidi,Pedro Machado,Amrit Sagoo,Ahmad Lotfi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A two-stage personalized thermal comfort system using multimodal sensing and reinforcement learning to move beyond static HVAC setpoints and population-level models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional HVAC systems rely on static setpoints and population-level comfort models that fail to capture individual physiological variability, compromising occupant wellbeing and limiting building-control responsiveness.

Method: A two-stage approach integrating multimodal physiological and environmental sensing with reinforcement learning-based decision-making.

Result: The paper presents a novel framework that enables personalized thermal comfort by combining real-time physiological monitoring with adaptive machine learning control strategies.

Conclusion: The proposed approach addresses the limitations of traditional HVAC systems by enabling truly personalized thermal comfort through intelligent, adaptive control based on individual physiological responses.

Abstract: Personalised thermal comfort is essential for occupant wellbeing and for the development of more responsive building-control strategies, yet conventional Heating, Ventilation, and Air Conditioning (HVAC) systems rely on static setpoints and population-level comfort models that fail to capture individual physiological variability. This paper presents a two-stage personalised thermal comfort approach integrating multimodal physiological and environmental sensing with reinforcement learning-based decision-making.

</details>


### [92] [BF1: A Causal Dyadic Sparse-Attention Retrofit for Efficient Long-Context Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20427)
*Hina Dixit*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BF1 is a deterministic block-aligned dyadic sparse attention method that combines local neighborhood, global first block, and logarithmic historical blocks to reduce computational cost while maintaining performance in long-context language modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dense causal attention remains computationally expensive for long contexts despite optimization efforts, creating a need for more efficient attention mechanisms that can scale to long sequences while preserving model quality.

Method: BF1 uses a deterministic block-aligned dyadic sparse attention pattern with three components: small exact local neighborhood, global first block, and logarithmically spaced historical blocks. It employs O(n log n) token interactions and O(log n) communication depth, with a correctness-gated pretrained-model retrofit approach.

Result: BF1 achieves significant performance improvements: 10.91x per-layer prefill speedup at 32K tokens on NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000, reduces whole-model time-to-first-token by up to 15.3% at 32K context, and achieves best perplexity (1.68639) in matched training experiments compared to dense and other sparse methods.

Conclusion: BF1 establishes itself as a reproducible sparse attention operator and selective retrofit primitive with practical value for long-context systems, offering computational efficiency improvements while maintaining competitive language modeling performance through a systematic sparse attention approach.

Abstract: Dense causal attention remains expensive at long context even when implemented with highly optimized exact kernels. We study BF1, a deterministic block-aligned dyadic sparse-attention route that combines a small exact local neighborhood, a global first block, and logarithmically spaced historical blocks. The route is related to prior log-sparse and dilated attention patterns; our contribution is a correctness-gated pretrained-model retrofit, a matched topology-control study, and a systems characterization that connects per-layer sparsity to whole-model latency. For fixed block width, every converted layer uses O(n log n) selected token interactions and has O(log n) graph communication depth. On an NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell GPU, an optimized BF16 implementation crosses dense attention between 2K and 4K tokens and reaches a 10.91x per-layer prefill speedup at 32K. Retrofitting eight of 28 Qwen3-0.6B attention layers lowers warm whole-model time to first token by 7.7%, 11.3%, and 15.3% at 8K, 16K, and 32K, respectively, while the remaining dense layers keep the complete model asymptotically quadratic. Under a matched 1,000-step, 16.384M-token adaptation protocol, BF1 ranks first across three training seeds: mean report perplexity is 1.68639 versus 1.69154 for a matched static-random nonlocal graph, 1.69258 for dense continued training, and 1.81505 for equal-budget local sliding. At seed 1234, the packed-report paired interval places Dense-CT 0.3169-0.4055% above BF1 and static-random graph 17 0.2441-0.3642% above BF1. These results establish BF1 as a reproducible sparse operator and selective retrofit primitive with real long-context systems value. This paper evaluates numerical correctness, selected-interaction scaling, kernel performance, partial-model inference, and matched next-token language modeling.

</details>


### [93] [Approximate Homomorphisms and Convergent Representations in Transducers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20428)
*Santiago Cifuentes*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper studies stability of minimal transducer representations under perturbations, showing that with certain structural restrictions (finite-rank interfaces, mild indistinguishability conditions), canonical representations are robust, but without such restrictions, convergence fails.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivated by recent experiments finding predictive-state structure in neural network latent representations, the paper investigates whether minimal representations of controlled stochastic processes exhibit structural stability under perturbations.

Method: Introduces approximate homomorphism concepts and metrics for comparing transducer dynamics (interfaces). Analyzes three transducer types: standard, linear, and predictive transducers. Proves mathematical properties including composability of approximate homomorphisms.

Result: For standard transducers, simple interfaces exist with no approximate homomorphism between different implementations. For finite-rank interfaces, all minimal linear transducers implementing nearby interfaces have approximate homomorphisms with linear error bounds. Similar stability results proven for predictive transducers under residual metrics with mild belief state indistinguishability conditions.

Conclusion: Canonical transducer representations are robust to perturbations under specific structural restrictions, providing theoretical support for the hypothesis that neural network latent representations exhibit structural convergence when these types of abstractions are embedded.

Abstract: We study the stability of minimal representations of controlled stochastic processes (in particular, transducers) under perturbations. This question is motivated by recent experiments finding predictive-state structure in the latent representations of neural networks. We consider standard, linear and predictive transducers. We introduce notions of approximate homomorphism capturing local structural similarity between them, together with metrics comparing their induced dynamics (which we refer to as interfaces), and prove properties such as composability of the approximate homomorphisms. For standard transducers, we show that there exist simple interfaces for which there is no approximate homomorphism between the different implementations of the dynamics. In contrast, for every finite-rank interface $\mathcal I$, we prove that all minimal linear transducers implementing interfaces sufficiently close to $\mathcal I$ have an approximate homomorphism to the minimal implementation of $\mathcal I$, with error linear in the perturbation size. We prove an analogous stability result for predictive transducers under a residual metric using some mild hypothesis regarding the indistinguishability of the belief states. These results identify conditions under which canonical transducer representations are robust to perturbations, while showing that such convergence fails without additional structural restrictions. Under the assumption that these type of abstractions are embedded into the hidden layers of modern AI models, this gives some theoretical support to the hypothesis that their latent representations exhibit structural convergence.

</details>


### [94] [Wrong-Physics Backdoors in Neural PDE Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20439)
*Hanbing Liang,Fujun Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a novel backdoor attack called "wrong-physics backdoor" that manipulates neural PDE operators to produce physically plausible but incorrect solutions by exploiting parameter provenance failures in multi-parameter training archives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current validation methods for neural PDE operators rely on clean prediction error and parameter-agnostic plausibility checks, which are insufficient to detect attacks that produce physically plausible but wrong-parameter solutions. There's a need to address structural vulnerabilities in multi-parameter training archives.

Method: The authors introduce cross-parameter relinking, a data-poisoning technique that makes a triggered input select a valid solution from the same PDE family but under incorrect physical parameters. The attack exploits tensor-to-parameter provenance failures by stamping surrogate inputs and relinking their supervision to cached alternate-parameter solutions for the same latent sample.

Result: Across 476 attack campaigns on Burgers, advection-diffusion, 2D Navier-Stokes, and elliptic Poisson PDEs, the attack achieved high success rates (FNO reached 1.0000 backdoor success on advection-diffusion and 2D Navier-Stokes while maintaining low clean error). Results show high attack success alone is insufficient - successful attacks must move predictions toward intended alternate-physics targets while preserving bounded clean error.

Conclusion: The paper exposes a structural validation gap in neural PDE operators: smoothness or generic solver-like behavior is insufficient for security. The provenance of intended physical parameters must also be verified to prevent wrong-physics backdoor attacks that can bypass current validation methods.

Abstract: Neural PDE operators are increasingly trained on reusable solver archives, yet validation often relies on clean prediction error and parameter-agnostic plausibility checks. We introduce cross-parameter relinking, a data-poisoning primitive that makes a triggered input select a valid solution from the same PDE family under an incorrect physical parameter. We term this a wrong-physics backdoor: the output remains physically plausible but is wrong for the intended parameter. The attack exploits tensor-to-parameter provenance failures in multi-parameter archives by stamping the surrogate input and relinking its supervision to a cached alternate-parameter solution for the same latent sample. Across 476 attack campaigns, we evaluate Burgers, advection-diffusion, two-dimensional Navier-Stokes, and an elliptic Poisson case. Fourier Neural Operators and DeepONet provide the primary evidence, with Transformer, GRU, and LSTM models as support. FNO reaches a backdoor success rate of 1.0000 on both advection-diffusion and two-dimensional Navier-Stokes while retaining low clean relative L2 error. Clean-label, label-only, and shuffled controls show that high attack success alone is insufficient: successful attacks must move predictions toward the intended alternate-physics target while preserving bounded clean error. These results expose a structural validation gap: smoothness or generic solver-like behavior is insufficient unless the provenance of the intended physical parameter is also verified.

</details>


### [95] [Decision Tree and K-Means Analysis of Raman Spectra for Edible Oils: A Physics-Informed AI Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20440)
*Amrita Shaw,Chandrasekar S. N.,Sai Muthukumar V.,Jhinuk Gupta,Deepak L. N. Kallepalli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A Raman spectroscopy + machine learning framework achieves near-perfect edible oil authentication using only 4 spectral features (0.21% of data), with Physics-Informed AI overcoming food matrix interference for real-world processed food applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a critical need for accurate authentication of edible oils in processed foods to ensure food quality, prevent fraud, and meet regulatory compliance requirements, especially given the spectral interference from food matrices that complicates traditional analysis methods.

Method: The study developed an integrated Raman spectroscopy and machine learning framework combining t-SNE visualization, K-means clustering, Decision Trees, and Physics-Informed AI using Non-Negative Least Squares (NNLS)-based spectral decomposition. Five edible oils were analyzed both in pure form and within fried potato chips to evaluate both ideal and realistic food matrix conditions.

Result: For pure oils, Decision Trees achieved 100% classification accuracy using only 4 Raman variables from 1866 spectral features (0.21% data). For matrix-containing samples, Physics-Informed AI spectral decomposition improved classification to 86.4% accuracy (paper-subtracted) and 85.4% (paper+potato-subtracted), while reducing important variables to 5 and 4 respectively. The compact 4-feature representation achieved 99.44% data reduction without accuracy loss.

Conclusion: The research demonstrates that physically meaningful, compact, and interpretable spectral representations enable accurate Raman-based oil authentication even in complex food matrices, providing a foundation for Frugal AI, Edge AI, portable sensing, and embedded food-quality monitoring systems.

Abstract: Authentication of edible oils in processed foods is important for food quality, fraud prevention, and regulatory compliance. This study establishes an integrated Raman spectroscopy and machine-learning framework that links intrinsic spectral organization, interpretable classification, and Physics-Informed Artificial Intelligence (PI-AI). Five edible oils were investigated in pure form and within a fried-potato-chip matrix using t-SNE, K-means clustering, Decision Trees, and Non-Negative Least Squares (NNLS)-based spectral decomposition. Unsupervised analyses revealed substantially stronger class organization and separability in pure oils, whereas food-matrix effects introduced pronounced spectral overlap. Decision Trees achieved 100% classification accuracy for pure oils using only four Raman variables from the original 1866-feature spectral space. These four variables, consistently identified by both pre-pruned and post-pruned models, represented only approximately 0.21% of the available spectral information while retaining perfect test-set performance. For matrix-containing samples, NNLS-based PI-AI spectral decomposition substantially improved classification by separating oil-related signatures from paper and potato contributions. Optimized post-pruned models achieved accuracies of 86.4% and 85.4% for paper-subtracted and paper-plus-potato-subtracted datasets, respectively, while reducing the number of important Raman variables to only five and four. The compact four-feature representation further reduced the data footprint by 99.44% without loss of classification accuracy. Collectively, these findings demonstrate that accurate Raman-based oil identification can be achieved through physically meaningful, highly compact, and interpretable spectral representations, providing a promising foundation for Frugal AI, Edge AI, portable sensing, and embedded food-quality monitoring.

</details>


### [96] [Shared Physics Responses Recover Hidden Rankings in Neural Operator Libraries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20441)
*Hanbing Liang,Fujun Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Framework ranks neural operators using linearized physical response without ground truth, achieving >99% accuracy in selecting optimal models across diverse PDE applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need for reliable model selection when deploying neural operators without access to high-fidelity reference solutions for validation.

Method: Leverages low-dimensional span of candidate differences under squared Hilbert-space loss; uses single anchor-based linearized response of governing equations as shared physical diagnostic.

Result: Method recovered 99.6% of pairwise preferences and 99.0% of optimal checkpoints across Fourier/convolutional operators for fluid, reaction-diffusion, wave dynamics; physical proxy frequently outperformed best individual candidates.

Conclusion: Framework enables reliable, efficient deployment of scientific surrogates without ground truth by exploiting local dynamical response rather than raw defect magnitude, with rigorous certification for strongly monotone discretizations.

Abstract: Selecting the optimal neural-operator prediction during deployment is challenging when high-fidelity reference solutions are unavailable. We demonstrate that under a squared Hilbert-space loss, ranking a finite model library depends strictly on the low-dimensional span of candidate differences, allowing us to score all models simultaneously using a single anchor-based linearized response of the governing equation. This shared physical diagnostic accurately recovered over 99.6\% of pairwise preferences and 99.0\% of optimal checkpoints across diverse Fourier and convolutional operator libraries for fluid, reaction-diffusion, and wave dynamics. Furthermore, the corrected physical proxy frequently outperformed the best individual candidates, and we establish computable sufficient conditions that rigorously certify exact decisions for strongly monotone discretizations. By exploiting the local dynamical response rather than raw defect magnitude, this framework enables the reliable and highly efficient deployment of scientific surrogates without requiring ground-truth data.

</details>


### [97] [Stored in Optimizer State, Valued by Later Training: A Causal Account of Subliminal Trait Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20442)
*Qinyang Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Subliminal trait transfer occurs through optimizer state transporting source perturbations, with later training determining behavioral values, enabling traits to survive source removal and change signs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how subliminal trait transfer allows student models to acquire behavioral dispositions from teacher data where traits are not semantically expressed, particularly how these signals survive source removal or acquire different signs under later training.

Method: Treat parameters and optimizer moments as a single trainer state; derive exact transport-valuation identity; use state surgery to identify first moment as causal carrier; conduct transplantation experiments with Qwen, Llama, SmolLM2 models; apply full-horizon costate prediction.

Result: First moment serves as causal carrier for subliminal signals; transplanting first moment alone produces growing parameter differences while maintaining same outputs; full-horizon costate predicts all Qwen route-mean signs and Llama ordinary-route signs; mechanism replicates across different models and architectures.

Conclusion: Subliminal trait transfer operates through a two-stage mechanism: optimizer state transports the source perturbation, and later training determines its behavioral value, explaining how traits survive source removal and can acquire different signs.

Abstract: Subliminal trait transfer allows a student model to acquire behavioral dispositions from teacher-generated data in which the trait is not semantically expressed. Recent work explains how such signals enter gradients, but not how they survive source removal or acquire different signs under later training. We treat parameters and optimizer moments as a single trainer state and derive an exact transport-valuation identity separating observer-independent propagation of the source perturbation from the value assigned by a future continuation and behavioral readout. State surgery identifies the first moment as a causal carrier. Transplanting it alone leaves parameters, hidden states, and outputs unchanged at the cut, yet source-free updates generate growing parameter and hidden-state differences; transplanting parameters with the first moment recovers the terminal behavioral response. Sending the same source-induced difference through matched futures produces negative, near-zero, and positive Qwen effects (-0.658, +0.008, and +0.658 seed means). This ordering recurs in all 12 Llama-3.2-1B seeds after eight updates, while state-difference norms remain nearly equal across routes. Both contrasts grow in every paired seed when the continuation extends to sixteen updates. A full-horizon costate predicts all 42 Qwen route-mean signs and all 21 resolved Llama ordinary-route signs. Observer-independent transport also replicates across Qwen, SmolLM2, and Llama, while the complete-state recurrence predicts physical, hidden, and fixed-head responses in non-LoRA MNIST systems, including CNNs trained with AdamW and momentum SGD. Together, these results identify a two-stage mechanism for subliminal trait transfer: optimizer state transports the source perturbation, and later training determines its behavioral value.

</details>


### [98] [Amortized Bandwidth Learning for Kernel Density Estimation under Logarithmic Score](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20445)
*Junyi Liang,Hailiang Du*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Amortized kernel density bandwidth selector trained across density tasks outperforms classical methods, especially for small/heterogeneous samples, with strong generalization across unknown densities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional kernel density estimation bandwidth selection methods have limitations: they require analytical/asymptotic rules or optimization for each new sample. Performance depends critically on bandwidth choice, and existing methods don't generalize well across different density structures or sample sizes.
