Method: Proposes an amortized framework that learns the sample-to-bandwidth mapping across a distribution of density-estimation tasks by optimizing the logarithmic score. Uses truncated-and-renormalized bounded-support formulation for stable learning, affine standardization for transfer across intervals, and finite Gaussian mixtures as a generic training mechanism leveraging their L¹ approximation property.

Result: The amortized selector consistently and substantially outperforms classical methods (Silverman's rule, Sheather-Jones selector, least-squares cross-validation), with especially large gains in small and heterogeneous samples. It generalizes strongly across different density structures, allowing direct application to samples from unknown densities without specifying distributional families.

Conclusion: The amortized framework combines broad applicability with strong empirical performance, making it attractive for applications where finite samples or ensembles must be converted into continuous probability densities, as it eliminates the need for distributional assumptions while outperforming classical bandwidth selection methods.

Abstract: Kernel density estimation converts finite samples into probability densities, but its performance depends critically on bandwidth selection. Classical selectors prescribe the sample-to-bandwidth rule analytically or asymptotically, or solve a new optimization for each sample. An amortized framework is proposed that instead learns this mapping across a distribution of density-estimation tasks by optimizing the logarithmic score. A truncated-and-renormalized bounded-support formulation enables stable learning across heterogeneous tasks, while affine standardization allows a selector trained on a single reference interval to transfer across bounded intervals. Experiments under Gaussian sampling, a multi-family benchmark, and randomized Gaussian-mixture training show that the amortized selector consistently and substantially outperforms Silverman's rule, the Sheather--Jones selector, and least-squares cross-validation, with especially large gains in small and heterogeneous samples. Finite Gaussian mixtures provide a generic training mechanism supported by their $L^1$ approximation property. Selectors trained in this way generalize strongly across different density structures, allowing the same trained selector to be applied directly to finite samples from unknown densities without specifying or fitting a distributional family. This combination of broad applicability and strong empirical performance makes the framework attractive for a wide range of applications in which finite samples or ensembles must be converted into continuous probability densities.

</details>


### [99] [Mutual information and sensitivity analysis for feature selection in customer targeting: a comparative study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20447)
*Nestor Barraza,Sergio Moro,Marcelo Ferreyra,Adolfo de la Peña*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper compares mutual information vs. data-based sensitivity analysis for feature selection in bank telemarketing prediction, finding mutual information slightly better for cost optimization while sensitivity analysis achieves similar performance with fewer features.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Feature selection is crucial for knowledge discovery, and while many techniques exist (including traditional mutual information and newer data-based sensitivity analysis), there's a need to systematically compare their advantages and disadvantages in practical applications.

Method: Applied both feature selection techniques to a bank telemarketing dataset, then built logistic regression models on the feature sets identified by each method (13 features from mutual information, 9 from sensitivity analysis). Compared performance metrics, particularly focusing on false positives and prediction accuracy.

Result: Data-based sensitivity analysis performed better for lower false positive rates, while mutual information was slightly better for higher false positive ratios. Mutual information is preferable when trying to slightly reduce contact costs without losing too many successes, while sensitivity analysis achieved good prediction results with fewer features.

Conclusion: Mutual information, though not a recent technique, remains a valid and effective feature selection method, especially for cost optimization scenarios. Data-based sensitivity analysis also performs well with fewer features, offering a different trade-off profile that may be preferable depending on specific business objectives.

Abstract: Feature selection is a highly relevant task in a data-driven knowledge discovery project. Several techniques have been developed aiming at finding the features that influence most an outcome to predict, including mutual information and, in recent years, the data-based sensitivity analysis. The present research focus on analyzing the advantages and disadvantages of each of these two techniques, by applying both to a bank telemarketing case. Thereafter, a logistic regression model is built on the tuned set of features identified by each of the two techniques as the most influencing set of features on the success of a telemarketing contact, in a total of 13 features for mutual information and 9 features for the data-based sensitivity analysis. The latter performs better for lower values of false positives while the former is slightly better for a higher false positive ratio. Thus, mutual information becomes a better choice if bank managers intend to reduce slightly the cost of contacts without risking losing a high number of successes. Such results show that mutual information, although not recent, is still a valid method for feature selection. On the other side, the data-based sensitivity analysis selection achieved good prediction results with less features.

</details>


### [100] [When Clean Data Hurts: Learning with Monotone Corruptions Beyond Binary Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20480)
*Julian Asilis,Shaddin Dughmi,Chirag Pabbaraju*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Monotone adversarial corruptions dramatically degrade learnability: multiclass and partial binary concept classes become unlearnable with linear corruption, while binary classification suffers only logarithmic error increase.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper investigates the impact of monotone adversarial corruptions on learning beyond binary classification, extending recent work that showed optimal binary learners degrade from O(d/n) to Ω(d log(n/d)/n) error under such corruptions.

Method: Uses an adaptive adversary that can view the original i.i.d. training set S and insert b corrupted datapoints. Demonstrates impossibility results by constructing a multiclass problem with DS dimension 2 that becomes unlearnable, and analogous partial binary concept class results.

Result: 1) Multiclass learning becomes unlearnable with linear corruption (b = |S| = n). 2) Partial binary concept classes similarly become unlearnable. 3) Classes remain learnable when corruptions are o(n). 4) Classic multiclass error rate O(d_DS/n) achievable against restricted adversaries (constant budget, semi-adaptive, or oblivious).

Conclusion: Monotone adversaries are frighteningly powerful for multiclass and partial binary learning, making learnable problems unlearnable with linear corruption, unlike binary classification where they only increase error by logarithmic factors. The bound b = o(n) is tight for learnability.

Abstract: Optimal learners are tailored to exploit the i.i.d.\ data assumption underlying the classic PAC model. What if an i.i.d.\ training sample were corrupted with correctly labeled examples drawn from an otherwise unrelated, even adversarial source? This model of learning with monotone adversarial corruptions was recently introduced by Larsen et al. (2026), who demonstrated that all known optimal binary learners suffer increased error rates in this setting, from $O(d / n)$ in the PAC model to $Ω(d \log(n / d) / n)$ under monotone corruption. Mehrotra (2026) proved this logarithmic factor to be necessary for binary classification, but left open the consequences of corruption for more general learning settings, such as multiclass classification and partial binary concept classes.
  As our primary result, we demonstrate that monotone adversaries are frighteningly more powerful in each of these settings. We exhibit a learnable multiclass problem, of DS dimension only 2, that becomes altogether unlearnable under a monotone adversary, and show an analogous result for partial binary concept classes. These results are achieved by an adaptive adversary permitted to view the original i.i.d.\ training set $S$ and to insert $b < \infty$ corrupted datapoints into $S$. In the multiclass example, the adversary need only insert a linear number $b = |S| = n$ of datapoints.
  We complement these impossibility results by proving that every class remains learnable when the number of adaptive additions is $o(n)$, which our previous multiclass lower bound proves to be tight. We further observe that the classic multiclass error rate of $O(d_{\mathrm{DS}} / n)$ remains achievable against adaptive adversaries restricted to a known constant budget $b = O(1)$, against semi-adaptive adversaries viewing only a $p$-fraction of $S$ for $p \in (0, 1)$, and against oblivious adversaries that cannot view $S$.

</details>


### [101] [Metag: A dataset to build agentic meta-reviewing capabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20488)
*Anirudh Sundar,Min Chen,Divya Tadimeti,Gemma Zhang,Alice Li,Nigel Boachie Kumankumah,Pavan Uttej Ravva,Sadid Hasan,Somya Chatterjee,Pruthvi Prakash Navada,Xiao Wang,Yue Kang,Sulaiman Vesal,Larry Heck*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Metag dataset enables AI agents to help meta-reviewers track changes in scientific papers during peer review by linking reviewer concerns, author responses, and actual manuscript modifications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Growing conference submissions increase meta-reviewers' burden in synthesizing reviewer feedback, author rebuttals, and manuscript revisions, requiring tools to track whether and where authors addressed reviewer concerns.

Method: Collect manuscript versions pre-review and post-acceptance, compute document differences, and use human annotators to align these diffs with action items from OpenReview discussions, creating a dataset linking concerns to actual changes.

Result: Created Metag dataset with 349 high-quality action items tied to paper differences, enabling development of methods to help meta-reviewers identify if and where authors addressed reviewer statements.

Conclusion: Metag dataset provides foundation for AI meta-reviewing agents to increase transparency and traceability in peer review by automating change tracking between review rounds.

Abstract: AI tools increasingly support tasks across the scientific research cycle, from experiment design and manuscript preparation to peer review. At the same time, the continuing growth in conference submissions has increased the burden on meta-reviewers, who must synthesize reviewer feedback, author rebuttals, and manuscript revisions. To address this concern, this paper introduces Metag, a dataset to accelerate the development of meta-reviewing agents, specifically to identify changes made to scientific articles during the review-rebuttal process. Each instance contains a reviewer concern, the author's proposed resolution, and the manuscript diffs implementing the stated change. Metag is collected by obtaining manuscript versions from before the review deadline and after acceptance, computing differences between the two documents, and asking human annotators to align these differences with action items from OpenReview discussions. The resulting dataset consists of 349 high-quality action items tied to paper differences and will enable building methods to empower meta reviewers to quickly identify whether authors have addressed reviewer statements and where in the paper those changes have been made, resulting in additional transparency and traceability throughout peer review. The dataset is publicly available at https://github.com/microsoft/Metag-dataset.

</details>


### [102] [Bern2Edge: A Neurosymbolic Compiler for Edge Deployment via Bernstein Polynomial Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20497)
*Malak Gamal El-Din,Yifan Zhang,Yasser Shoukry,Sitao Huang,Salma Elmalaki*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Bern2Edge is an end-to-end framework that converts pretrained neural networks into hardware-efficient Bernstein polynomial activations, enabling both high-fidelity LUT-based deployment and interpretable rule-based inference for edge devices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deploying accurate neural networks on resource-constrained edge devices is challenging due to gaps between software training, compression, and hardware synthesis, with limited interpretability support.

Method: Uses knowledge distillation to convert pretrained teacher feed-forward networks into hardware-efficient Bernstein polynomial activations, creating two deployment paths: LUT-based realization preserving model fidelity, and symbolic rule-based representation derived from Bernstein activation geometry.

Result: BNNs achieve up to 2.12 pp accuracy improvement over ReLU under compression; system-level: up to 99.8% latency reduction and 95.2% BRAM reduction on Xilinx KV260 FPGA while maintaining <0.5 pp accuracy drop; rule-based path reduces DSP usage by 89.0% with 1.5 pp accuracy cost.

Conclusion: Bern2Edge provides an effective end-to-end framework for deploying neural networks on edge devices with both high efficiency and interpretability, bridging the gap between software training and hardware deployment.

Abstract: Deploying high-accuracy neural networks on resource-constrained edge devices remains challenging, as existing approaches treat training, compression, and hardware synthesis as separate stages, leaving a gap between software-trained models and efficient end-to-end deployment with limited support for interpretability. We propose Bern2Edge, an end-to-end framework that uses knowledge distillation to convert a pretrained teacher feed-forward network into hardware-efficient representations via Bernstein polynomial activations. This representation enables two deployment paths: (i) a high-fidelity LUT-based realization that preserves model fidelity under compression, and (ii) a symbolic rule-based representation derived from Bernstein activation geometry, enabling interpretable inference with explicit input-space constraints. The resulting BNNs achieve up to 2.12 percentage-point (pp) accuracy improvement over ReLU under identical compression constraints. At the system level, Bern2Edge achieves up to 99.8% latency reduction and 95.2% BRAM reduction relative to a W8A8 quantized teacher on an AMD Xilinx KV260 FPGA, while maintaining accuracy within 0.5 pp, and further deploys on a low-power Spartan-7 XC7S15 FPGA. The rule-based path reduces DSP usage by up to 89.0% at a cost of 1.5 pp in total accuracy.

</details>


### [103] [When Graph-JEPA Learns the Wrong Thing: Diagnosing and Repairing Category-Conditional Collapse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20516)
*Gollam Rabby,Sören Auer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Joint-embedding architectures can appear healthy on standard metrics (linear probing, effective rank) while actually containing zero usable instance information, and can saturate on targets that carry no structural information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to expose failures in current evaluation metrics for self-supervised learning methods, particularly showing that commonly used metrics like linear probing accuracy and effective rank can indicate good performance while the actual representations contain no usable information about instances.

Method: The authors use a Graph-JEPA model on a scientific-reasoning graph corpus with 57,903 articles (each a subgraph). They predict masked aspects from remaining aspects in subgraphs, analyze variance allocation between subgraph vs aspect identity, and repair configurations to test different objectives.

Result: The Graph-JEPA achieves 0.871 linear probe accuracy and effective rank of 18-47, yet retrieval recovers 0.00 of 14.4 bits (MRR nearly at chance). Three upper bounds recover nearly all information. The paper identifies a degenerate global minimum in the objective where variance is misallocated (0.39% on subgraph vs 99.61% on aspect identity). A repaired configuration reaches near-perfect bits but this target is reducible to node census.

Conclusion: Current evaluation metrics (rank, probes, etc.) can all saturate on uninformative targets, revealing fundamental issues with how we assess representation learning. The authors provide a harness with reducibility audit and target quality gates to prevent such failures.

Abstract: Joint-embedding predictive architectures are selected almost universally by linear probing and effective rank. We report a case where both read healthily while the representation carries zero usable instance information. We repair it, and a second failure appears: the repaired metric saturates on a target carrying no structural information. Our corpus is a scientific-reasoning graph over 57,903 articles, each a subgraph. A Graph-JEPA predicts one masked aspect from a subgraph's remaining aspects, attaining linear-probe accuracy 0.871 and effective rank 18-47, yet retrieval recovers 0.00 of 14.4 bits (MRR 1.9e-4 vs chance 1.99e-4, p=0.98). Three upper bounds on the same pool and code recover nearly everything (+14.28, +14.34, +14.22 bits), ruling out corpus, masking, pool, and metric as causes. We trace this to variance allocation - frozen inputs place 86.05% of variance on subgraph identity and 0.40% on aspect identity, while trained latents place 0.39% and 99.61%. This is a property of the objective's optimum: the degenerate solution is a global minimum of the coupled predictor/EMA-target objective, present already at init. A repaired configuration reaches 14.377 of 14.379 bits, above the 13.865-bit oracle; reverting the loss to regression drops it to 0.307 bits, confirming it. Yet the repair licenses nothing about reasoning: the target is reducible, since intra-subgraph edges are a deterministic function of node census. The oracle reaches 96.4% of the ceiling, and our largest effect is the learning-rate schedule, not architecture. Bits and a reasoning probe show no relation across ten cells. A data-derived target fails a quality gate - 25.96% of nodes are duplicate placeholders, and the rest is more generic than supporting evidence. Rank, probes, and metrics can all saturate on an unsupportive evaluation. We release a harness with a reducibility audit and target gate.

</details>


### [104] [Learning Exact NVIDIA SASS Encoders with $\mathbb{F}_2$ Linear Algebra](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20532)
*Jiading Gai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: F2Asm is the first system to learn NVIDIA SASS instruction encoders as vector-valued affine maps over F2 and the first open-source NVIDIA SASS assembler supporting Rubin SM107 GPUs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NVIDIA provides a SASS disassembler but no public assembler for recent data-center GPUs, limiting controlled machine-code rewriting capabilities.

Method: Learns exact 128-bit SASS encoders from paired disassembly and original CUBIN instruction words using Gaussian elimination over F2 to incrementally build compact bases, detect inconsistencies, and reject inputs outside learned span. Separates target-specific control bits, relocation rules, and metadata from learning algorithm.

Result: Successfully trained encoders for Hopper SM90/SM90a, Blackwell SM100, and Rubin SM107 using 3,225 CUBINs. In round-trip tests, reassembled disassembled SASS exactly matches original executables.

Conclusion: F2Asm provides a practical solution for NVIDIA SASS assembly on recent GPUs, enabling controlled machine-code rewriting through learned encoders that guarantee exact reassembly.

Abstract: NVIDIA provides a SASS disassembler but no public SASS assembler for recent data-center GPUs, limiting controlled machine-code rewriting. We present F2Asm, which learns exact 128-bit SASS encoders from paired disassembly and original CUBIN instruction words. To our knowledge, F2Asm is the first system to learn SASS instruction encoders as vector-valued affine maps over F2 and the first open-source NVIDIA SASS assembler to support Rubin SM107. F2Asm uses Gaussian elimination over F2 to incrementally build a compact basis, detect inconsistencies, and reject inputs outside the learned span. F2Asm separates target-specific control bits, relocation rules, and CUBIN metadata from its learning algorithm. We train encoders for Hopper SM90/SM90a, Blackwell SM100, and Rubin SM107 using 3,225 CUBINs from pinned NVIDIA and third-party production libraries, CUDA 13.3 packages, and CUDA 13.4 Developer Preview archives. In round-trip tests, F2Asm reassembles the disassembled SASS for each CUBIN, and all compared executable text sections match the originals exactly.

</details>


### [105] [AgentDecarbonizer: Carbon-Aware Execution for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20566)
*Leyi Yan,Shuangning Li,Sihang Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AgentDecarbonizer reduces AI agent carbon emissions by up to 57.9% using carbon-aware scheduling that shifts execution to lower-carbon periods or locations while handling execution time uncertainty and cache recomputation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents perform long-running workflows with repeated model calls that incur substantial carbon emissions, creating an environmental impact problem that needs addressing.

Method: Characterized emissions of OpenClaw agent workloads using WildClawBench, identified deadline flexibility as opportunity, and developed AgentDecarbonizer - a carbon optimizer that conservatively estimates task duration and selects deadline-feasible execution schedules while handling cache recomputation overhead.

Result: AgentDecarbonizer reduces carbon emissions by up to 57.9% compared to carbon-agnostic baseline and up to 37.5% compared to grid-optimizing baseline on WildClawBench with 60 agent tasks across four grids.

Conclusion: Carbon-aware execution of AI agent tasks through temporal and spatial shifting with proper handling of execution time uncertainty and cache recomputation can significantly reduce emissions while meeting deadlines.

Abstract: AI agents extend large language models from single prompt-response interactions to long-running, goaldirected workflows that issue many model calls, invoke tools, and interact with external environments. These workflows enable tasks such as software repair, data analysis, and experiment management, but their repeated model invocations can incur substantial carbon emissions. This paper characterizes the carbon emissions of OpenClaw agent workloads using WildClawBench, and shows that emissions depend on token consumption, context cache reuse, and the carbon intensity of the grid. Our characterization identifies deadline flexibility as an opportunity for carbon-aware execution: agent tasks can wait for lower-carbon-intensity periods or shift to lower-carbon grids. However, doing so requires handling uncertain execution time for temporal shifting and cached context recomputation during spatial shifting. We present AgentDecarbonizer, a carbon optimizer for AI agents that runs alongside OpenClaw. Given a task prompt and user-specified deadline, AgentDecarbonizer conservatively estimates task duration and selects deadline-feasible execution schedules, while accounting for cache recomputation overhead during spatial shifting. Evaluated on WildClawBench workloads with 60 agent tasks across four grids, AgentDecarbonizer reduces carbon emissions by up to 57.9 % compared with a carbon-agnostic baseline and by up to 37.5 % compared with a baseline that selects the carbon-optimal grid at task start time.

</details>


### [106] [Faults That Fortify: CNN Adversarial Robustness via GPU Undervolting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20572)
*Behnam Omidi,Ahmad Tahmasivand,Husam Alsyouri,Saba Al-Sayouri,Chongzhou Fang,Ihsen Alouani,Khaled N. Khasawneh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GPU undervolting during CNN training introduces stochastic perturbations that improve adversarial robustness while reducing energy consumption, providing a hardware-level defense without algorithmic changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CNNs face dual challenges: vulnerability to adversarial attacks and high training costs, especially problematic as learning shifts to energy-constrained edge devices. Adversarial training is effective but expensive.

Method: Characterize undervolting-induced faults at bit level, then train LeNet, VGG-6, and MobileNetV3 on MNIST and CIFAR-10 under both standard and adversarial training regimes at nominal and undervolted voltages. Evaluate all models against adversarial attacks.

Result: Undervolted models consistently achieve higher adversarial accuracy than nominal-voltage counterparts in both standard and adversarial training regimes. Dynamic power savings are substantial due to quadratic scaling with supply voltage.

Conclusion: GPU undervolting is a readily deployable hardware-level defense that simultaneously improves robustness and energy efficiency without requiring algorithmic changes, opening a promising direction where these two goals move together.

Abstract: Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) face a dual challenge: vulnerability to adversarial attacks and prohibitive training cost. Adversarial training is effective but expensive, a burden that grows as learning shifts to the energy-constrained edge. This paper addresses both through GPU undervolting during training. Reducing supply voltage introduces stochastic perturbations that act as implicit regularization, improving robustness while lowering power. We characterize undervolting-induced faults at the bit level, then train LeNet, VGG-6, and MobileNetV3 on MNIST and CIFAR-10 under two training regimes, standard and adversarial, each at nominal and undervolted voltage, and evaluate all models against adversarial attacks. In both regimes, the undervolted model consistently achieves higher adversarial accuracy than its nominal-voltage counterpart, showing that hardware-induced faults strengthen even adversarial training. Because dynamic power scales quadratically with supply voltage, these robustness gains arrive with substantial energy savings. GPU undervolting is therefore a readily deployable hardware-level defense requiring no algorithmic change, and opens a promising direction in which robustness and energy efficiency move together.

</details>


### [107] [Provable Edge-of-Stability for Adam on a One-Dimensional Quadratic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20638)
*Yiman Fong,Heng Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Adam optimizer exhibits edge-of-stability behavior even on simple quadratic functions, with dynamics that can converge to or break away from a stability threshold depending on parameter settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The edge-of-stability phenomenon in Adam optimizer is widely observed but not fully understood dynamically. The paper aims to isolate and study the optimizer-induced dynamics behind EoS using a simplified setting.

Method: Analyzes uncorrected Adam on a one-dimensional quadratic function, using constant curvature to isolate optimizer dynamics from loss geometry evolution. Characterizes dynamics across parameter space and proves restoring tendency toward stability threshold.

Result: Proves Adam exhibits restoring tendency toward frozen stability threshold 2(1+β₁)/[η(1-β₁)] in broad regimes. Identifies breakdown conditions: strictly subcritical periodic orbits and specially tuned trajectories that remain uniformly supercritical while converging to optimum.

Conclusion: Provides concrete dynamical explanation for Adam's edge-of-stability phenomenon in simplified setting, while also exposing limitations where edge-seeking mechanism breaks down.

Abstract: The edge-of-stability (EoS) phenomenon of Adam has been widely observed, while its underlying dynamical mechanism is not yet fully understood. We study uncorrected Adam on a one-dimensional quadratic, a clean setting where constant curvature isolates the optimizer-induced dynamics behind the EoS. We characterize the resulting dynamics across the parameter space. In broad regimes, we prove that Adam exhibits a restoring tendency toward its frozen stability threshold $2(1+β_1)/[η(1-β_1)]$. We also identify settings in which this edge-seeking mechanism breaks down, including strictly subcritical periodic orbits and specially tuned trajectories that converge to the optimum while remaining uniformly supercritical. These results give a concrete dynamical explanation for Adam's EoS in a setting free of evolving loss geometry, while also exposing its limitations.

</details>


### [108] [Meta-clustering of milk mid-infrared spectra identifies dairy cow groups associated with negative energy balance in early lactation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20653)
*T. Touil,E. R. Paquet*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Using FTIR milk spectra, researchers identified five distinct cow groups in early lactation linked to negative energy balance severity through spectral clustering approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clustering FTIR milk spectra can reveal latent cow groups associated with milk traits and health disorders, helping prevent conditions or monitor at-risk animals in early lactation.

Method: Used 407,632 milk MIR records from 3,408 farms, combining spectral filtering, dimensionality reduction (PCA and autoencoder), and clustering algorithms (k-means and spectral clustering) to create eight clustering approaches, then regrouped clusters into meta-clusters.

Result: Five distinct meta-clusters of early-lactation cows significantly associated with milk traits, representing a gradient of negative energy balance severity: severe, moderate, possibly mild, and two recovery states (rapid restoration and early recovery).

Conclusion: Multiple clustering approaches converged on the same five meta-clusters, with the classic PCA-based k-means using full spectrum being sufficient to identify clusters found by more sophisticated methods, suggesting practical applications for monitoring cow health.

Abstract: Clustering methods have been used to identify distinct groups of milk samples, cows, or herds. Fourier-transform infrared (FTIR) spectroscopy, particularly mid-infrared (MIR) spectroscopy, has been applied to individual cow milk samples to predict various milk traits. Applying clustering directly to MIR spectral data may reveal latent groups of cows associated with milk traits or health disorders and can help prevent these conditions or monitor at-risk animals. This study aimed to identify groups of individual dairy cows in early lactation directly from milk MIR spectra and to analyze their associations with milk traits. Using a dataset of 407,632 individual milk MIR records from 3,408 commercial farms, we combined (i) spectral filtering that selects informative wavenumbers, (ii) two dimensionality-reduction methods: principal component analysis (PCA) and an autoencoder, and (iii) two clustering algorithms: k-means and spectral clustering to yield eight different clustering approaches. We regrouped the assigned clusters into meta-clusters that encompassed the most similar ones identified by the eight approaches. Our results revealed five distinct meta-clusters of early-lactation individual dairy cows significantly associated with milk traits. Despite substantial differences, the eight approaches converged on the same five meta-clusters, and the classic, computationally efficient PCA-based k-means approach using the full spectrum recaptured clusters identified by more sophisticated, computationally intensive approaches. The five meta-clusters were strongly associated with DIM and appeared to reflect a gradient of negative energy balance (NEB) severity: severe, moderate, and possibly mild, while the remaining two likely represented cows recovering from NEB, one with rapid restoration of energy balance and one in early recovery.

</details>


### [109] [RiskTraf: Risk-Extrapolated Residual Learning for Multi-Variate Traffic Flow Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20656)
*Guangyu Wang,Zhidan Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RiskTraf is a model-agnostic framework that uses speed and occupancy data to improve traffic flow forecasting by learning residual corrections that mitigate regime-dependent shortcuts in traffic data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard traffic flow forecasting models rarely exploit all three raw measurements (flow, speed, occupancy) reliably. Existing datasets often omit or inconsistently record speed and occupancy, and direct empirical risk minimization may exploit shortcuts due to varying relationships between these variables in free-flow vs congested states.

Method: Proposes PEMSB-3V benchmark with raw flow, speed, occupancy measurements, and RiskTraf - a lightweight residual head that freezes a trained backbone and learns horizon-wise flow corrections from speed and occupancy data using risk extrapolation to mitigate regime-specific shortcut correlations.

Result: RiskTraf consistently improves diverse forecasting backbones and outperforms debiasing and distribution-shift adaptation methods in extensive experiments.

Conclusion: RiskTraf effectively leverages speed and occupancy data to improve traffic flow forecasting by mitigating regime-dependent shortcuts without modifying existing backbone models, with the PEMSB-3V benchmark providing proper multi-variable data for future research.

Abstract: Traffic sensors commonly record flow, speed, and occupancy, but standard traffic flow forecasting benchmarks and models rarely exploit all three raw measurements reliably. Although speed and occupancy provide sensor-native traffic-state information beyond flow alone, existing releases often omit these variables, replace them with proxies, or contain logically inconsistent records. Moreover, direct empirical risk minimization over three-variable inputs may exploit regime-dependent shortcuts, as the relationships among flow, speed, and occupancy vary substantially between free-flow and congested states. We introduce \textbf{PEMSB-3V}, a public benchmark suite that preserves raw flow, speed, and occupancy measurements from PeMS detectors for flow prediction. We also propose \textbf{RiskTraf}, a model-agnostic risk-extrapolated residual plug-in. For each trained spatio-temporal backbone, RiskTraf freezes the selected checkpoint and learns a lightweight zero-start residual head from historical speed and occupancy. The residual head constructs ordered traffic-risk environments and optimizes horizon-wise flow corrections with a risk extrapolation objective, thereby mitigating regime-specific shortcut correlations without modifying the backbone. Extensive experiments demonstrate that RiskTraf consistently improves diverse forecasting backbones and outperforms debiasing and distribution-shift adaptation methods. Our code and benchmark are available at https://github.com/Guangyu4/RiskTraf.

</details>


### [110] [C-Score: Beyond Accuracy for Robustness Assessment in Semi-Supervised Learning under Open-World Unlabeled Contamination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20667)
*Tsao-Lun Chen,Chi-Cheng Fu,Han-Yi E. Chou,Shun-Feng Su*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Pseudo-label SSL evaluation framework (C-Score) detects hidden learning collapse under OOD contamination in unlabeled data, revealing issues that clean test accuracy alone misses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world SSL faces open-world environments where unlabeled data contains OOD samples, causing hidden deterioration in learning dynamics that clean test accuracy fails to detect, creating unreliable robustness assessment.

Method: Proposes C-Score, a diagnostic evaluation framework with three complementary spaces: prediction space (PLE and CCI metrics for unlabeled prediction behavior), feature space (Sem-Drift for deviation from labeled semantic anchors), and optimization space (Grad-Align for labeled-unlabeled optimization compatibility).

Result: Experiments on CIFAR datasets with various OOD sources show C-Score metrics reveal hidden degradation: SVHN contamination causes CCI to rise 280% while accuracy stays within 3% of baseline; near-OOD sources cause up to 14.9% accuracy collapse. Clean accuracy alone is insufficient for evaluating SSL robustness.

Conclusion: Clean accuracy metrics are insufficient for assessing SSL robustness in open-world environments with OOD contamination. Internal diagnostic signals like C-Score are necessary for reliable robustness evaluation, revealing hidden learning deterioration that standard metrics miss.

Abstract: Pseudo-label-based semi-supervised learning has achieved strong performance due to its simplicity and scalability. However, it is typically developed under a closed-world assumption that unlabeled data are drawn from the same distribution as labeled data. In practical deployment, unlabeled data are often collected from open environments and may contain OOD samples. Under such contamination, OOD samples may still receive high-confidence predictions and be incorporated into training as if they were valid target examples. This creates an important evaluation problem: clean in-distribution test accuracy may appear stable even when the internal learning dynamics of SSL have already deteriorated. To address this issue, we study hidden collapse in pseudo-label-based SSL under open-world unlabeled contamination from a diagnostic evaluation perspective. We present C-Score, a compact framework that evaluates training behavior in three complementary spaces: prediction, feature representation, and optimization. C-Score includes PLE and CCI for unlabeled prediction behavior, Sem-Drift for deviation from labeled semantic anchors, and Grad-Align for the compatibility between labeled and unlabeled optimization. Experiments on CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 with multiple OOD sources, varying contamination ratios, and four pseudo-label-based SSL algorithms show that C-Score metrics reveal hidden degradation that clean accuracy alone fails to detect: under SVHN contamination, CCI rises over 280% while best-accuracy remains within 3% of the uncontaminated baseline; near-OOD sources (CIFAR-100, STL-10) cause up to 14.9% accuracy collapse (FlexMatch, r=0.5). The results suggest that clean accuracy alone is insufficient for evaluating SSL robustness in open-world environments, and that internal diagnostic signals are necessary for more reliable robustness assessment under unlabeled contamination.

</details>


### [111] [Lightweight Adaptive ReduNet via Hyperspherical Manifold Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20668)
*Zhenglin Huang,Qifa Yan,Bin Dai,Xiaohu Tang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LA-ReduNet is a lightweight adaptive neural network that achieves comparable classification accuracy to ReduNet while requiring dramatically fewer layers and parameter storage, using hyperspherical manifold learning and adaptive step sizes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The original ReduNet architecture, while mathematically interpretable, requires a large number of layers for the MCR² objective to stabilize, leading to high parameter storage requirements. This inefficiency motivates the development of a more compact architecture.

Method: LA-ReduNet refines ReduNet's layer-wise update rule by incorporating hyperspherical manifold learning and adaptive step sizes, enabling discriminative feature representation with substantially fewer unfolded layers while maintaining the MCR² principle.

Result: LA-ReduNet achieves comparable classification accuracy while requiring only about 1/29 of the parameter storage of the original ReduNet. It reduces the number of layers needed for MCR² objective stabilization by an order of magnitude.

Conclusion: LA-ReduNet successfully addresses the efficiency limitations of ReduNet while preserving its interpretable, white-box architecture, making it a more practical solution for discriminative feature learning with significantly reduced computational and storage requirements.

Abstract: In recent years, a white-box neural network called ReduNet has been proposed, which employs the maximal coding rate reduction (MCR$^2$) principle to transform raw data into low-dimensional discriminative features via a forward layer-wise construction process. Unlike traditional deep networks that rely on backpropagation, ReduNet explicitly derives the parameters of each layer from the features of its preceding layer, offering a mathematically interpretable paradigm. However, this layer-wise construction often requires a large number of layers for the MCR$^2$ objective to reach a stable value, which increases the parameter storage of the unfolded module. To address this issue, we propose LA-ReduNet, a lightweight adaptive architecture that refines the layer-wise update rule and enables discriminative feature representations to be obtained with substantially fewer unfolded layers. Specifically, LA-ReduNet employs hyperspherical manifold learning and adaptive step sizes, thereby reducing by an order of magnitude the number of layers required for the MCR$^2$ objective to reach a stable value. Simulation results demonstrate that, while maintaining comparable classification accuracy, LA-ReduNet requires significantly fewer layers for the MCR$^2$ objective to reach a stable value. Remarkably, under the considered experimental settings, LA-ReduNet requires only approximately $1/29$ of the parameter storage of the unfolded ReduNet module for the MCR$^2$ objective to reach a stable value.

</details>


### [112] [Reinforcement Learning for Continuous-Time Jump Markov Decision Processes with Applications to Network Dynamic Pricing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20680)
*Huiling Meng,Ningyuan Chen,Xuefeng Gao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Develops model-free q-learning algorithms for Continuous-Time Jump Markov Decision Processes with general discrete state spaces, overcoming limitations of existing continuous-time RL methods that only work with continuous Euclidean spaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing continuous-time RL techniques (like those in Jia and Zhou 2023) focus on continuous state spaces in ℝ^d and rely on semimartingale theory, making them inapplicable to CTJMDPs with general discrete state spaces that lack vector space structures. Applications like multi-product dynamic pricing with capacitated resources require handling such discrete state spaces.

Method: Formulates an entropy-regularized continuous-time control problem with stochastic policies to model exploration-exploitation tradeoff. Establishes theoretical foundations of q-learning for CTJMDPs and develops model-free q-learning algorithms, avoiding naïve time discretization approaches.

Result: Numerical experiments in network dynamic pricing show the proposed RL algorithm reliably learns near-optimal policies, consistently outperforms standard benchmark methods, demonstrates superior solution quality, and effectively scales to large-scale network instances.

Conclusion: The paper successfully bridges the gap in continuous-time RL by developing theoretically grounded q-learning methods for CTJMDPs with discrete state spaces, offering both conceptual and empirical advantages over discretization approaches, with practical applications in operations management problems like dynamic pricing.

Abstract: We study reinforcement learning (RL) in Continuous-Time Jump Markov Decision Processes (CTJMDPs) featuring general discrete state spaces (which need not possess a vector space structure) and continuous/discrete action spaces. The setup covers many well-known applications in operations such as multi-product dynamic pricing with capacitated resources (Gallego and van Ryzin 1997). To model the exploration-exploitation tradeoff, we formulate an entropy-regularized continuous-time control problem with stochastic policies. Recent continuous-time RL techniques such as $q$-learning for controlled diffusions in (Jia and Zhou 2023) focus on continuous state spaces $\mathbb{R}^d$ and rely heavily on semimartingale theory in $\mathbb{R}^d$ for their theoretical analysis. Consequently, their methods cannot be directly applied to CTJMDPs with general discrete state spaces, which may lack the algebraic addition and subtraction structures inherent to Euclidean spaces. To bridge this gap, we establish the theoretical foundations of $q$-learning for CTJMDPs and develop model-free $q$-learning algorithms. Compared to naïve time discretization and approximating CTJMDPs using discrete-time MDPs, our approach has several conceptual and empirical benefits. Numerical experiments in network dynamic pricing (Gallego and van Ryzin 1997) show that our proposed RL algorithm reliably learns near-optimal policies and consistently outperforms standard benchmark methods, demonstrating superior solution quality and effective scalability to large-scale network instances.

</details>


### [113] [Geometric Regularization for Long-Tailed Semi-Supervised Learning via Gaussian Feature Bridges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20710)
*Hongyang He,Xinyuan Song,Yan Zhong,Daizong Liu,Yanbin Li,Yang-fan He,Wenqiao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Gaussian Bridge Consistency (GBC) is a novel semi-supervised learning framework that addresses long-tailed label distributions and noisy pseudo-labels by creating semantic interpolation paths between unlabeled samples and high-quality class anchors, improving performance on tail classes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world semi-supervised learning faces challenges with long-tailed label distributions and noisy pseudo-labels, which hinder generalization and amplify confirmation bias. Existing methods struggle with these realistic settings where most classes have few labeled examples while head classes dominate.

Method: The GBC framework maintains a dynamic Prototype Atlas storing diverse labeled and pseudo-labeled exemplars per class. For each unlabeled instance, it creates class-conditional Gaussian Feature Bridges in latent space, allowing the student model to traverse from uncertain predictions to reliable class prototypes. A bridge consistency loss enforces alignment with geometrically interpolated target distributions. BridgeMix, a confidence-aware feature mixing strategy, interpolates both sample and anchor pairs to enhance cross-sample generalization.

Result: Extensive experiments on CIFAR10-LT and ImageNet-LT (USB benchmarks) demonstrate GBC's robustness and effectiveness under realistic long-tailed SSL settings. The method consistently improves long tail-class performance without sacrificing scalability.

Conclusion: Gaussian Bridge Consistency provides an effective solution to challenges in real-world semi-supervised learning by constructing semantic interpolation paths, mitigating issues with long-tailed distributions and noisy pseudo-labels while maintaining computational efficiency.

Abstract: Real-world semi-supervised learning (SSL) often encounters significant challenges with long-tailed label distributions and noisy pseudo-labels, which hinder generalization and amplify confirmation bias. In this work, we introduce a novel framework, Gaussian Bridge Consistency (GBC), to address these challenges by constructing semantic interpolation paths between unlabeled samples and high-quality class anchors. Our method maintains a dynamic Prototype Atlas that stores a diverse and evolving set of labeled and pseudo-labeled exemplars per class. For each unlabeled instance, GBC forms a class-conditional Gaussian Feature Bridge in the latent space, enabling the student model to traverse a smooth trajectory from uncertain predictions to reliable class prototypes. A bridge consistency loss is applied along this path to enforce alignment with a geometrically interpolated target distribution. Furthermore, we propose BridgeMix, a confidence-aware feature mixing strategy that interpolates both sample and anchor pairs to amplify cross-sample generalization. Extensive experiments on CIFAR10-LT and ImageNet-LT (USB benchmarks) validate the robustness and effectiveness of GBC under realistic long-tailed SSL settings, consistently improving long tail-class performance without sacrificing scalability.

</details>


### [114] [Hidden Axis of Uncertainty: Latent-Posterior Alignment in Graph Neural Networks with Bayesian Output Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20758)
*Suk Hoon Choi,Damdae Park,Junhyuk Choi,Hyein Jung,Changsoo Kim,Ung Lee,Kyeongsu Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GNNs with Bayesian output layers show predictive uncertainty reduction through latent representations shifting toward lower-variance posterior directions (Latent-Posterior Alignment), not posterior contraction; new training method (AGL) explicitly promotes this alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite Bayesian Neural Networks with Bayesian output layers providing principled uncertainty quantification, the mechanisms behind uncertainty reduction remain unclear. Conventional theory attributes uncertainty reduction to posterior contraction, but these assumptions may not hold for deep models like GNNs.

Method: The authors study Graph Neural Networks with Bayesian output layers, observe Latent-Posterior Alignment (LPA) phenomenon, conduct interventional experiments to validate its functional role, and propose Alignment-Guided Learning (AGL) that explicitly promotes this alignment during training.

Result: AGL effectively reduces predictive uncertainty while preserving accuracy and improves structural calibration, ensuring model confidence faithfully mirrors underlying data density. Experimental results support LPA's functional role in shaping predictive uncertainty.

Conclusion: The findings provide a new perspective on uncertainty dynamics in GNNs with mean-field Bayesian output layers, shifting focus from posterior magnitude to the geometric interplay between latent and parameter spaces, challenging conventional assumptions about uncertainty reduction mechanisms.

Abstract: Bayesian Neural Networks (BNNs) with Bayesian output layers provide a principled and tractable framework for quantifying predictive uncertainty, yet the mechanisms shaping that uncertainty remain unclear. While conventional theory attributes uncertainty reduction to posterior contraction, the corresponding assumptions need not hold for deep models. In the Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) with Bayesian output layers studied here, we observe that predictive uncertainty decreases as latent representations shift toward lower-variance posterior directions, even though the posterior variance does not contract. We term this behavior Latent-Posterior Alignment (LPA) and conduct interventional experiments that support its functional role in shaping predictive uncertainty. Building on this insight, we propose Alignment-Guided Learning (AGL), which explicitly promotes this alignment during training. AGL effectively reduces predictive uncertainty while preserving accuracy and improves structural calibration, ensuring that the model confidence faithfully mirrors underlying data density. These findings provide a new perspective on uncertainty dynamics in GNNs with mean-field Bayesian output layers, shifting the focus from the magnitude of the posterior to the geometric interplay between latent and parameter spaces.

</details>


### [115] [Fuzzy-MoE: Interpretable Regime-Conditioned Expert Routing for Non-Stationary Multivariate Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20761)
*Lan Guo,Jie Xiao,Zhao Su,Jun Shen,Haoran Li,Weixia Ma,Qingguo Zhou,Binbin Yong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Fuzzy-MoE: A fuzzy logic-based Mixture-of-Experts model for multivariate time series forecasting that uses interpretable fuzzy rules for expert routing to capture heterogeneous temporal dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing deep forecasting models compress heterogeneous temporal dynamics into unified end-to-end mappings, leading to suboptimal modeling of time-varying dynamics and limited interpretability regarding which forecasting mechanisms are activated under different latent states.

Method: Propose Fuzzy-MoE framework with multiple parallel expert mapping networks and a dual-view fuzzy router. The router jointly exploits local convolutional dynamics and global segmented statistics to infer latent temporal states, computes expert activation strengths through learnable Gaussian membership functions, enabling explicit IF-THEN rule-based expert selection.

Result: Significantly outperforms mainstream forecasting methods on multiple public time series benchmark datasets in forecasting accuracy. Provides interpretable routing diagnostics through fuzzy memberships and rule activations.

Conclusion: Fuzzy-MoE effectively captures heterogeneous temporal dynamics while improving model interpretability through transparent, traceable fuzzy rule-based routing, unlike traditional MoE models with black-box routing.

Abstract: In non-stationary multivariate time series, different variables and samples often exhibit heterogeneous latent dynamic states, while existing deep forecasting models usually compress them into a unified end-to-end mapping, leading to suboptimal modeling of time-varying dynamics and limited interpretability regarding which forecasting mechanism is activated under different latent states. To overcome these limitations, we reformulate time series forecasting as a unified framework of latent temporal state identification and interpretable expert routing, and propose Fuzzy-MoE, a fuzzy logic-based dynamic Mixture-of-Experts model. Fuzzy-MoE consists of multiple parallel expert mapping networks and a dual-view fuzzy router. By jointly exploiting local convolutional dynamics and global segmented statistics, the router infers latent temporal states and computes expert activation strengths through learnable Gaussian membership functions, enabling explicit IF-THEN rule-based expert selection. This fine-grained routing strategy allows different variables within the same sequence to activate different experts, effectively capturing heterogeneous temporal dynamics while improving model interpretability. Experimental results on multiple public time series benchmark datasets show that Fuzzy-MoE significantly outperforms mainstream forecasting methods in forecasting accuracy. Moreover, fuzzy memberships and rule activations provide interpretable routing diagnostics, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed framework in both forecasting performance and mechanism transparency. Unlike traditional MoE models that use black-box routing, Fuzzy-MoE`s routing is based on clear, interpretable fuzzy rules. This makes the expert selection transparent and traceable.

</details>


### [116] [Resolution-Consistent Greedy Neural Approximation on Infinite-Dimensional Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20812)
*Pablo M. Berná,Antonio Falcó,Diego Mondéjar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Constructive approximation and learning guarantees for shallow neural networks with infinite-dimensional inputs observed through finite coordinates, featuring dimension-free statistical guarantees but nonconvex computational challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the challenge of neural network approximation and learning when dealing with infinite-dimensional input spaces, which are only observed through finitely many coordinates. This is motivated by practical scenarios where inputs are high-dimensional or functional in nature, and the recent infinite-dimensional universal approximation results for neural networks provide theoretical context for this work.

Method: The approach uses a parameter-normalized neural dictionary and associated weighted variation class. Key methodological elements include: 1) Separating approximation error into distribution-dependent coordinate-truncation term and greedy finite-width term; 2) A fully-corrective greedy procedure for empirical regression; 3) Framework that extends to Hilbert-valued responses without explicit output dimension dependence; 4) Application of quasi-Polish construction from recent infinite-dimensional approximation theory.

Result: The paper establishes: 1) Population guarantees for regression with statistical complexity uniform in retained input resolution; 2) Dimension-free statistical statements that don't depend explicitly on output dimensions for Hilbert-valued responses; 3) Clear separation between approximation error components; 4) Validation through synthetic experiments showing predicted resolution, width, and sample-size regimes.

Conclusion: The framework provides constructive approximation and learning guarantees for shallow neural models with infinite-dimensional inputs, achieving dimension-free statistical complexity while maintaining theoretical rigor. However, the approach faces computational challenges as selecting new neurons requires solving nonconvex parameter-search problems, highlighting that the dimension-free benefits are statistical rather than computational in nature.

Abstract: We develop constructive approximation and learning guarantees for shallow neural models with infinite-dimensional inputs observed through finitely many coordinates. The analysis is based on a parameter-normalized neural dictionary and its associated weighted variation class. Within this class, the approximation error separates into a distribution-dependent coordinate-truncation term and a greedy finite-width term. For empirical regression, a fully-corrective greedy procedure yields population guarantees whose statistical complexity is uniform in the retained input resolution. The same framework extends to Hilbert-valued responses without an explicit dependence on the output dimension. The dimension-free statements are statistical, not computational: selecting a new neuron still requires solving a nonconvex parameter-search problem. The quasi-Polish construction underlying recent infinite-dimensional universal approximation results provides a motivating example, and synthetic experiments illustrate the predicted resolution, width, and sample-size regimes.

</details>


### [117] [Scaling Muon for Diffusion Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20818)
*Chenghao Li,Xiao Han,Xinxin Huang,Wei Liu,Boyang Li,Bing Xiao,Heran Zhang,Juanma Perez Rua,Ke Xu,Kangning Liu,Linjun Kuang,Na Li,Tan Wang,Tian Xie,Wei Peng,Yang Pei,Yifan Xu,Yuanhao Zhai,Yuwei Lin,Zhe Wang,Zihao He,Daniel Li,Junbiao Tang,Ziyang Jiang,Dake Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Periodic Row-wise Muon reduces optimizer overhead in large DiT training while preserving generative quality advantages over AdamW


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While Muon optimizer shows advantages for large models, its computational overhead (Newton-Schulz iterations and full-momentum materialization) offsets step-efficiency benefits at scale, requiring optimization for end-to-end efficiency.

Method: Introduces Periodic Row-wise Muon: performs full spectral updates every K steps, uses low-cost row-wise constrained updates at other steps, plus distributed implementation with sharded momentum and communication-computation overlap.

Result: Reduces optimizer time by 46.9-54.3%, end-to-end step time by 15.7-24.3%, and communication by 66.7%, while maintaining or improving generative quality by 4.5% at 9B, with 33.7-64.8% less active training time.

Conclusion: Periodic Row-wise Muon preserves Muon's generative quality advantages while achieving practical end-to-end training efficiency for large Diffusion Transformers.

Abstract: The matrix-aware optimizer Muon improves large model training by balancing updates across singular directions, yet its scaling behavior and end-to-end efficiency on large Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) remain unclear. We first establish Muon's scaling behavior on DiTs from 1.3B to 15B parameters, showing that its optimization and generative quality advantages over AdamW persist across model scales. However, at scale, the 5-step Newton--Schulz iteration (NS5) performed at every optimization step, together with full-momentum materialization, introduces substantial computation and communication overhead that can offset Muon's step-efficiency advantage. We introduce \emph{Periodic Row-wise Muon}, which performs a full NS5 spectral update once every \(K\) steps and applies a low compute and communication cost row-wise constrained update based on the current momentum at the remaining steps. We further co-design a distributed implementation that operates directly on sharded momentum during non-refresh steps and accelerates spectral refreshes through bucketed all-gather and communication--computation overlap. Across all scales, Muon improves the best observed generative quality over AdamW by 12.9--19.1\%. Compared with vanilla Muon, Periodic Row-wise Muon remains within 0.5\% in best generative quality on the 1.3B--4B models and improves it by 4.5\% at 9B. It reduces optimizer time by 46.9--54.3\%, end-to-end step time by 15.7--24.3\%, and logical communication volume by 66.7\%, while reaching its respective best generative quality with 33.7--64.8\% less active training time. These results show that Periodic Row-wise Muon preserves Muon's generative quality advantage while translating it into end-to-end training efficiency for large DiTs.

</details>


### [118] [Nothing Changed but the Model: CellFill -- Bounded In-Cell Learning for Bit-Identical, Revocable Updates to Quantized LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20873)
*Zifeng Liu,Zhiyong Du,Yaxin Lu,Yiming Mao,Zhenhe Wang,Wenqi Shi,Zhengkun Jing*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A method called CellFill enables updating 4-bit quantized language models by learning only within the dequantization gap, preserving the original checkpoint bit-for-bit while allowing exact revocation and bounded drift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional model update methods (fine-tuning, adapter merging, model editing) replace the original checkpoint, invalidating all evaluations and caches that referred to those exact bits. The paper seeks a method that preserves the original artifact while enabling updates.

Method: Learns within the dequantization gap of 4-bit quantized models by writing new knowledge only into the per-weight residual that lives strictly inside each quantization decision cell. With integer codes and scales frozen, updates are constrained to the residual space. Re-quantization returns the original artifact bit-for-bit. Three training paths including CellFill enforce invariance structurally rather than through constraints.

Result: Constrained training matches unconstrained reference on fact recall (58.9% vs 59.3%), with minimal performance loss. CellFill reduces cross-domain forgetting compared to serving updates as unmerged adapters. Method scales to 27B hybrid linear-attention model (2.4e10 constrained weights) with verified bit-identical preservation. At larger scale, matched recall costs half as much cross-domain perplexity as at 1.7B.

Conclusion: Exact invariance is nearly free in practice, and learning within quantization cells acts as a trust region that bounds drift and enables bit-for-bit reproducibility. The method provides machine-checkable guarantees, exact revocability, and bounded drift while maintaining performance.

Abstract: Every way of teaching a deployed language model something new -- full fine-tuning, adapter merging, model editing -- replaces the released checkpoint, and with it every evaluation and cache that referred to those exact bits. We instead learn inside the dequantization gap: with the integer codes and scales of a 4-bit release frozen, new knowledge is written only into the per-weight residual that lives strictly inside each quantization decision cell. Re-quantization then returns the released artifact bit-for-bit, a machine-checkable guarantee; updates are exactly revocable by dropping the residual; and drift is bounded. We give six propositions and three training paths, including CellFill, a bounded reparameterization that makes invariance structural rather than enforced.
  Exact invariance turns out to be nearly free: across three paired seeds the constrained dense path matches an unconstrained reference whose weights provably escape the artifact (58.9 vs 59.3 percent fact recall; paired difference -0.5 points, 95% CI [-5.0,+4.0]), and is better on held-out cross-domain perplexity. Against the natural null hypothesis -- serving the same update as an unmerged adapter -- projecting into the cells reduces cross-domain forgetting in every run that converged, and a diverged control shows the boundary: projection is a trust region, not a repair. What no method escapes is the cost of knowledge itself, and the apparent free lunch of in-domain perplexity improving past the anchor is an artifact of rehearsal sharing a corpus with the metric. Methods differ threefold at matched rehearsal in knowledge bought per point of cross-domain perplexity, a ranking that is not the recall ranking. The method transfers to a 27B hybrid linear-attention model (2.4e10 constrained weights, verified bit-identical), where matched recall costs about half as much cross-domain perplexity as at 1.7B.

</details>


### [119] [Decoupling Policy Extraction for Offline Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20909)
*Xuyao Lin,Yixiang Shan,Jinru Duan,Tao Yang,Xinyu Zhao,Runyu Lei,Yiming Zhao,Jiaxin Fan,Zongbao Feng,Peng Jia*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes decoupling policy improvement from actor training in offline RL, where the actor models behavior distribution and the critic reranks actor-generated actions at inference time, avoiding coupled learning issues.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional coupled actor-critic training in offline RL creates problems: 1) Actor can drift toward out-of-distribution actions and amplify critic overestimation, 2) Conservative methods create trade-offs between suppressing OOD actions and selecting high-value actions. Since offline RL has fixed training data, actor improvement doesn't generate new data to validate/correct critics.

Method: Decouple policy improvement from actor training. Actor is trained solely to model behavior distribution (behavior cloning). At inference time, generate multiple action proposals from actor, then use a separately learned critic to rerank/select the best action. This creates a "decoupled policy extraction paradigm" where actor provides in-distribution candidates and critic performs value-based selection.

Result: Extensive experiments show the decoupled paradigm outperforms both behavior cloning methods and jointly learned offline RL approaches. The method remains effective even with a naive Q-learning critic.

Conclusion: The decoupled policy extraction paradigm addresses fundamental limitations of coupled actor-critic training in offline RL, providing better performance by separating behavior modeling (actor) from value optimization (critic), leveraging each component's strengths without their combined weaknesses.

Abstract: Offline RL methods commonly jointly train the actor and critic, where the critic is used to guide the actor toward higher-value actions. This coupled learning process is well motivated in online RL, where an improved actor collects new data that can further update the actor and the critic. However, training data remains fixed in offline RL, making actor-side policy improvement unable to generate new data to validate or correct the critic. Moreover, retaining this coupled paradigm leads to two related challenges. Firstly, actor updates can drift toward high-valued but potentially out-of-distribution (OOD) actions and amplify critic overestimation. Secondly, conservative value estimation or behavior-cloning regularization creates a difficult trade-off between suppressing OOD actions and selecting high-value actions within the data-supported region. Motivated by this observation, we revisit the conventional offline RL paradigm and propose decoupling policy improvement from actor training. Specifically, we train the actor solely to model the behavior distribution and perform policy improvement at inference time by reranking multiple actor-generated proposals with a separately learned critic. We refer to this paradigm as the decoupled policy extraction paradigm. Under such paradigm, the actor provides behavior-supported action candidates, while the critic performs value-based selection within this candidate set. Extensive experiments show that the decoupled policy extraction paradigm outperforms both behavior cloning and jointly learned offline RL methods, while remaining effective even with a naive Q-learning critic.

</details>


### [120] [Training, learning and inference: unified dynamics of neural systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20965)
*Mian Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces Generation-Fact Graphs (GFG) to record AI training histories, establishes a unified theory of training-learning dynamics, and demonstrates prediction of training outcomes with high accuracy while showing these principles extend beyond nanoGPT to other architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to provide a formal, compilable substrate for recording AI generation histories and establish a unified theoretical framework that connects training dynamics with learning outcomes, addressing the need to systematically understand and predict how AI models learn and perform inference.

Method: 1. Define atomic generation facts with origin, transformation, occurrence, result and relation roles.
2. Compile these into Generation-Fact Graphs (GFG) as an AI-native scientific fact substrate.
3. Establish GFG-based recursive scientific process with analysis, intervention, replay and validation cycles.
4. Using nanoGPT, model training as parameter-optimizer system evolution with receiving states and target-specific update geometry.
5. Model learning as persistent reorganization of distributed functional support by training responses.
6. Develop second-order predictor using target-boundary state, update geometry, and parameter-Adam receiving state coordinates.
7. Extend to inference as frozen projection of training-learning dynamics with component gating and rollback experiments.
8. Validate on ResNet/CIFAR-100 and diffusion/CIFAR-10 beyond nanoGPT.

Result: The second-order predictor achieved 91.43% accuracy and 91.49% macro-averaged recall across four transitions on held-out runs. Experiments showed causal recruitment and non-additive combination of query-conditioned support formed during training. ResNet/CIFAR-100 and diffusion/CIFAR-10 experiments confirmed receiving-state-conditioned responses, persistent support reorganization, and frozen inference projection principles extend beyond nanoGPT.

Conclusion: The paper successfully establishes GFG as a compilable scientific fact substrate for AI generation histories and provides a unified theory connecting training dynamics to learning outcomes. The framework enables prediction of training transitions before post-update outputs are read and shows how inference emerges as a frozen projection of training-learning dynamics across different architectures.

Abstract: We define an atomic generation fact f=(u,tau,omega,z;rho), recording the origin, realized transformation, concrete occurrence, generated result and relation role. Compiled into a Generation-Fact Graph (GFG), these facts provide an AI-native, compilable scientific fact substrate preserving generation histories. We establish a GFG-based recursive scientific process in which analysis, intervention, replay and validation form facts for later cycles. Using nanoGPT, we establish unified training-learning dynamics. Training is the evolution of a parameter-optimizer system with state and memory: each actual training action enters the receiving state and produces a finite-amplitude nonlinear functional response conditioned by that state and target-specific update geometry. Learning is the persistent reorganization of distributed functional support by these responses; capability formation, maintenance, decline or recovery becomes observable when target-specific states are evaluated against their readout boundaries. Three primary coordinates - target-boundary state, target-specific update geometry and parameter-Adam receiving state - yield a second-order predictor operating before post-update outputs are read. On held-out runs, it achieved 91.43% accuracy and 91.49% macro-averaged recall across four transitions. We further establish inference as a frozen projection of training-learning dynamics. Component gating and rollback show causal recruitment and non-additive combination of query-conditioned support formed during training, deriving organizational conditions realized by Attention. Controlled feedback indicates possible double-edged reinforcement effects. ResNet/CIFAR-100 and diffusion/CIFAR-10 experiments confirm receiving-state-conditioned responses, persistent support reorganization and frozen inference projection beyond nanoGPT.

</details>


### [121] [A Critical Audit of Spatiotemporal Forecasting Benchmark Datasets and Baselines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20980)
*Kenneth Martin,Simon Heilig,Asja Fischer,Michel F. C. Haddad,Adam M. Sykulski,Moshe Eliasof*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GNNs for time series forecasting are evaluated on limited benchmarks where simple linear models remain competitive, questioning dataset reliability and advocating for more rigorous statistical evaluation methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graph neural networks (GNNs) are widely used for short-range forecasting on spatial-temporal data, but they're primarily evaluated on a limited set of benchmark datasets (Chickenpox, PedalMe, WikiMaths, METR-LA, PEMS-BAY). The motivation is to understand why simple linear models often compete effectively with GNNs on these datasets, questioning whether these benchmarks provide reliable discriminative evaluation.

Method: The authors conduct a statistical analysis of benchmark datasets using classical time series methods. They develop a toolset to identify significant spatial and temporal correlations, and analyze structural biases introduced by first-order differenced datasets. They also apply their findings to a simple hybrid model to demonstrate practical applications.

Result: The analysis reveals that spatially-unaware linear models are stronger competitors than previously reported, uncovering structural biases in benchmark datasets and casting doubt on their discriminative reliability. The statistical framework helps identify when and why simple models perform well, revealing limitations in current evaluation practices.

Conclusion: The paper recommends reducing over-reliance on limited benchmark datasets for GNN method comparison and advocates for more rigorous statistical evaluation. The analysis provides a methodology that can lead to novel ways of developing GNN models by better understanding dataset characteristics and appropriate modeling approaches.

Abstract: Graph neural networks (GNNs) are routinely employed for short-range forecasting on multivariate time series with a spatial graph structure. Despite the availability of many alternative datasets, method innovations within this domain are predominantly assessed against a rather limited set of benchmark datasets, most notably Chickenpox, PedalMe, WikiMaths, METR-LA, and PEMS-BAY. The evaluation protocols contain baselines spanning from historical averages to classical machine learning approaches. These baselines often show competitive performance compared to GNNs. In the present work, we take a step back and analyse the benchmark datasets via classical time series methods to uncover why spatially-unaware linear models pose a stronger competitor than previously reported, casting further doubt on the discriminative reliability of the aforementioned widely adopted datasets. Our statistical analysis provides a toolset for identifying significant spatial and temporal correlations, while revealing a structural bias introduced by first-order differenced datasets. We therefore recommend reducing the over-reliance on such datasets for method comparison, and instead advocate for more rigorous statistical evaluation. By applying the results of our analysis to a simple hybrid model, we show how our methodology can lead to novel ways of developing GNN models

</details>


### [122] [Jacobian-guided Noise Injection for Quantization Robustness in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20988)
*Deepanshu Pandey,Arnav Chavan,Nahush Lele,Sankalp Dayal,Deepak Gupta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposed Jacobian-Guided Noise Injection training strategy to improve LLM quantization stability by addressing softmax sensitivity to quantization errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large Language Models (LLMs) quantization faces challenges due to the sensitivity of the self-attention mechanism's softmax operator to discretization errors, particularly its sensitivity to outliers and state-dependent Jacobian.

Method: Proposed Jacobian-Guided Noise Injection: training strategy injecting zero-mean Gaussian noise into pre-attention logits, with variance derived from the Jacobian Frobenius norm, identifying optimal noise variance based on local attention sensitivity.

Result: Method demonstrates improved robustness over popular PTQ methods, achieving up to +37% relative gains on Top-1 accuracy on ImageNet-1K for SigLIP and up to 40% improvement in relative perplexity on WikiText for LLMs in low-bit quantization settings.

Conclusion: The theoretically-grounded approach of suppressing Jacobian norm through noise injection effectively bounds quantization-induced performance degradation, proving more effective than heuristic methods that directly penalize the Jacobian.

Abstract: Quantization of Large Language Models (LLMs) is often hindered by the sensitivity of the self-attention mechanism to discretization errors. We identify the softmax operator as a bottleneck for quantization stability due to its sensitivity to outliers and state-dependent Jacobian. We theoretically establish that suppressing the norm of this Jacobian helps in bounding quantization-induced performance degradation. Based on this, we propose Jacobian-Guided Noise Injection, a training strategy that injects zero-mean Gaussian noise into pre-attention logits, with variance derived directly from the Jacobian Frobenius norm. Unlike prior approaches that rely on heuristic or penalise jacobian directly, our method provides a way to identify the optimal noise variance based on the local attention sensitivity. We evaluate the method on SOTA LLM architectures, where it demonstrates improved robustness over popular PTQ methods.
