Empirical analysis reveals that the proposed method gives up to +37% relative gains on Top-1 accuracy on ImageNet-1K for SigLIP and improves relative perplexity by upto 40% on WikiText for language models in low bit quantisation settings, proving the efficacy of the approach.

</details>


### [123] [Trojaning the Alignment: Stealthy Backdoor Attacks against Graph Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20991)
*Minhua Lin,Zhicheng Gao,Yilong Wang,Hanqing Lu,Xiang Zhang,Suhang Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: STAG is a stealthy backdoor attack framework for Graph Foundation Models on text-attributed graphs that coordinates graph triggers with text prompts to exploit the graph-language alignment interface.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing backdoor attacks on Graph Foundation Models treat graph and text modalities independently, which is ineffective for text-attributed graphs. Graph-only triggers are constrained by clean text, while text-only triggers don't shift graph representations directly. TAGs also pose stealth challenges as triggers are exposed in both node text and graph structure.

Method: STAG coordinates a graph-trigger generator with text-side soft prompts so trigger-attached graph representations and triggered text representations move toward the same target-class text region. For stealthiness, it realizes trigger nodes as readable text through candidate retrieval and regularizes the trigger-attached subgraph to maintain local structure similarity.

Result: Extensive experiments on multiple TAG datasets and representative GFMs demonstrate the effectiveness and stealthiness of STAG, showing it successfully bypasses existing detection methods.

Conclusion: STAG provides an effective framework for stealthy backdoor attacks on graph-language aligned GFMs, highlighting security vulnerabilities in these systems and providing a benchmark for future defense research.

Abstract: Graph Foundation Models (GFMs) on text-attributed graphs (TAGs) align graph representations with language semantics to support transferable graph learning. Despite these advantages, the backdoor vulnerability of GFMs on TAGs remains insufficiently understood, especially under graph-language alignment, where graph and text representations are trained to constrain each other in a shared semantic space. Existing backdoor attacks mainly target either the graph side or the text side, treating the two modalities independently. This makes direct adaptation ineffective: graph-only triggers can be constrained by clean text semantics, while text-only triggers alter the language view but do not directly shift the graph representation being aligned and scored. TAGs also impose a stealth challenge because triggers are exposed as both node text and local graph structure, making incoherent trigger attributes or anomalous subgraphs easy to inspect or filter. In this paper, we propose STAG, a stealthy trojan attack framework designed for the graph-language alignment interface of GFMs on TAGs. STAG coordinates a graph-trigger generator with a text-side soft prompt so that trigger-attached graph representations and triggered text representations move toward the same target-class text region. To address TAG-specific stealthiness, STAG realizes trigger nodes as readable text through candidate retrieval and regularizes the trigger-attached subgraph so that its local structure remains close to the original subgraph. Extensive experiments on multiple TAG datasets and representative GFMs demonstrate the effectiveness and stealthiness of STAG. Our code is available at https://github.com/ventr1c/STAG.

</details>


### [124] [Free-Probability Kernels for Zero-Rollout Hyperparameter Selection in Reservoir Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20998)
*Sara Malacarne,Andrea Ceni,Claudio Gallicchio*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A deterministic method using free-probability kernels to select reservoir computing hyperparameters without costly rollout simulations, achieving near-exhaustive search performance while avoiding thousands of rollouts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current reservoir computing suffers from inefficient hyperparameter selection that requires many rollouts to tune key parameters (recurrent gain, input scale, leakage rate), which is computationally expensive and loses the efficiency advantage of reservoir computing.

Method: Uses free probability theory to derive deterministic temporal kernels that approximate finite-reservoir feature geometry. For leaky linear reservoirs with coordinate-wise nonlinear features, computes cross-lag propagation coefficients that summarize how reservoirs mix past inputs. Kernel ridge regression on short pilot sequences ranks candidate operating regimes without needing to instantiate or roll out actual reservoirs.

Result: On 10 synthetic benchmarks, achieves mean deployment score of 0.772 vs 0.774 for exhaustive simulation search, while avoiding 156,600 selection rollouts. With small rollout budgets, provides strongest mean performance and reaches exhaustive reference using only 4.8% of its rollout cost. On real-world ETT forecasting datasets, retains 5 candidates to recover exhaustive operating points on 3 datasets. On cellular-traffic forecasting, uses only 15 rollouts per cell to reach 462-rollout exhaustive reference.

Conclusion: Free-probability kernels serve as effective deterministic surrogates for selecting reservoir computing operating regimes when validation rollouts are limited, significantly reducing computational costs while maintaining performance comparable to exhaustive search methods.

Abstract: Reservoir computing (RC) couples a fixed recurrent dynamical system with a trained lightweight readout, but this efficiency is partly lost during hyperparameter selection: the recurrent gain, input scale, and leakage rate determine the reservoir's stability and temporal processing regime and are usually tuned through many rollouts. We introduce a deterministic, pilot-informed selector for leaky linear reservoirs followed by coordinate-wise nonlinear features. Free probability yields cross-lag propagation coefficients that summarize how the reservoir mixes past inputs. In the large-width limit, these coefficients define a deterministic temporal kernel that approximates the finite-reservoir feature geometry. Kernel ridge regression on a short labelled pilot sequence therefore ranks candidate operating regimes without instantiating or rolling out a reservoir, and the selected configuration transfers across widths. Across ten synthetic temporal benchmarks, zero-rollout selection obtains a mean deployment score of $0.772$, compared with $0.774$ for exhaustive simulation-based search, while avoiding $156\,600$ selection rollouts. With a small rollout budget, the proposed ranking provides the strongest mean performance at every tested budget and reaches the exhaustive reference using $4.8\%$ of its rollout cost. On four public electricity-transformer-temperature (ETT) forecasting datasets, five retained candidates recover the exhaustive operating point on three datasets. On multivariate cellular-traffic forecasting, 15 rollouts per cell reach the 462-rollout exhaustive reference and outperform random search and Bayesian optimization at low budgets. These results position free-probability kernels as deterministic surrogates for selecting reservoir operating regimes when validation rollouts are scarce.

</details>


### [125] [RODE: A Radial-Orthogonal Decoupled Engine for Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21024)
*Guoxiang Xu,Bince Qu,Qi Sun,Cheng Zhuo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RODE is a new optimizer that decouples radial (norm) and directional updates for matrix-aware optimization, outperforming Muon variants on language modeling and image classification tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current matrix-aware optimizers add conditioned matrix steps directly to weights, which jointly changes both norm and direction. This interaction matters because norm affects angular motion, and directional learning can drive norm growth, altering later steps.

Method: RODE provides separate update rules and step sizes for radial and directional components: (1) explicitly updates matrix Frobenius norm through scalar radial rule, (2) performs Newton-Schulz-conditioned updates in tangent space for directional channel.

Result: RODE outperforms both Muon variants in every direct comparison across language modeling and image classification tasks. At 1.5B scale, lowers loss from 4.145 to 3.346 and final global norm from 11964 to 2183. For Qwen3.5-9B fine-tuning, achieves highest mean on GSM8K and MATH-500 among six optimizers.

Conclusion: Decoupling radial and directional dynamics offers a more effective and controllable approach to matrix optimization compared to joint updates in existing optimizers.

Abstract: Modern neural network training increasingly uses matrix-aware optimizers, yet their conditioned matrix step is typically added directly to the weight, jointly changing its norm and direction. This interaction matters because the current norm determines angular motion, while directional learning can drive norm growth and thereby alter later steps. We introduce RODE, which gives the radial and directional components separate update rules and step sizes. RODE explicitly updates the matrix Frobenius norm through a scalar radial rule, while its directional channel performs Newton--Schulz-conditioned updates in the tangent space. Controlled GPT-2 interventions show gains from both direct norm control and RODE's directional update. Across two language-modeling and two image-classification tasks, RODE outperforms both Muon variants in every direct comparison and ends with lower full-model norms. At 1.5B scale, using the learning rate transferred directly from the Qwen2-style LM sweep, RODE lowers loss from 4.145 to 3.346 and final global norm from 11964 to 2183 relative to Muon RMS, with fixed-radius RODE improving further. For Qwen3.5-9B full-parameter fine-tuning, all six optimizers use the same tuning budget and the same formal-training and evaluation settings; RODE outperforms both Muon variants on all four evaluation tasks and attains the highest mean on GSM8K and MATH-500. Thus, decoupling radial and directional dynamics offers a more effective and controllable approach to matrix optimization.

</details>


### [126] [Designing a Robust LLM-Based Evaluation System for Agentic AI in Drug Discovery Through Human Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21057)
*Emma Granqvist,Rocío Mercado,Samuel Genheden*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents a human-aligned LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation framework for agentic drug discovery systems, validating LLM judges against expert humans and optimizing them for scientific workflow evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a fundamental bottleneck in evaluating open-ended, tool-augmented outputs from agentic LLM systems in chemistry and drug discovery. Reference-based metrics (BLEU, ROUGE) fail to capture semantic correctness, while expert human evaluation doesn't scale to match system iteration speeds. Existing LLM-as-a-Judge approaches in drug discovery lack validation of their alignment with human experts.

Method: The researchers developed an LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation framework for ChatInvent (AstraZeneca's agentic drug discovery assistant). They defined four quality evaluation dimensions (Completeness, Relevancy, Structural Clarity, Scope Adherence) plus Tool Call Correctness checks. They conducted a human alignment study with five expert annotators comparing four LLM judges (Gemini 3.1 Pro, Claude Opus 4.7, GPT-5, Llama 3.1 70B). They optimized the best judge using few-shot demonstrations of human-annotated examples.

Result: The optimized judge improved alignment with human majority vote from 0.80 to 0.86. When applied to 70 held-out questions, the framework revealed that informal phrasings don't systematically degrade output quality, and having the LLM rewrite questions before agent querying can be beneficial. Concrete limitations of the system were identified through this evaluation.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a reusable template for human-aligned evaluation of agentic systems in scientific domains, addressing the scalability challenge while ensuring alignment with expert judgment through validation and optimization techniques.

Abstract: Agentic large language model (LLM) systems are reshaping scientific workflows in chemistry and drug discovery, but evaluating their open-ended, tool-augmented outputs remains a fundamental bottleneck. Reference-based metrics such as BLEU and ROUGE fail to capture semantic correctness, while expert human evaluation does not scale to the iteration speed these systems demand. The LLM-as-a-Judge paradigm has emerged as a scalable alternative, but existing drug discovery benchmarks deploy LLM judges without validating their alignment with human experts. In this work, we present an LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation framework for ChatInvent, an agentic drug discovery assistant deployed at AstraZeneca, with four contributions. First, we define four output-quality evaluation dimensions---Completeness, Relevancy, Structural Clarity, and Scope Adherence---alongside deterministic Tool Call Correctness checks. Second, we validate the judge through a human alignment study with five expert annotators, comparing Gemini 3.1 Pro, Claude Opus 4.7, GPT-5, and Llama 3.1 70B as candidate judges. Third, we optimize the best-performing judge using few-shot demonstrations of human-annotated examples, improving alignment with the human majority vote from 0.80 to 0.86. Fourth, applying the optimized judge to 70 held-out questions, we surface concrete limitations and find that informal phrasings do not systematically degrade output quality; if anything, it is helpful to have the LLM rewrite the original question before querying the agent. Our framework provides a reusable template for human-aligned evaluation of agentic systems in scientific domains.

</details>


### [127] [TracingFlow: A Simulation-Free Trajectory Inference Framework Based on Second-Order Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21070)
*Yuhao Sun,Zekun Wu,Zixun Huang,Peijie Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TracingFlow: A second-order Flow Matching framework that generalizes beyond first-order Optimal Transport by learning acceleration fields to capture complex biological dynamics like cell differentiation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Optimal Transport frameworks for inferring continuous system evolution are limited to first-order dynamics (memoryless velocity fields), which fails to account for regulatory momentum and time-delayed responses inherent in processes like cell differentiation.

Method: Introduces TracingFlow, a simulation-free Flow Matching framework that generalizes to second-order dynamics. Uses neural networks to regress acceleration fields, providing an exact, efficient solution to the Dynamical Optimal Acceleration Transport (DOAT) problem.

Result: Achieves superior accuracy in distributional reconstruction and trajectory faithfulness on complex synthetic and large-scale scRNA-seq datasets. Captures high-curvature transitions and nonlinear evolutions that first-order methods oversmooth. Integrates lineage tracing priors to recover mathematically optimal and biologically plausible dynamical structures.

Conclusion: Second-order TracingFlow framework overcomes limitations of first-order Optimal Transport methods by learning acceleration/force fields, enabling more accurate modeling of complex biological processes with momentum and time-delayed responses.

Abstract: Inferring continuous system evolution from sparse temporal snapshots is a key challenge in generative modeling and single-cell omics. While Optimal Transport (OT) is popular, existing frameworks are largely restricted to first-order dynamics, assuming memoryless velocity fields. This limits expressiveness, as first-order systems fail to account for regulatory momentum and time-delayed responses inherent in processes like cell differentiation. Here, we introduce TracingFlow, a simulation-free Flow Matching framework generalizing to second-order dynamics. By using neural networks to regress the acceleration field, TracingFlow provides an exact, efficient solution to the Dynamical Optimal Acceleration Transport (DOAT) problem. Unlike first-order methods yielding over-smoothed trajectories, our second-order formulation captures high-curvature transitions and nonlinear evolutions by learning the underlying force fields. Evaluated on complex synthetic and large-scale scRNA-seq datasets, TracingFlow achieves superior accuracy in distributional reconstruction and trajectory faithfulness. Moreover, by integrating lineage tracing priors, it recovers dynamical structures that are both mathematically optimal and biologically plausible.

</details>


### [128] [Causal Modeling of Adverse Pregnancy Outcomes via Adaptive LLM Proposals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21079)
*Kavimayil P. Komarasamy,Saurabh Mathur,Ameet Soni,David M. Haas,Kristian Kersting,Sriraam Natarajan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A neurosymbolic framework combining LLM knowledge with empirical data scoring to generate plausible causal hypotheses for adverse pregnancy outcomes, outperforming pure data-driven methods and recovering expert-validated edges while discovering new relations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adverse Pregnancy Outcomes (APOs) have long-term consequences but their causes remain elusive. Causal discovery is challenging due to limited data and incomplete domain knowledge, causing pure data-driven methods to fail and LLM outputs to be inconsistent or contradictory.

Method: Introduces a neurosymbolic framework that iteratively combines LLM broad prior knowledge with empirical data scoring. Treats LLM as an adaptive proposal distribution generating hypotheses that are scored against data; high-scoring graphs update LLM context to steer subsequent generations toward promising hypothesis space regions.

Result: Evaluated on real-world clinical dataset for modeling APOs and risk factors. Method recovers all expert-validated edges and identifies additional plausible causal relations not previously listed by experts, potentially providing new insights for targeted interventions.

Conclusion: The neurosymbolic framework successfully addresses causal discovery challenges in APOs by combining LLM knowledge with empirical validation, generating reliable hypotheses that align with expert knowledge while discovering novel relations for potential clinical interventions.

Abstract: Adverse Pregnancy Outcomes (APOs) such as preterm birth and gestational diabetes can have long-term consequences for both the mother and child, yet an understanding of their causes remains elusive. Causal discovery in this domain is especially challenging due to a paucity of data and incomplete domain knowledge. As a result, pure data-driven methods fail, and Large Language Model (LLM) outputs remain inconsistent or contradictory. We introduce a neurosymbolic framework for generating plausible causal hypotheses that iteratively combines the broad prior knowledge of LLMs with empirical scoring on data. Our method treats the LLM as an adaptive proposal distribution, generating hypotheses that are scored against empirical data; the resulting high-scoring graphs are then used to update the LLM's context, steering subsequent generations toward more promising regions of the hypothesis space. We evaluate our approach on a real-world clinical dataset for modeling APOs and their risk factors, comparing our results against an expert-constructed causal graph. Our method recovers all expert-validated edges and identifies additional plausible causal relations not previously listed by experts, potentially providing new insights for targeted interventions.

</details>


### [129] [FlatLand: Personalized Graph Federated Learning via Tailored Lorentz Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21096)
*Jiahong Liu,Ram Samarth B B,Xinyu Fu,Menglin Yang,Weixi Zhang,Rex Ying,Irwin King*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FlatLand is a personalized federated learning method for graphs that uses Lorentz hyperbolic geometry to handle data heterogeneity by separating client-specific parameters from common knowledge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current personalized federated learning methods for graphs fail to account for the intrinsic geometric properties of diverse graph structures, particularly the heterogeneous nature of client data in graph federated learning.

Method: Embeds clients' data in tailored Lorentz space of hyperbolic geometry, uses time-like dimension to encode client-specific heterogeneity, and implements parameter decoupling strategy separating heterogeneous information (time-like parameters) from common knowledge (space-like parameters).

Result: Empirical results show FlatLand achieves superior performance on diverse federated graph learning tasks, especially in low-dimensional settings.

Conclusion: Hyperbolic geometry in Lorentz space provides an effective framework for handling graph heterogeneity in federated learning, enabling direct aggregation without requiring client similarity estimation or additional modules.

Abstract: Federated learning enables privacy-preserving collaborative training, but highly heterogeneous client data remain challenging, especially in graph federated learning where clients possess structurally diverse graphs. Existing personalized federated learning (PFL) methods ignore the intrinsic geometric properties of diverse graph structures. We propose FlatLand, a novel personalized federated learning method that embeds different clients' data in tailored Lorentz space of hyperbolic geometry. Our key insight is that hyperbolic geometry naturally accommodates the intrinsic negative curvature prevalent in real-world graphs, while the time-like dimension in Lorentz space provides a principled way to encode client-specific heterogeneity. We develop a parameter decoupling strategy that separates heterogeneous information (captured in time-like parameters) from common knowledge (preserved in space-like parameters), enabling direct aggregation without requiring client similarity estimation and extra calculation modules. Empirical results on diverse federated graph learning tasks demonstrate that FlatLand achieves superior performance, particularly in low-dimensional settings.

</details>


### [130] [BackDFL: A Unified Benchmark For Backdoor Attacks and Defenses In Decentralized Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21137)
*Mouhamed Amine Bouchiha,Gregory Blanc,Yufei Han*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DFL is highly vulnerable to backdoor attacks; existing defenses fail under realistic conditions, especially with modest malicious participation rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper argues that DFL robustness has been significantly overestimated due to simplified threat models, non-adaptive adversaries, fragmented evaluation protocols, inconsistent topologies, and ad hoc configurations, leading to incomplete security understanding.

Method: Presents BackDFL - a unified benchmark for systematically evaluating DFL under realistic and adaptive backdoor attacks, addressing limitations of existing studies.

Result: Extensive experiments show both state-of-the-art Byzantine-robust DFL methods and adapted FL backdoor defenses fail under modest malicious participation rates (as low as 15%), especially in heterogeneous settings, with robustness varying substantially across communication graph topologies.

Conclusion: DFL has critical failure modes exposed by the BackDFL benchmark, demonstrating severe vulnerabilities to backdoor attacks that were previously underestimated, highlighting the need for more rigorous security evaluation in decentralized settings.

Abstract: Decentralized Federated Learning (DFL) promises trust-free collaborative learning by replacing the centralized parameter server with peer-to-peer model exchange. However, this architectural shift fundamentally reshapes the threat landscape. Without globally coordinated aggregation, DFL becomes particularly susceptible to backdoor attacks, in which malicious participants implant persistent hidden behaviors while maintaining high clean-task performance. In this paper, we argue that the robustness of DFL has been significantly overestimated. Existing studies rely on simplified threat models, non-adaptive adversaries, fragmented evaluation protocols, inconsistent communication topologies, and ad hoc training configurations, leading to an incomplete understanding of DFL security. To address these limitations, we present BackDFL, a unified benchmark for systematically evaluating DFL under realistic and adaptive backdoor attacks. Through extensive experiments, BackDFL exposes critical failure modes of decentralized learning. Our results demonstrate that both state-of-the-art Byzantine-robust DFL methods and adapted FL backdoor defenses fail under modest malicious participation rates (as low as 15%), especially in heterogeneous settings, while their robustness varies substantially across communication graph topologies.

</details>


### [131] [COEC: Calibrated Orthogonal-Equivalence Compensation for Structured Pruning of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21142)
*Peiqi Yu,Nam Ling,Wei Wang,Wei Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: COEC is a training-free compensation framework that uses alternating orthogonal rotations and singular value rescaling to improve accuracy of structured pruning in LLMs without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Structured pruning reduces LLM size and inference cost but degrades accuracy due to output errors. Existing training-free compensation methods are limited because they only use additive bias or single orthogonal rotations, leaving the input singular frame unchanged and limiting how retained weights can adapt after column removal.

Method: COEC applies alternating left and right orthogonal rotations to retained weights. The right rotation is optimized on a reduced Stiefel manifold, singular values are rescaled using generalized cross-validation for regularization strength per layer. It tempers the calibration Gram matrix to reduce high-energy activation dominance and includes alignment penalty to preserve geometric relations between adjacent attention projections. All components use second-order statistics from small calibration set without backpropagation or retraining.

Result: Experiments on Llama-3, Llama-3.1, and Qwen2.5 model families across multiple structured sparsity levels show COEC improves perplexity on every model and zero-shot accuracy in most settings over existing compensation methods, with larger gains at higher sparsity.

Conclusion: COEC demonstrates that post-pruning compensation can recover part of performance lost to column removal, offering an effective training-free method independent of pruning criteria that works with multiple structured pruning methods.

Abstract: Structured pruning reduces the size and inference cost of large language models (LLMs) by removing weight columns, but the resulting output error can degrade accuracy. Existing training-free compensation methods use an additive bias or a single orthogonal rotation on the output side of the retained weight. These corrections leave its input singular frame unchanged and therefore limit how the retained weight can adapt after column removal. We propose COEC (Calibrated Orthogonal-Equivalence Compensation), a training-free compensation framework that applies alternating left and right orthogonal rotations to the retained weight. The right rotation is optimized on a reduced Stiefel manifold, while singular values are rescaled using generalized cross-validation to select the regularization strength for each layer. COEC further tempers the calibration Gram matrix to reduce the dominance of high-energy activation directions and introduces an alignment penalty that preserves the geometric relation between adjacent attention projections.All components use second-order statistics from a small calibration set and require neither backpropagation through the LLM nor retraining of the model parameters. COEC is independent of the column pruning criterion and can be applied to multiple structured pruning methods. Experiments on the Llama-3, Llama-3.1, and Qwen2.5 model families across multiple structured sparsity levels show that COEC improves perplexity on every model and zero-shot accuracy in most settings over existing compensation methods, with larger gains at higher sparsity. These results show that post-pruning compensation can recover part of the performance lost to column removal.

</details>


### [132] [Capturing Cardiac Cyclicity through Phase-Equivariant Self-Supervised Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21147)
*Blaise Delaney,Dominic Dootson,Juan Jose Juan Castella,Salil Patel,Andrew Pfaff,Yuji Xing,Jonny Hancox,Karin Sevegnani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Winder is a self-supervised method that learns phase-equivariant representations of cardiac signals by exploiting the cyclic nature of heartbeats, achieving competitive diagnostic accuracy with minimal parameters while maintaining interpretable latent geometry tied to physiological phase.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The cardiac cycle provides a well-defined cyclic structure that can serve as a natural prior for self-supervised representation learning. Current methods may not fully exploit this physiological symmetry, potentially missing opportunities for parameter efficiency and interpretability.

Method: Winder is a joint-embedding architecture that organizes representations into phase-invariant coordinates and phase-rotating harmonic subspaces. It uses a fixed, closed-form transport operator derived from the cycle's geometry rather than learning it, adding no extra parameters.

Result: On PTB-XL dataset with frozen linear-probe protocol, Winder achieves diagnostic accuracy comparable to state-of-the-art self-supervised methods while using only ~1M parameters. The method exhibits phase-equivariant latent geometry directly tied to measurable physiological quantities.

Conclusion: Explicitly encoding cardiac-phase symmetry preserves diagnostically useful information while yielding parameter-efficient, legible latent geometry directly connected to physiological measurements. This demonstrates the value of incorporating known physiological structure into representation learning.

Abstract: The cyclic structure of physiological processes offers a natural prior for self-supervised representation learning, and the cardiac cycle provides a particularly well-defined setting in which to exploit it. We derive a phase-equivariant self-supervised objective and introduce Winder, a joint-embedding architecture that organises representations into phase-invariant coordinates and phase-rotating harmonic subspaces. Its transport operator is fixed and closed-form, derived from the cycle's geometry rather than learned, and adds no parameters. Evaluated on PTB-XL under a frozen linear-probe protocol, Winder attains diagnostic accuracy within the range reported by state-of-the-art self-supervised methods at a ~1 M parameter footprint, while exhibiting phase-equivariant latent geometry. These findings demonstrate that explicitly encoding cardiac-phase symmetry can preserve diagnostically useful information while yielding a latent geometry that is legible, parameter-efficient, and directly tied to a measurable physiological quantity.

</details>


### [133] [Thermo-FL: Thermal-Aware Robust Federated Fine-Tuning of Large Language Models for Edge AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21172)
*Shiva Shrestha,Kazi Shaharair Sharif,Zongxing Xie,Jiajing Huang,Anhao Xiang,Honghui Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Thermo-FL is a thermal-aware federated fine-tuning framework that uses device temperature to control LoRA fine-tuning and introduces robust aggregation (TERRA) for handling both hardware thermal constraints and adversarial attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated fine-tuning of LLMs on edge devices faces two key challenges: hardware thermal constraints causing device throttling/training slowdowns, and adversarial attacks corrupting model updates. Existing approaches don't address both problems together.

Method: Thermo-FL uses device temperature as a control signal to adjust active LoRA-layer fraction and transmitted update density during thermal stress. Server-side introduces TERRA pipeline: norm filtering, mask-aware directional validation, adaptive active-coordinate clipping, and mask-aware aggregation for robust handling of sparse LoRA updates.

Result: In emulator: Thermo-FL improves robustness under adversarial sparse aggregation, achieves strongest BoolQ accuracy across clean/attack settings while competitive on GSM8K. In physical testbed: stabilizes device temperature, reduces compressed upload size via bitmap sparse encoding, preserves GSM8K utility under sign-flip/scale and MITM attacks.

Conclusion: Secure edge LLM adaptation must jointly address hardware behavior, workload regulation, sparse communication, and aggregation robustness, showing Thermo-FL's effectiveness in handling both thermal constraints and adversarial attacks in federated fine-tuning.

Abstract: Federated fine-tuning enables large language models to adapt on edge devices without centralizing private data, but practical deployments must address hardware instability and adversarial update corruption together. Thermally constrained clients may throttle, slow local training, or delay synchronous aggregation, while Byzantine clients and communication-layer adversaries can corrupt the updates used to form the global model. To address these challenges, we present Thermo-FL, a thermal-aware federated LoRA fine-tuning framework that uses device temperature as an active control signal for local adapter training and sparse update transmission. On the client side, Thermo-FL adjusts the active LoRA-layer fraction and transmitted update density as devices heat or cool, reducing workload under thermal stress. On the server side, Thermo-FL introduces TERRA, a robust aggregation pipeline for dynamically sparse LoRA updates that combines norm filtering, mask-aware directional validation, adaptive active-coordinate clipping, and mask-aware aggregation. We evaluate Thermo-FL using both a large-scale emulator and a Jetson-based physical testbed. In the emulator, Thermo-FL improves robustness under adversarial sparse aggregation and achieves the strongest BoolQ accuracy across clean and attack settings while remaining competitive on GSM8K. In the physical prototype, Thermo-FL stabilizes device temperature, reduces compressed upload size through bitmap sparse encoding, and preserves GSM8K utility under sign-flip/scale and MITM perturbations. These results show that secure edge LLM adaptation should jointly consider hardware behavior, workload regulation, sparse communication, and aggregation robustness.

</details>


### [134] [A Neurosymbolic Approach for Constructing Planning Domain Models from Clinical Narratives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21186)
*Ranveer Singh,Saurabh Mathur,Michael Skinner,Prasad Tadepalli,Kristian Kersting,Sriraam Natarajan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Neurosymbolic framework NSPIN combines LLMs with symbolic reasoning to extract surgical workflows from clinical narratives and induce probabilistic planning domain models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Formalizing surgical workflows (like laparoscopic appendectomy) is challenging due to unstructured clinical narratives, implicit actions, and limitations of existing methods - neither purely symbolic approaches nor LLMs alone can adequately address these issues.

Method: NSPIN framework: (1) Uses pretrained LLM to extract and impute structured event sequences from raw clinical text, (2) Induces a PPDDL (probabilistic planning domain definition language) model, (3) Refines preconditions with LLM-proposed revisions guided by empirical validation.

Result: Evaluated on 2,660 laparoscopic appendectomy notes from 9 surgeons. NSPIN yields models that generalize to unseen notes, and expert clinical review indicates induced knowledge is largely consistent with surgical practice.

Conclusion: Neurosymbolic approach successfully combines strengths of LLMs and symbolic reasoning for inducing probabilistic planning domain models from unstructured surgical narratives, addressing limitations of individual methods.

Abstract: Surgical procedures such as laparoscopic appendectomy are complex, high-stakes processes, yet formalizing their workflows for decision support remains a significant challenge. Inducing probabilistic planning domain models in this setting is particularly difficult due to the lack of structured event data and the prevalence of implicit actions in clinical narratives, which neither empirical symbolic methods nor Large Language Models (LLMs) can adequately address on their own. We introduce NSPIN, a neurosymbolic framework for inducing probabilistic planning domain models from unstructured clinical narratives. Our method extracts and imputes structured event sequences from raw text using a pretrained LLM, then induces a PPDDL model and refines its preconditions with LLM-proposed revisions, guided by empirical validation. We evaluate the approach on 2,660 laparoscopic appendectomy notes written by 9 surgeons. NSPIN yields models that generalize to unseen notes, and expert clinical review indicates its induced knowledge is largely consistent with surgical practice.

</details>


### [135] [Tydra: An Efficient Hybrid Model for Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21199)
*Mieszko Komisarczyk,Saurabh Mathur,Maurice Kraus,Sriraam Natarajan,Kristian Kersting*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Tydra: A hybrid Transformer-State Space Model (SSM) architecture for tabular in-context learning that balances accuracy and efficiency, achieving 30% faster inference than TabPFN while maintaining predictive performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transformers like TabPFN provide strong predictive performance but suffer from quadratic computational costs, while SSM-based alternatives like Hydra trade accuracy for efficiency. There's a need to balance both accuracy and computational efficiency in tabular foundation models.

Method: Introduces Tydra, a hybrid Transformer-SSM architecture that interleaves attention and SSM layers for tabular in-context learning. This design combines the strengths of both approaches to optimize both predictive performance and computational efficiency.

Result: On 30 OpenML datasets: 1) Reduces inference time by 30% relative to TabPFN while retaining most of its predictive performance, 2) Outperforms an approximately ten-times-larger Hydra model while providing faster inference.

Conclusion: Hybrid Transformer-SSM architectures like Tydra represent a promising direction for developing efficient yet accurate tabular foundation models, successfully balancing the trade-off between computational cost and predictive performance.

Abstract: Transformer-based tabular foundation models such as TabPFN achieve strong predictive performance but incur quadratic computational cost with context length. On the other hand, subquadratic SSM-based alternatives such as Hydra trade away accuracy for efficiency. To balance both, we introduce Tydra, a hybrid Transformer-State Space Model (SSM) architecture for tabular in-context learning that interleaves attention and SSM layers. Across 30 OpenML datasets, Tydra reduces inference time by 30% relative to TabPFN while retaining much of its predictive performance. Tydra also outperforms an approximately ten-times-larger Hydra model while providing faster inference. The results indicate that hybrid architectures are a promising direction for tabular foundation models.

</details>


### [136] [Curriculum-Aware Interpolate-then-Refine: Learned Physiological Time-Series Imputation under Realistic Missingness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21207)
*Yu-Chao Huang,Haochen Zhang,Nicholas Konz,Tianlong Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CAIR is a two-stage physiological time-series imputation framework that first learns a coarse base curve then refines it through successive corrections, outperforming all baselines across different missingness mechanisms while preserving clinically important metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing imputation methods perform poorly on physiological time series due to two domain-specific properties: 1) gaps often occur when signals are clinically extreme rather than typical, and 2) gap lengths can span orders of magnitude. Simple linear interpolation was found to outperform all learned imputers in recent benchmarks on real-world clinical signals with realistic gaps.

Method: CAIR (Curriculum-Aware Interpolate-then-Refine) is a two-stage framework: first, a bidirectional-GRU interpolator learns a coarse base curve; second, a Transformer refiner corrects this estimate over three successive passes. The model is trained jointly under a broad, signal-agnostic random-gap curriculum that accounts for varying gap lengths and missingness mechanisms.

Result: CAIR outperforms all baselines across three missingness mechanisms (MCAR, MAR, NMAR) on continuous glucose monitoring (AI-READI) and arterial pressure (MIMIC-III) data. Performance gap grows with difficulty: 9% improvement under MCAR to 19% under value-dependent dropout. Importantly, CAIR preserves clinically relevant burden metrics while maintaining low reconstruction error - most imputers either achieve low error OR preserve clinical metrics, but not both.

Conclusion: The CAIR framework successfully addresses domain-specific challenges of physiological time-series imputation by combining coarse interpolation with iterative refinement. It demonstrates that preserving clinically important metrics requires more than just low reconstruction error, and that specialized architectures accounting for physiological signal properties are necessary for effective clinical data imputation.

Abstract: Imputing physiological time series (arterial blood pressure, blood glucose, etc.) is essential for addressing the missingness that pervades clinical data. Yet modern imputation methods perform poorly in this domain: a recent benchmark found that simple linear interpolation outperformed every learned imputer on real-world clinical signals with realistic gaps. We show that this reflects two properties of physiological missingness that generic imputers ignore: gaps may occur when the signal is clinically extreme rather than typical, and gap lengths can easily span orders of magnitude. To this end, we introduce Curriculum-Aware Interpolate-then-Refine (CAIR), a two-stage framework for physiological time-series imputation. Our key motivation is to learn a coarse base curve and then repeatedly correct it toward physiological realism, rather than predict a gap in a single pass. Consequently, CAIR couples a bidirectional-GRU interpolator with a Transformer refiner that corrects its own estimate over three successive passes, trained jointly under a broad, signal-agnostic random-gap curriculum. We evaluate imputers stratified by gap length and missingness mechanism (MCAR, MAR, NMAR) rather than by a single average, and CAIR is the most accurate under every mechanism on continuous glucose monitoring (AI-READI) and arterial pressure in intensive care (MIMIC-III). Its margin over the strongest baseline grows with difficulty, from 9% under MCAR to 19% under value-dependent dropout, where generic learned imputers are weakest. We further show low reconstruction error alone does not recover the burden metrics clinicians act on: interpolants matching CAIR's error fail to preserve those metrics, imputers that recover them are far less accurate, and CAIR alone ranks among the best on both axes.

</details>


### [137] [TRACE-C: Rank-Calibrated Relational Anomaly Detection for Multi-Stream Operational Telemetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21251)
*Matthew Faucher*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TRACE-C is a multi-stream anomaly detector that identifies joint anomalies across telemetry streams using three statistical channels (local sum, copula-form dependence contrast, and AR(1) innovations), with their ranks Fisher-aggregated for final detection ranking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing anomaly detection may miss joint anomalies where individual streams appear normal within their familiar ranges but collectively exhibit abnormal patterns. There's a need for auditable, rank-calibrated detectors that can identify such multi-stream anomalies while maintaining interpretability and avoiding probability misinterpretations.

Method: TRACE-C uses same-regime rolling median/MAD residuals from aligned telemetry streams, processed through three channels: 1) maximum normalized local sum, 2) Gaussian copula-form dependence contrast on robust-z residuals, 3) worst standardized AR(1) innovation. The channel ranks are Fisher-aggregated and compared against historical aggregates for anomaly ranking.

Result: The method successfully detected Storm Atiyah (ranked first in 2019 test) but attribution analysis showed the local channel (not copula channel) drove this detection. It ranked a frequency event much lower than temporal-only methods would have. In 2020, no windows were selected, consistent with record-rule saturation rather than absence of events. The method provides interpretable rankings but with important limitations about probability interpretations.

Conclusion: TRACE-C provides an auditable, rank-calibrated approach for multi-stream anomaly detection that reveals joint anomalies individual detectors miss. However, the resulting p-values are selection quantities rather than event probabilities, and empirical rank counts serve as diagnostics rather than coverage proofs, requiring careful interpretation of results.

Abstract: Operational telemetry can be jointly anomalous while every individual stream stays inside its familiar range. TRACE-C is an auditable strictly-prior rank-calibrated detector for aligned multi-stream telemetry: same-regime rolling median/MAD residuals feed three window channels -- a maximum normalized local sum, a Gaussian copula-form dependence contrast on robust-z residuals, and a worst standardized AR(1) innovation -- whose channel ranks are Fisher-aggregated and ranked against earlier aggregates.
  We evaluate six Great Britain grid streams with a January-April 2019 fit, July-December 2019 development evidence, and a 2020 hold-out frozen before inspection. TRACE-C ranks Storm Atiyah first among 2019 test windows, but a disclosed channel ablation attributes that rank to the local channel, not the copula-form channel: copula-only ranks Atiyah 59th. The short 9 August frequency event is ranked far lower by the fused detector (143) than by the temporal channel alone (40), and reconstruction baselines rank it first. In 2020 no window is selected, which is consistent with record-rule saturation rather than an uneventful year; the highest-ranked frozen window was later interpreted as Storm Ellen.
  Three interpretive limits carry throughout. The resulting p-values are selection quantities, not event probabilities. The copula-form channel is not a literal copula density: the method applies no probability-integral or normal-score transform. Empirical rank counts are diagnostics, not coverage or false-discovery proofs. Every table and figure in this paper is generated from committed machine-readable reports.

</details>


### [138] [ConceptTS: LLM-Guided Concept Bottlenecks for Interpretable Multivariate Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21277)
*Yichen Jiang,Yueqiao Chen,Dongyu Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ConceptTS is an interpretable multivariate time-series forecasting framework that organizes predictions around human-readable concepts, using LLMs to generate concept definitions and achieving competitive accuracy with black-box models while providing transparency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multivariate time-series forecasters lack transparency - their opaque representations provide limited insight into why forecasts are produced, restricting their use in settings where practitioners need to understand and assess the factors underlying predictions.

Method: Uses LLM to propose task-relevant concepts and generate executable labeling rules, organizing concepts into three complementary bottlenecks (historical context, local forecast intervals, full forecast horizon). A shared decoder combines representations from predicted concept activations to construct forecasts, making decision process explicit.

Result: Experiments on Beijing Multi-Site Air Quality dataset show ConceptTS achieves accuracy competitive with strong black-box baselines while producing semantically meaningful concept activations.

Conclusion: ConceptTS provides an interpretable forecasting framework that organizes predictions around human-readable concepts, making model decisions transparent while maintaining competitive accuracy with black-box methods, enabling practical use in settings requiring interpretability.

Abstract: State-of-the-art multivariate time-series forecasters can model complex temporal and cross-variable dependencies, yet their opaque representations provide limited insight into why a particular forecast is produced. This lack of transparency restricts their use in settings where practitioners must understand and assess the factors underlying a prediction. We introduce ConceptTS, an interpretable forecasting framework that organizes its predictions around named, human-readable concepts. ConceptTS uses a large language model to propose task-relevant concepts and generate executable labeling rules, translating the language model's domain knowledge into direct supervision without costly manual concept annotation. The proposed concepts are organized into three complementary bottlenecks that describe the historical context, local forecast intervals, and the full forecast horizon. A shared decoder combines representations derived from their predicted activations to construct the forecast, making the model's decision process explicit and supporting direct concept-level interventions. Experiments on the Beijing Multi-Site Air Quality dataset show that ConceptTS achieves accuracy competitive with strong black-box baselines while producing semantically meaningful concept activations.

</details>


### [139] [SPARCL: Spectral Partitioned Analytic Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21307)
*James Hartley,Zeropy Surio,Daniel Whitmore,Hannah Clarke,Thomas Reed*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SPARCL addresses forgetting in analytic continual learning by identifying spectral interference as the cause and proposing a spectral partitioning method that freezes old-class classifiers in high-energy subspaces while updating only residual components.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses why analytic continual learning methods still experience forgetting despite exact recursive solvers, identifying spectral interference as the underlying cause. Traditional forgetting narratives based on gradient overwriting don't explain why closed-form ridge updates cause drift in old-class performance when new task samples load onto old dominant eigendirections.

Method: SPARCL (spectral partitioned analytic continual learner) decomposes the running autocorrelation matrix into high-energy core and residual complement subspaces. It freezes old-class classifier components in the core subspace and updates only the residual block using recursive least squares. An optional residual random-projection expansion can be added for better performance.

Result: SPARCL closes most of the performance gap between classical analytic learners and strong representation matchers across multiple datasets (CIFAR-100, CUB-200, ImageNet-R, ImageNet-A) under frozen ViT-B/16 protocol. The method provides provable invariance guarantees for core contributions of old logits.

Conclusion: The paper successfully identifies spectral interference as the fundamental cause of forgetting in analytic continual learning and proposes SPARCL as an effective solution that maintains closed-form updates while providing theoretical guarantees. The approach is complementary to sparse feature-decorrelation methods like Fly-CL.

Abstract: Analytic continual learning has emerged as a strong exemplar-free alternative to gradient-based class-incremental learning because it replaces iterative optimization with closed-form ridge updates. Yet the usual forgetting narrative, centered on stochastic gradient overwriting, does not explain why analytic methods still drift on old classes despite exact recursive solvers. We identify the culprit as spectral interference: the joint ridge classifier for all tasks shares the inverse autocorrelation operator $(R+λI)^{-1}$, so incoming task samples that load onto old dominant eigendirections dilute the spectrum and perturb old-class logits even when old labels are never revisited. Based on this view, we propose SPARCL, a spectral partitioned analytic continual learner that decomposes the running autocorrelation into a high-energy core and a residual complement, freezes old-class classifier components in the core subspace, and updates only the residual block through recursive least squares with an optional residual random-projection expansion. This yields a simple closed-form update with a provable invariance guarantee for the core contribution of old logits. Across CIFAR-100, CUB-200, ImageNet-R, and ImageNet-A under a frozen ViT-B/16 protocol, SPARCL closes most of the gap from classical analytic learners to strong representation matchers, while remaining complementary to sparse feature-decorrelation approaches such as Fly-CL.

</details>


### [140] [Rethinking Expressivity and Efficiency in Test-Time Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21308)
*Zeyun Zhong,Joya Chen,Manuel Martin,Frederik Diederichs,Juergen Gall,Juergen Beyerer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: E²-TTT is a Test-Time Training method that enables efficient long-context processing by bridging the gap between expressive per-token updates and efficient chunk-wise approximations through closed-form state transitions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Test-Time Training (TTT) methods struggle to balance expressivity of per-token update dynamics with hardware efficiency of chunk-wise approximations for long-context processing.

Method: Derived a closed-form state transition that exactly reproduces chunk-end fast-weight and momentum states of per-token recurrence while using chunk-start weights, enabling fully parallelized chunk-level training while preserving temporal update structure.

Result: Trains models up to 1.3B parameters; performs on par with TTT and hybrid attention baselines in language modeling; outperforms on in-context retrieval; achieves >90% accuracy at 8x training context length in Needle in a Haystack test; matches training throughput of efficient chunk-wise methods.

Conclusion: E²-TTT effectively reconciles expressivity with efficiency in Test-Time Training for long-context processing, achieving strong performance while maintaining hardware efficiency.

Abstract: Test-Time Training (TTT) enables long-context processing via continuous weight updates during inference, but current methods struggle to balance the expressivity of per-token update dynamics with the hardware efficiency of chunk-wise approximations. We propose E$^2$-TTT (Expressive and Efficient TTT) to bridge this gap. Under the standard approximation of taking gradients at the chunk-start weights, we derive a closed-form state transition that exactly reproduces the chunk-end fast-weight and momentum states of the per-token recurrence. This enables fully parallelized chunk-level training while preserving the temporal structure of the update rule that prior chunk-wise methods discard. We validate E$^2$-TTT by training models up to 1.3B parameters from scratch. It performs on par with previous TTT and hybrid attention baselines in language modeling while outperforming them on in-context retrieval. Its advantage is most pronounced in length extrapolation: on the standard ``Needle in a Haystack'' passkey test, it retains over 90% accuracy at $8\times$ the training context length. Meanwhile, E$^2$-TTT can match the training throughput of efficient chunk-wise methods, demonstrating that it effectively reconciles expressivity with efficiency. The code is available at https://github.com/zeyun-zhong/E2-TTT.

</details>


### [141] [Time-Aware Tranformer-Based Prediction Model for AECOPD](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21324)
*Weihao Qu,Ling Zheng,Dongyang Wang,Jiacun Wang,Haowen Pan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A Time-Aware transformer model for predicting AECOPD using ventilator data outperforms traditional methods by capturing symptom temporal progression and minimizing latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AECOPD prediction models rely on clinical/lab data causing latency, while home monitoring with ventilator data offers timeliness but lacks models that capture symptom temporal progression.

Method: Developed a Time-Aware transformer-based model that generates meaningful patient representations from daily-use ventilator data, capturing both symptoms and their temporal progression patterns.

Result: The Time-Aware transformer approach outperformed traditional methods across multiple classification tasks, demonstrating improved AECOPD prediction accuracy.

Conclusion: Time-Aware transformers show strong potential for timely AECOPD prediction in home monitoring scenarios, offering reduced latency compared to clinical/lab data-based approaches.

Abstract: The rapid symptom change of Acute exacerbation of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (AECOPD) makes it critical to have time-sensitive prediction models. However, most current machine learning models studying AECOPD use clinical and laboratory data, which will inevitably cause latency. To ensure timely detection of AECOPD and minimize latency, this paper focuses on home monitoring scenarios where only respiratory data from daily-use ventilators is available. We introduce a Time-Aware transformer-based AECOPD prediction model, which generates meaningful patient representations using the Time-Aware transformer to capture the symptoms and their temporal progression in ventilator data. Our experimental results demonstrate that our Time-Aware transformer-based approach outperforms traditional methods in multiple classification tasks, highlighting its potential to enhance AECOPD prediction accuracy.

</details>


### [142] [Across-Design Uncertainty in Short Pricing Panels: Evidence from Simulated Price Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21334)
*Pedro Cadahia Delgado*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Short pricing panels have many observations but few price movements, creating statistical challenges. The paper shows that most estimation error comes from variation across different possible price trajectories, not within a single observed trajectory. Solutions involve creating independent price variation rather than relying on passive panel data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Short observational pricing panels often contain many observations but only a small number of distinct price movements, creating inferential challenges. Traditional resampling methods that rely on single realized price trajectories fail to account for uncertainty across alternative possible trajectories generated by the same pricing process.

Method: The authors use synthetic data-generating processes calibrated to sparse pricing regimes, separating uncertainty conditional on realized price trajectories from variation in estimation error across alternative trajectories. They analyze three key relationships: 1) empirical relation between dispersion and price movement characteristics, 2) effects of adding regions vs. independent units, and 3) variance component estimation using Paule-Mandel method.

Result: Across-design dispersion accounts for 97.6% of variance in estimation error for gradient-boosted specifications. The empirical relationship sigma_hat ≈ 0.182 V^(-0.271) describes dispersion well, where V = moves × magnitude². Adding regions doesn't create independent price trajectories, while independent unit averaging reduces dispersion at square root rate. Paule-Mandel variance estimation increases empirical coverage from 0.469 to 0.931 in homogeneous simulations.

Conclusion: The key insight is that inferential uncertainty in sparse pricing panels comes primarily from variation across possible price trajectories rather than within observed trajectories. The broader implication is a paradigm shift toward designing data-generating processes that create independent identifying variation rather than relying solely on fixed passive panels.

Abstract: Short observational pricing panels can contain many observations while offering only a small number of distinct price movements. This paper studies the inferential consequences of that distinction in a synthetic data-generating process calibrated to a sparse pricing regime. We separate uncertainty conditional on a realised price trajectory from variation in estimation error across alternative trajectories generated by the same pricing process. In the baseline simulations, the latter component accounts for 97.6% of the variance of estimation error for the gradient-boosted specification. Within-panel resampling procedures use the information of one realised trajectory and do not identify this across-design component.
  Three results organise the analysis. First, across-design dispersion is well described by the empirical relation sigma_hat approx 0.182 V^(-0.271), where V equals moves times magnitude squared. Second, adding regions sharing a common price path reduces outcome noise but does not create independent price trajectories; conversely, averaging across units with independent design-specific errors reduces dispersion at the standard square root rate. Third, a Paule-Mandel variance component estimated across independently priced units substantially increases empirical coverage in homogeneous simulations, from 0.469 to 0.931. The broader implication is a shift toward designing data-generating processes that create independent identifying variation rather than relying solely on fixed passive panels.

</details>


### [143] [Asymmetric Capacity Allocation in Self-Refinement Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21345)
*Zhuoyi Yang,Ian G. Harris,Salar Hashemitaheri,Cassie Huang,Yuangang Li,Hyunwoo Oh,Paul Dourish,Tony Givargis,Mohsen Imani,Li Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Larger models for generation and refinement stages improve self-refinement performance, while critic stage is insensitive to model size - enabling more efficient computational allocation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing self-refinement approaches treat model size as an implementation detail rather than studying its impact on different stages (generation, critique, revision), potentially leading to inefficient resource allocation. Little work systematically examines how model size affects each stage or whether equally capable models are needed for all three stages.

Method: Conducted the first stage-wise model size study of self-refinement pipelines across 5 benchmarks from different domains using 6 model sizes of Qwen3 and 4 model sizes of Gemma 3. Systematically examined how varying model sizes for generation, critique, and revision stages affects overall pipeline performance.

Result: 1) Larger generators and refiners generally improve pipeline performance, and an undersized refiner can even harm performance. 2) Performance is highly insensitive to the size of the critic - including even a small critic consistently outperforms omitting critique altogether, despite the critic's size not mattering much.

Conclusion: Model capacity should not be allocated uniformly across self-refinement pipelines. Different stages exhibit distinct size scaling characteristics, providing practical guidance for designing more computationally efficient multi-stage language model systems by strategically allocating computational resources.

Abstract: Self-refinement, typically structured as generation, critique, and revision, is a widely adopted paradigm for improving LLM generation and serves as a core mechanism in many LLM agents. While the three stages involve different cognitive demands, most existing approaches conveniently treat the model size as an implementation detail rather than a subject of study, which may lead to a waste of resources. Little work has systematically examined how model size affects each stage or whether effective self-refinement requires equally capable models for generation, critique, and revision. We present the first stage-wise model size study of the self-refinement pipeline on 5 benchmarks from different domains using 6 model sizes of Qwen3 and 4 model sizes of Gemma 3. We conclude that larger generators and refiners generally improve the pipeline, whereas an undersized refiner can even harm performance. Second, performance is highly insensitive to the size of the critic, although including even a small critic consistently outperforms omitting critique altogether. Our findings demonstrate that model capacity should not be allocated uniformly across self-refinement pipelines. Instead, different stages exhibit distinct size scaling characteristics, providing practical guidance for designing more computationally efficient multi-stage language model systems.

</details>


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [144] [Level-k Distinguishable Mechanisms for Evaluating Bounded Rationality in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21296)
*Binchi Zhang,Atrisha Sarkar*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: LLMs demonstrate accurate strategic reasoning in novel games when using recursive reasoning, but struggle with inductive inference from opponent behavior, with errors stemming from incorrect depth selection rather than flawed best-response computation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations of strategic reasoning in LLMs rely on canonical games that might allow memorization rather than genuine reasoning, making it difficult to distinguish true strategic depth from pretraining artifacts.

Method: Formalized a level-K distinguishability condition, constructed novel game structures meeting this standard, evaluated LLMs' strategic depth using both Chain-of-Thought tokens and actual actions under recursive reasoning, and inductive inference from opponent game-play data.

Result: Models maintain accurate strategic depth under recursive reasoning with strong consistency between stated reasoning and actions, but inductive inference degrades accuracy sharply; explicit strategic mentalizing in chain-of-thought improves performance; errors come from using wrong reasoning depth rather than incorrect best-response computation.

Conclusion: LLMs can perform genuine strategic reasoning in novel contexts, but their performance depends heavily on the inference method used, with recursive reasoning outperforming inductive inference from opponent behavior.

Abstract: Strategic depth of reasoning is essential for human interaction of Large Language Models (LLMs) operating in boundedly rational environments. However, existing evaluations are primarily based on canonical games prevalent in pretraining corpora, making it difficult to disentangle true strategic reasoning from memorisation. To address this, we formalise a necessary level-K distinguishability condition for strategic depth inference and construct a suite of novel game structures that meet this standard. Using these games, we evaluate strategic depth in LLMs from both the Chain-of-Thought tokens and actual actions under recursive reasoning and an inductive trace of opponent game-play data. Across experimental trials spanning four LLMs, four game structures, and ten levels of iterated reasoning, we find that model models maintain accurate strategic depth under recursive reasoning, with strong internal consistency between stated reasoning and actions at every level. Errors arise from using the wrong number of iterated depth of reasoning steps, not from computing best responses incorrectly. However, inductive inference from opponent play degrades accuracy sharply and unevenly across games, and explicit strategic mentalizing in the chain of thought substantially improves overall performance.

</details>
